# Architecture (MVP)

`PROJECT.md` describes SWARM's long-term vision: a stateless GCP cloud orchestrator (Cloud Run + Pub/Sub + Firestore) talking gRPC to a local daemon. **For the MVP, skip the GCP layer entirely** and mirror Cascade's architecture instead — a router + queue + worker running locally (Docker Compose, same as Cascade), reachable from GitHub via a Cloudflare Tunnel instead of a cloud ingress service. The gRPC/cloud-engine design in `PROJECT.md` is not being built yet; treat it as a possible future phase once the local-only version works end-to-end for one user, not as the current target. If this changes, update this file and `README.md` in the same change.

## Why copy Cascade's shape instead of `PROJECT.md`'s

`PROJECT.md` was written before we had a concrete reference implementation to copy. Cascade already solves "webhook comes in → figure out which project/task it belongs to → run an agent → report back" for exactly this kind of pipeline, in the same language, and its author knew Node.js well. Rebuilding that shape from scratch (or building the gRPC/Pub/Sub version) is strictly more work for no MVP-stage benefit. SWARM's two product differences from Cascade are:

1. **PM provider = GitHub Projects** first, which Cascade has no adapter for at all — that piece is net-new. (Linear has since landed too, issue #530, then Jira with #580 and Trello with #588, and for all three Cascade *is* the precedent; `PMType`'s four values now all resolve to a registered provider.)
2. **SCM = GitHub**, same as Cascade — copy this piece close to verbatim.

Everything else — router/queue/worker split, manifest-based provider registration, credential scoping, loop prevention — should be copied, not reinvented.

## Components (MVP)

```
GitHub (repo + Projects v2)
   │  webhooks (pull_request, pull_request_review, issue_comment, issues, check_suite, projects_v2_item)
   ▼
Cloudflare Tunnel (external, not our concern — just a public HTTPS URL pointed at the router)
   ▼
Router  (Hono HTTP server, local Docker container)
   — verifies webhook signatures
   — resolves which SWARM project the event belongs to
   — records a durable dispatch (Postgres, ADR-002) and publishes its BullMQ
     wake-up (PR review-lifecycle jobs are prioritized ahead of PM-board jobs,
     so a review never queues behind a planning or implementation run —
     `src/queue/producer.ts`'s `priorityFor`)
   ▼
Worker  (host process — NOT containerized, one job at a time or a small pool)
   — looks up the trigger handler for the event
   — provisions a Git worktree (see "Worktree lifecycle" below)
   — spawns `claude` or `antigravity` CLI with the worktree as CWD
   — validates structured agent hand-offs and performs deterministic SCM delivery
   — persists step progress, then posts results back to GitHub Projects

API server (Hono HTTP server + tRPC, host process)
   — exposes a `/health` check and mounts a tRPC API under `/trpc`
   — self-hosted, single-process model running locally on the host
   — binds to `127.0.0.1`; authorizes every request except `/health` and `ping` by a per-user session cookie (issue #281 task 2) — or, when local single-user mode is enabled (`SWARM_SINGLE_USER_MODE`, issue #298), by resolving a bootstrapped `localhost-admin` with no cookie at all — then scopes every project-keyed procedure to the caller's project membership (issue #281 task 4)
   — serves the built dashboard SPA (`dashboard/dist`) statically as a fallback when present (self-hosted mode)
```

Redis (for BullMQ) and Postgres (for project config, credentials at rest, and run history — same role it plays in Cascade) run in the same Docker Compose stack as the router. The **worker and the API server are the exceptions**: they run directly on the host rather than in containers — the worker because it provisions Git worktrees and spawns the `claude` / `antigravity` CLIs (which need the developer's own PATH, auth, and config), and the API server (scaffolded via Hono and tRPC, and serving the dashboard SPA) for local-first, single-process execution. They connect to Redis/Postgres over their published host ports (`REDIS_URL` / `DATABASE_URL` in `.env`). There is no separate "cloud" process for the MVP; router, worker, API server, Redis, and Postgres are all local.

**How a worker connects (ADR-003 §1).** There is **one** worker program (issue #553, closing ADR-004's open question 2): `src/transport/connect-entry.ts`, started with `npm run dev:worker`, holding **only** `SWARM_WORKER_CREDENTIAL` + `SWARM_CONTROL_PLANE_URL` — never `DATABASE_URL`/`REDIS_URL`, even on a host that has them, and, since issue #765, no operator SCM credential either (it arrives per assignment — see "Per-worker operator SCM credential" below). The in-process, DB-direct executor that used to serve the same-machine case was deleted rather than kept behind a flag: a second path kept "just in case" is how the DB-free one stopped being exercised (issue #535 found a phase body that had never once run on it). It reaches the router's authenticated `/worker/*` endpoint (`POST /worker/session` handshake, then a `/worker/stream` WebSocket carrying heartbeats), reconnecting with exponential backoff. **Including the one on the control-plane host** (issue #551): that machine used to run a second, database-holding executor of its own, so the same role shipped as two programs and only one of them was exercised on a given run; it now points `SWARM_CONTROL_PLANE_URL` at its own router over loopback (`http://localhost:<ROUTER_PORT>`) and runs the identical program a remote worker does, over the identical code path. A remote worker reaches the same URL through the Cloudflare tunnel instead. Both keep the same `worker_sessions` liveness lease fresh, so the eligibility gate treats a remote worker exactly like a local one. The transport carries a session handshake, heartbeats, and the split-delivery back-channel (ADR-003 §2, issue #407): a `TaskAssignment` pushed cloud→worker and `TaskExecutionResult`/`StreamLog`/`TaskProgress` reported worker→cloud. Never source. Beyond the handshake and heartbeats, a transport worker **executes** a pushed `TaskAssignment` DB-free (`src/transport/assignment-execution.ts` `runAssignmentDbFree`, ADR-003 §2): it reconstructs the `ProjectConfig` from the assignment's non-secret slice (`src/transport/db-free-project.ts`) plus its own checkout path (`SWARM_WORKER_REPO_ROOT`, default cwd), runs source-carrying delivery under the operator's own token (`SWARM_OPERATOR_GH_TOKEN` → `SCMProvider.operatorDeliveryProvider`, resolved through the registry since issue #462), and streams `ack → running → live logs → terminal result` back over the #406 back-channel — no `DATABASE_URL`/`REDIS_URL`, no source on the wire, live output streamed rather than written (the **control plane** persists every worker's `stream-log` batches to `run_output_events` — `src/router/stream-log-persistence.ts`, issue #544), and cancellation delivered as a pushed `task-cancel` frame (issue #549) rather than read from Redis — a frame the worker now *answers* rather than only applies (issue #724): it is the only party that knows whether the phase is still executing there, so a cancel it cannot apply settles the dispatch as the terminal cancelled result, and one whose real result it still holds undelivered re-reports that instead. **That back-channel sink spans sessions (issue #718):** a phase runs independently of the heartbeat loop, so it routinely outlives the session it was pushed on, and its terminal `TaskExecutionResult` used to be dropped with the dead socket — a one-second blip discarded the outcome, a *successful* one included, and the dispatch settled ~30 minutes later on the back-channel timer as "did not report a result within the lease window". The sink now lives for the process: a terminal result that could not be written is held in a bounded, one-per-dispatch queue and delivered on the next session, and a re-pushed assignment for a dispatch whose result is still held is answered with that result instead of re-running the phase. Exactly-once stays the control plane's property (`deliverDispatchResult` consumes the waiter, so a duplicate frame is a logged no-op) and its waiter survives the disconnect untouched, so a re-sent result settles the dispatch normally. Output is deliberately *not* queued — it is unbounded and best-effort, so a lost session still costs a window of `stream-log` lines, never the run's outcome. **The control plane now says so out loud (issue #723):** the router is the only side that knows the moment a `/worker/stream` socket closes and the moment one opens, so it records the interruption against every dispatch it is awaiting on that worker (`src/router/dispatch-results.ts`) and writes one control-plane-authored note into each of their runs' output streams on loss and on restore (`persistControlPlaneNote`, on the same per-`runId` append chain as the streamed batches, so it never interleaves with real output and never blocks the socket handler on Postgres) — a superseded socket's stale close writes none, since nothing was interrupted. Otherwise a run whose output merely stopped *arriving* — heartbeat line included — is indistinguishable from one that stopped progressing. The same bookkeeping is what a result-wait timeout is then attributed to: a dispatch whose transport was interrupted fails with a message naming the drop, its count, and the worker's own `assignment phase finished — sending result` line to check against, instead of the "did not report a result within the lease window" that blamed a worker which may well have delivered. A timeout with no interruption keeps that original wording. Output itself is still not replayed — this is the legibility half only. Worktree coordination is host-local on this path (`src/worktree/host-local-runtime.ts`): atomic filesystem leases prevent two local provisioners from reclaiming one checkout, preservation pins retain resumable work, and no Redis/Postgres lease, liveness, reclaim-pin, or cancellation lookup is loaded. One scope above those per-`(project, task)` leases, the daemon holds a single lock on the *checkout* for its whole life (`src/worktree/checkout-lock.ts`, issue #689 — below), so two separately registered workers cannot be pointed at one working tree. Everything a DB-free worker cannot do itself reaches its backing resource through the router's delivery API instead (below), called with the worker's *own* credential (`src/transport/delivery-client.ts`), so no project credential and no database connection is ever needed on the worker: **Implementation**'s two board writes and **Respond-to-review**'s board-card lookup + two status-report moves go up as PM delivery calls under the project's PM credential, **Review**'s `submitReview` goes up as an SCM delivery call under the project's reviewer PAT, and two things whose backing store is the control plane's *database* go up too — Review's three **review-verdict ledger** calls (`src/pipeline/review-ledger.ts` → `src/transport/review-ledger-delivery.ts`), so the review-verdict cap (issue #235) and the re-review signal (issue #328) keep working rather than being skipped for want of a `DATABASE_URL`, and Respond-to-review's **follow-up Review** enqueue (`src/pipeline/follow-up-review.ts` → `src/transport/follow-up-review-delivery.ts`), which writes a dispatch row and queues a synthetic event so a pushed fix is re-reviewed (issue #241). Since issue #536 a transport worker runs **all six** phases — `respond-to-ci`, `resolve-conflicts`, `implementation`, `review`, `respond-to-review`, `planning` — so phase support no longer depends on which machine a worker happens to be. **Planning** was the last holdout, and what it added was width rather than a new kind of operation: its whole server-side surface is board metadata, so five more PM delivery routes (`pm/find-comment`, `pm/create-item`, `pm/update-item`, `pm/label`, `pm/blocked-by`) cover it, and its agent run, `proposed_plan.md`, split contract and deterministic scope gate all stay worker-side. The split's idempotency travels unchanged, and **issue #543 then closed the hole it travelled with**: `findComment` on the plan-delivery marker short-circuits a replayed delivery *before* `applySplit` runs, and `planned` still marks only children whose preparation completed — but that marker is the plan comment, and `applySplit` runs before it is posted, so a split that died partway used to leave its already-created children unmarked and have the retry re-create them (confirmed by execution; pre-existing and identical on the in-process path, but `createWorkItem` is the one write outside the per-child `try` and it is now on the wire). The split is therefore **resumable**: every child is created carrying a per-delivery `<!-- swarm-split-child:<runId>:<index> -->` marker in its own body, and each iteration resolves that marker first (`findWorkItemByDescriptionMarker`), adopting the card this delivery already made instead of spawning a second one — while a genuine replan, a new run row hence a new id, still performs its own split. The three board-driven phases get the delegate-less transport writer (`createWriteOnlyTransportPmProvider`): every board write plus five narrow reads — `listBlockers`, `findWorkItemByUrlSuffix`, the repository-scoped `findWorkItemForArtifact`, `findComment`, and `findWorkItemByDescriptionMarker` — ride the transport, and the two *enumerating/whole-item* reads (`getWorkItem`, `listWorkItems`) refuse loudly rather than guessing, because the control plane already performed the reads the assignment was composed from. `listBlockers` is transported, not stubbed, precisely because nothing else gates dependencies — `findGatingBlockers` runs inside the Implementation phase, not in the dispatcher — so answering "no blockers" would build a task whose prerequisites are still open (issue #330). It answers with both blocker sources, because the gate needs the prose-only ones to *surface* even though it no longer defers on them (issue #643), and the notice it posts rides the same transported `addComment`. `findWorkItemByUrlSuffix` and `findWorkItemByDescriptionMarker` are the *narrow* form of the board read (one URL tail or one marker in, at most one card out), which is why `listWorkItems` keeps refusing: a worker has no business enumerating a board to answer a one-card question. Respond-to-review's fix commit, push and response comment stay on the operator token — that reply is the *implementer* answering the review, so routing it through a composite built for the reviewer persona would have the reviewer answering itself. (The same attribution now holds on the control-plane delivery path, where the reply *does* ride the transport: the `/pr-comment` frame names the implementer, so the router writes it under that persona rather than the reviewer's — issue #444.) (A dependency block detected on the worker still enters the control plane's bounded recheck: the worker reports it as a `dependency` deferral carrying the still-open blockers, and the control plane rebuilds `DependencyBlockedError` from that frame so the token-free `recheck` budget and its terminal "must be done first" board message apply unchanged — issue #438.) The supported-phase gate remains as the worker-side backstop even though no phase is excluded from it any more — a daemon predating #536 still declares only five, and its dispatches must keep routing rather than failing. Which phases a *machine* can run is **enforced by the dispatcher rather than only stated here** (issue #467): the daemon declares its phase repertoire at handshake (`HandshakeRequestSchema.supportedPhases`, persisted as `workers.supported_phases`), so the eligibility gate refuses a candidate for a phase it cannot run (`missing-phase-capability`) and the dispatch waits for a capable worker instead of being pushed to one that answers with a terminal failure. A daemon that declares no phases is recorded as supporting all of them, which is how the dispatcher behaved before the field existed. **A third declaration rides the same handshake (issue #687):** which repository the daemon's one local checkout actually is (`HandshakeRequestSchema.repository`, persisted as the nullable `workers.repository`), resolved once at startup from that checkout's `origin` remote through the shared normaliser `GitWorktreeManager.assertRepoIdentity` already uses (`normalizeRepoSlug` / `repoSlugFromRemoteUrl` / `RepoSlugSchema`, extracted into the provider-neutral `src/scm/repo-slug.ts`) — no new environment variable, since `SWARM_WORKER_REPO_ROOT` already names the checkout. The control plane has no other way to learn it: `repoRoot` is host-local and never travels, and `hostname` is diagnostic and unpersisted. Optional and additive in both directions, so `TRANSPORT_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is not bumped — a daemon that predates the field omits it and the column records NULL, which is exactly today's behaviour, and a checkout with no identifiable `origin` declares nothing rather than failing to start. An omitted field *clears* the column, the one deliberate asymmetry with `supportedPhases`: the row states the checkout of the program currently operating it, so a stale-but-wrong declaration would be worse than an absent one. **The first thing gated on that fact is the worker's own pre-flight check (issue #688):** `runAssignmentDbFree` compares the assignment's `projectConfig.repo` against the declaration — both through the shared `repoSlugsMatch` — right after the supported-phase gate and *before* it reconstructs the project or touches the checkout, and settles the dispatch terminal-`failed` with a message naming both repositories and `SWARM_WORKER_REPO_ROOT`. That case is reachable because enrollment is per `(worker, project)`: a worker enrolled in two projects with different repositories is pushed both, and holds one checkout to run them in. Terminal rather than deferred, deliberately — no retry on *this* worker can make the two repositories match, so a deferral would re-push impossible work until the budget ran out (the same reasoning the phase gate applies). A daemon whose checkout it could not identify declares nothing and so refuses nothing, keeping exactly today's behaviour, and `assertRepoIdentity` stays as the provision-time backstop for both. **The second thing gated on the checkout is the checkout itself (issue #689):** before it handshakes, the daemon takes a host-local lock on its `SWARM_WORKER_REPO_ROOT` (`src/worktree/checkout-lock.ts`), so two separately *registered* workers cannot both run against one working tree and contend on the same main repository's `index.lock` — the session lease (`src/identity/worker-session.ts`) only ever stopped two daemons sharing one *credential*. The guard is host-local because the control plane cannot be: `repoRoot` never travels, and two checkouts of one repository (on one machine or two) are legitimate capacity, so what is being protected is the checkout rather than the repository. It is the same atomic `mkdir` + `owner.json` claim `host-local-runtime.ts` makes for a `(project, task)` checkout — with the liveness/expiry primitives now shared rather than duplicated (`src/worktree/local-lock.ts`) — kept at `~/.swarm/checkout-locks/<sha256(realpath(repoRoot))>/` rather than inside the checkout, because a daemon holds no project config at startup and so knows no `worktreeRoot`, and writing to `<repoRoot>/.swarm-state` would litter the operator's own repository. Keying on the **realpath** means a symlinked, relative, or trailing-slash spelling is recognised as the same checkout. It carries its **own** 15-minute TTL, restamped every third of it by the holder, deliberately not that module's unrefreshed 4h `LEASE_TTL_MS`: a worker process lives for days where an agent run does not, so reusing that TTL would leave a dead daemon's checkout blocked for hours. So the lock is reclaimable on liveness grounds — at once when the holder's pid is gone, on the lapsed refresh when a recycled pid impersonates it, and immediately when the holder released it on a graceful stop — and never permanently blocks its own checkout. A second daemon exits non-zero *before* handshaking, with a line naming the holder: its `workerId` once the holder's own handshake told it which worker it is (delivered worker-side only through `WorkerTransportOptions.onSession`, so there is no wire or schema change), its pid and host until then. **That per-checkout key is also what makes the daemon startable without a credential in hand (issue #788):** the one program now has a second *launcher*, `swarm run:worker`, which reads `~/.swarm/worker-credentials/<the same sha256(realpath(repoRoot))>/credential.json` — written by both registration paths on the machine that ran them, `700`/`600`, keyed through the helper the lock itself now uses (`src/worktree/checkout-key.ts`) rather than a second copy of the derivation — and spawns `npm run dev:worker` with `SWARM_WORKER_REPO_ROOT` = the cwd and that credential in the child's environment. The daemon's env contract, handshake, and lock are untouched: it is the *supply* of the two host-local values that changed, for the case where the machine that registered the worker is the machine running it. `npm run dev:worker` with both set explicitly stays the path for everything else — a remote machine, a process supervisor — which is why the credential is still printed at registration. **The last thing gated on the declaration is the enrollment itself (issue #690):** the control plane now polices the `(worker, project)` pairing at both moments it becomes knowable — `enrollWorker` refuses a write whose project is not the worker's declared repository (`EnrollmentRepositoryMismatchError` → `BAD_REQUEST` / one CLI line), and `handleHandshake` suspends the enrollments a reconnecting daemon's declaration contradicts — so the mismatch is stated on the roster rather than only surfacing as the daemon's refused assignments (see "Worker-project enrollment" below for what it deliberately does *not* do). **A fourth declaration rides the same handshake (issue #918):** the SWARM build the daemon is actually running (`HandshakeRequestSchema.build`, persisted as the nullable `workers.build_commit` / `workers.build_dirty`) — the commit its install root is on, plus one flag covering both ways the running code can differ from it (uncommitted changes, or a `dist/` build older than HEAD). The handshake already carried `daemonVersion`, but that resolves to `package.json`'s `version` — `0.1.0`, never bumped — so it reads identically on every daemon in the fleet whatever code it runs, and "is this worker running the fix?" had no answer from the control plane at all. The install root is **not** `SWARM_WORKER_REPO_ROOT` and not `cwd`: on the control-plane host several daemons serve several different project repositories while all loading SWARM from one npm-linked checkout, so the only correct anchor is the module's own `import.meta.url` (`src/lib/build-identity.ts`, the way `resolveDaemonVersion()` already finds `package.json`), and `npm_package_version` is the *project's* version when the daemon was launched from a project's npm script. Optional and additive in both directions, so `TRANSPORT_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is not bumped here either — a daemon too old to declare it keeps connecting and working exactly as today, and an install root that is not a git checkout (the Compose router image has no `.git` at all) declares nothing rather than failing to start. An omitted field clears both columns, the same asymmetry `repository` makes. It carries no secret — a commit id is public coordinates, and like every handshake field it is never reflected in an error body. **Nothing is gated on it, and still nothing is** (issue #925, phase 2/2): the dashboard's Workers screen now *reports* it — the per-worker detail view shows the declared build beside the API server's own (`resolveOwnBuildIdentity()` in the process serving the dashboard, which is what `npm run reload` updates), and a machine whose build is not that one is marked `OUTDATED` there and beside its name on the roster, so "which workers are behind?" is answerable from the control plane rather than by grepping each machine's startup log. The verdict (`DashboardWorkerView.buildIsCurrent`) is **equality**, not ancestry — the control plane may never have fetched the worker's commit, so the copy says the builds *differ* rather than inventing a distance — and it is three-valued: an undeclared build and a control plane that cannot resolve its own (the Compose router image has no `.git`) both render no mark at all rather than stamping the whole fleet stale. No dispatch refusal, no eligibility reason, no notification — a machine on a different build is dispatched to exactly as before. The daemon declares its repertoire at the moment it proves it owns the row — at handshake — which is deliberate rather than incidental: the stored set must never outlive the program that wrote it, or a row narrowed by one older daemon would keep refusing `planning` on a host that can run it, with no path to widen it again. Separately from what a machine *can* run, what a project may *give* it is the enrollment's own `allowedPhases` (issue #509) — and that is the whole of it: **every worker has the same permissions**, `planning` included (issue #542). `planning` was for a time permitted only on an instance admin's own worker (`PlanningRequiresInstanceAdminError`, now deleted); that gate is gone, because `instance_admin` is an installation role rather than a statement about a machine, and board authorship is already authorized at a better scope — the owner picks `allowedPhases`, a project administrator approves the enrollment, and since issue #537 the board writes run control-plane side under the *project's* PM credential, which no worker holds. Migration `0043_backfill_planning_instance_admin.sql` had stripped `planning` from every non-admin owner's enrollment; those rows stay stripped, so re-granting it is an explicit owner choice rather than something the removal does silently. **The agent's own identity is the worker operator's, on every worker** (ADR-004 §2, and universal since #551/#553 retired the host's second executor): the implementer *is* `SWARM_OPERATOR_GH_TOKEN`'s account, which authors the branch, the commits, the PR and the implementer-side comments. `credentials.implementer` is not read on the execution path and is not even in `ScmCredentialReferencesSchema` (issue #396) — what changes with a project is the repository, not who commits to it. The two identities that *are* per-project, the **reviewer** PAT and the **PM** credential, never reach a worker at all: those writes ride the delivery API, so a submitted review's author and a board write's actor are the same whichever machine ran the phase. See **Control-plane dispatch** below.

### Process responsibilities — who owns what (issue #550)

Beyond executing a phase, a SWARM installation has a handful of recurring chores. Each has **one** stated owner, so nothing depends on a process happening to reach it on the way to something else. The worker is `src/transport/connect-entry.ts` on every host — a DB-free daemon that executes phases and nothing else — so none of these can live there.

| Responsibility | Owner | Where |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Database migrations | **Router** | `src/router/index.ts` |
| BullMQ dispatch consumer + ADR-001 eligibility gate | **Router** | `src/router/dispatcher.ts`, `src/worker/consumer.ts` (`processJob`, the shared machine it drives) |
| Dispatch-lease + stale-`running`-run reconciliation | **Router** | `src/router/dispatcher.ts`, `src/dispatch/reconciler.ts` |
| Run cancellation delivery | **Router** | `src/router/dispatch-cancellation.ts` (pushes `task-cancel`, issue #549) |
| Startup orphaned-`running` reap (worker-less runs) | **API server** | `src/api/maintenance.ts` |
| Worktree retention sweep (`SWARM_WORKTREE_SWEEP_INTERVAL_MS`) | **API server** | `src/api/maintenance.ts` |
| Fleet abandoned-worktree sweep (`SWARM_WORKTREE_ABANDONED_SWEEP_INTERVAL_MS`) | **API server** | `src/api/maintenance.ts`, `src/api/worktree-sweep-fanout.ts` |
| Executing a pipeline phase | **Worker** (host or remote — one program either way) | `src/transport/assignment-execution.ts`, `runAssignedPhase` (`src/worker/consumer.ts`) |
| CLI capability/quota discovery + reporting | **Worker** | `src/transport/quota-reporting.ts` |

The two host-local chores go to the **API server** rather than the router because the router runs in Docker (`docker-compose.yml`) with no repository checkout — exactly what the sweep prunes — while the API server runs on the control-plane host with `DATABASE_URL` and the checkout. `startHostMaintenance` (`src/api/maintenance.ts`) starts unconditionally there. The **fleet** sweep added by issue #956 is the third chore in that same function and needs no checkout at all — only a clock and the database: it reaches other machines' checkouts by *asking each machine to sweep its own*, which is the whole shape issue #951 settled on. It lands here for the other half of the rule above — this is the stated owner of the control plane's non-phase chores, and it is the **asking** side of the `swarm:worktree-sweep` hand-off exactly as `workers.requestWorktreeSweep` is, the router being the side that subscribes and pushes. Its setting is a **due** interval rather than a timer period: an hourly tick compares it against a durable marker in the `app_settings` blob (`maintenance.lastFleetWorktreeSweepAt`), so an API server restarted daily fans out weekly rather than daily, and a machine offline when the signal went out is handed its request on the next connection or, failing that, asked again the following week. Nothing is staged, drained or halted — `src/api/worker-update-rollout.ts` is more than this needs, since a sweep has no bad-build risk and takes no machine out of the dispatch pool. It owned CLI capability/quota discovery as a third chore until issue #823, and that one **moved to the worker** rather than being dropped: the API server's probe could only ever describe *this* machine, which is nobody's worker, so the row it wrote named no owner and was shown to every user as their own, while a daemon probing its own host produces exactly the attributable fact the page needs. The reap the API server still runs is scoped to **worker-less** runs (`workerId = null`): this process is no worker, and a federated run is already owned by the dispatch-lease reconciler plus `failStaleRunningRuns`, so it is deliberately not widened to another host's in-flight runs. A *launch/authentication* failure still triggers no recovery probe from the dispatch path (issue #553), for the same reason the probe moved: that failure describes the worker's PATH and logins, not the control plane's.

CLI-quota reporting is the one chore the **worker** owns beyond executing a phase, and it is the only process that can: a snapshot describes one machine's installed CLIs and logins (issue #825, phase 2 of #823). `startWorkerQuotaReporting` (`src/transport/quota-reporting.ts`) runs a full, live probe shortly after the daemon connects and again on a coded six-hour interval — the cadence and the "best-effort per iteration, every timer `unref`'d" style of the retired control-plane loop, though *not* its cheap tick: the report is an upsert, so a probe that spawns no CLI would overwrite the stored allowance with an empty row and nothing re-fills it (the page's Refresh only re-reads storage) — and POSTs the snapshots to `POST /worker/delivery/quota`, which persists each one against **the worker the credential authenticated as**, never a worker id the request names (the frame carries none). It is an HTTP delivery route rather than a WebSocket frame on purpose: `handleWorkerStreamFrame` closes the socket with `WS_CLOSE.MALFORMED_FRAME` on a frame it cannot parse, so a newer worker against an older router would reconnect-loop, while an unserved *route* is a 404 `postDelivery` explains in one line and the daemon survives — which is why `TRANSPORT_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is not bumped for it. Nothing about it can touch a run: a failed probe or a refused report is one `warn` line.

The sweep reads the lease store *this host's worker writes* (`retentionWorktreeRuntime`, `src/worktree/retention.ts`): the host-local filesystem leases/pins under `<repoRoot>/<worktreeRoot>/.swarm-state`, which is what the DB-free daemon writes. Asking Redis instead — the store the retired in-process worker used — would find every checkout unleased and prune one out from under a live phase (issue #551). It also **sweeps** that store: `sweepStaleHostLocalState` removes expired lock directories, preservation pins, takeover guards and stranded `preserve()` staging files under `.swarm-state` by the TTLs the module already defines (issue #721). It rides this sweep rather than a schedule of its own precisely because those TTLs are otherwise evaluated only by the next provisioner *for that same task*, so a task never dispatched again kept its debris indefinitely — and the `<pin>.<uuid>.tmp` staging file has no next reader at all. Being TTL-only is the design: this process is not the daemon, so it can neither see a daemon's in-flight set nor trust a pid it did not write, and since issue #717 the recorded timestamp is the authority on every liveness path anyway.

**Two modules survive with the router as their only caller**, and are named here so a future reader does not mistake them for worker code: `src/worker/consumer.ts` (`processJob`, the dispatch/settle machine — `runAssignedPhase` in the same file is the worker's half) and `src/worker/eligibility-gate.ts`. They stayed under `src/worker/` when the in-process executor was deleted; moving them to `src/dispatch/` is a rename worth doing separately.

Two gaps are known and stated rather than papered over, both of them *background* facts about a host the control plane cannot see: a **remote** worker's checkout has no retention sweep — no process on the control-plane host can see that filesystem — and a worker-reported CLI quota carries no run-derived exhaustion hint. The page itself is no longer one of them: issue #825 supplied the writer that fills it. The snapshot is no longer ambiguous about *whose* allowance it shows, and is now attributable to a person: `cli_quotas` is keyed on `(worker_id, cli)` with a cascading FK to `workers` (issue #823, re-keyed off the `(host, cli)` of issue #703), so a row is one *worker's* installation and `quota.getQuotas` scopes the read to `ctx.user` through `listCliQuotasForOwner` — the only read there is, with no `instanceAdmin` override, so an admin sees their own machines like everyone else. Removing the control-plane probe in the same change left the page empty until each daemon began reporting its own host (above), which is what fills it: the admin's own worker included, since an admin's machine is reached by the same owner filter as everyone else's. **What a worker-reported snapshot does not carry is the run-derived exhaustion hint**: `getFallbackRateLimitInfo` reads `runs.next_retry_at` from Postgres and a worker holds no `DATABASE_URL`, so it reports live data or none, and enriching it server-side would need that lookup scoped per worker — its own change. The **operator-invoked** recovery actions are not among the gaps either (issue #592). They reach a remote checkout without reaching its filesystem, by carrying the recovery intent on the assignment (`TaskAssignment.recoveryMode`) so the worker that holds the checkout applies it in its own recovery gate: "Reset & restart" sends `'discard'` — on every reset since issue #744, not only a forced one — and the worker removes the wedged checkout, dirty and unpushed work included, before provisioning. That is why no worktree-teardown frame pair exists: the reset's own replacement dispatch already routes back to the worker holding the checkout (worker affinity), so the intent is enough.

### The router's operator surfaces (issues #798, #799)

Besides webhook ingress, the worker transport, and the worker delivery API, the router hosts the two surfaces an **operator's CLI** needs over the network. They are here rather than on the API server for the same reason the worker transport is: `SWARM_CONTROL_PLANE_URL` points at *this* process, which is the only tunnel-exposed one, while the API server binds `127.0.0.1` and ships in no Compose service. A login an operator cannot reach is not a login.

- **`/operator/session`** (`src/router/operator-session.ts`, issue #798) — `POST` to log in with an identifier + password, `GET` to resolve a bearer, `DELETE` to revoke. No new credential type, table, or hashing: it reuses `verifyCredentials`/`createSession`/`resolveSession`/`revokeSession` (`src/identity/auth.ts`), so an operator token is an ordinary `user_sessions` row under `SWARM_SESSION_TTL_HOURS` and re-running `swarm login` is the renewal path.
- **`/operator/trpc/*`** (`src/router/operator-api.ts`, issue #799) — the tRPC endpoint that token buys. Its context resolves `ctx.user` from `Authorization: Bearer <token>` through the same `resolveSession`, so an absent, unknown, expired or revoked token lands as `user: null` and `authedProcedure` (`src/api/trpc.ts`) answers `UNAUTHORIZED`. No procedure on it is public.

Three decisions there are worth not re-deriving:

- **It serves a strict subset, never `appRouter`.** `operatorRouter` (`src/api/operator-router.ts`) is `router({ workers: workersRouter })` and nothing else. This process is internet-reachable, so everything mounted on it is published; projects, credentials, settings, users and runs stay on the loopback-bound API server rather than relying on their own authorization as the only barrier. Widening it publishes a namespace, so it wants its own issue.
- **No procedure's authorization is relaxed for being reached this way.** The same `workersRouter` serves both mounts and a bearer caller is an ordinary `SwarmUser`, not a service identity: the unscoped installation roster stays `instanceAdmin`, `remove`/`rename`/`setConsent`/`scmCredentials.*` stay strictly-owner-only, and the project roster stays `assertProjectAccess`.
- **`SWARM_SINGLE_USER_MODE` is deliberately not honoured on either surface.** The API server can resolve the bootstrapped passwordless `localhost-admin` for every `/trpc/*` request precisely because it is loopback-only; the same branch on the tunnel-exposed router would make anyone who found the URL an installation admin. A single-user installation runs `swarm users set-password localhost-admin` once and logs in like anyone else.

Two procedures exist on `workersRouter` for this surface rather than for the dashboard (issue #799), because a DB-free CLI cannot do either itself: **`workers.register`**, the network equivalent of `swarm workers register` — the one procedure in the tree that returns a secret, the one-time worker credential whose column stores only a SHA-256; registering for yourself needs only a session, while registering for another owner is `instanceAdmin`, matching how `swarm workers register <owner-identifier>` is used today, and that `FORBIDDEN` precedes the owner `NOT_FOUND` so the procedure is not an identifier-existence oracle — and **`workers.projectScmProvider`**, a `contributor` read returning the provider id `requireProjectSCMProviderId(project)` resolves (never `project.scm ?? 'github'`, RULES.md §2), so a `register-and-enroll` with no `DATABASE_URL` can name the provider it stores the operator credential under. That second one is also the single project-keyed procedure that **does not** collapse "no such project" into "not yours" (issue #899): it reads the project row first, so a caller with no membership for a project that really exists gets `FORBIDDEN` naming the `swarm members add` remedy, and only an id no project carries gets `NOT_FOUND`. The collapsed refusal sent an operator hunting a project id that was correct all along, and the carve-out is affordable precisely here — one caller (`register-and-enroll`), an operator provisioning their own machine, a payload that is a provider id and nothing else. It is the deliberate inverse of the `workers.register` note above: this procedure *is* a project-id existence oracle for an authenticated caller, which for a `private` project is a real weakening (a `discoverable` one is already published by `projects.listDiscoverable`). `workers.roster` and every other project-keyed procedure are unchanged — `assertProjectAccess` keeps its existence-hiding semantics for all of them. No transformer is configured on either tRPC mount, so the wire stays plain JSON and a hand-rolled CLI client needs no tRPC dependency.

**The CLI side of that surface is `src/cli/_shared/operator-client.ts`** (issue #800), the operator twin of `src/transport/delivery-client.ts`: one module owning the base-URL join, the `Authorization: Bearer` header, an injectable `fetch`, and an error contract where a non-2xx throws carrying the server's own message (`UNAUTHORIZED` becomes "run `swarm login`"; an unserved procedure or an unmounted `/operator/trpc` becomes a version-skew line). It speaks tRPC's HTTP protocol directly — `GET …/<path>?input=<json>` for a query, `POST …/<path>` with the raw input as the body for a mutation, `{result:{data}}` back — which is exactly what "no transformer" buys. **A mutation POST must carry an explicit `content-type: application/json`**: `@hono/trpc-server`'s non-batch POST path answers `UNSUPPORTED_MEDIA_TYPE` without it, where its GET path falls back to JSON regardless of headers. `tests/unit/router/operator-api.test.ts` drives the real client through the real mount so none of that is inferred. `src/cli/commands/workers.ts` is its only caller, and holds no `DATABASE_URL` as a result; the CLI's `(worker-id, project-id)` arguments reach the enrollment-id-keyed mutations through `workers.getById`, whose detail carries an `enrollmentId` per visible project.

### Observability

All code logs through one shared logger (`src/lib/logger.ts`) — a deliberately tiny, dependency-free wrapper (no pino/winston) mirroring Cascade's `logger.info/warn/error/debug(message, context)` shape. It emits **structured JSON** (one `{level,time,msg,...context}` object per line) for machine parsing, or — for a human watching a terminal — a local-clock timestamp, a colored level tag, the bound `component` as a `[component]` prefix, and remaining context as `key=value` pairs (color only on a real TTY, never when `NO_COLOR` is set). Format follows `SWARM_LOG_FORMAT` (`json`|`pretty`) and auto-picks pretty on an interactive TTY, json when piped/containerized — **leave it unset** in `.env` rather than hardcoding either value: the router's own `docker-compose.yml` environment block already pins it to `json` for that container regardless of `.env`, so setting it in `.env` only ends up overriding the host worker's auto-detection (and silently losing pretty/color) without changing the router at all. `SWARM_LOG_LEVEL` (`debug|info|warn|error`, default `info`) sets the minimum emitted level. Because router and worker share a log stream, each entry point calls `configureLogger({ component })` at startup so every line it emits is tagged `component: "router"` / `"worker"` and stays attributable.

## Project record vs. scoped project config (issue #684)

A project's configuration has **two shapes**, and which one a module takes says what it is allowed to touch.

**`ProjectRecord`** is the authored and persisted project (`ProjectRecordSchema`, `src/config/schema.ts`; the `projects` row): everything genuinely shared — `pm`, `credentials`, `agents`, `pipeline`, `repoRoot`, `worktreeRoot`, `visibility`, `maxConcurrentJobs`, and the project's `scm` provider — stated once, plus `repositories`, a list whose entries carry what is genuinely per-repository (`repo`, `baseBranch`, `branchPrefix`, and the optional `pmRoutingToken` below). Only the config-management surfaces speak it: `swarm.config.json` via `validateConfig`/`applyConfig`, `swarm init`'s template, and the projects/credentials tRPC routers.

**`scm` is the project's, not a repository's** (issue #727). An entry could override it between #684 and #727, and that was incoherent rather than merely unimplemented: SCM credentials are project-wide and single-provider — `credentials.scm` is resolved under `project.scm` alone (issue #628) and the dashboard's Source Control tab edits exactly that one provider's block — so an overriding repository named a provider whose credentials no surface could enter, and every operation scoped to it threw out of `requireScmCredential`. A project spanning two providers is **two projects**, each with its own `repoRoot`, credentials and board mapping, which is what those models already assume; reintroducing the field as the obvious symmetry with `baseBranch` means moving credentials per repository first. An entry stored before #727 keeps parsing — the key is stripped, not rejected — and its repository resolves the project's provider even where the two differed.

**`ProjectConfig`** is that record **scoped to one of its repositories** — the per-repository fields flattened back to the top level beside the project's own `scm`, produced by `scopeProjectToRepository(record, repo?)` (`src/config/project-repository.ts`). It is what every runtime call site takes, and its field set is deliberately **unchanged** by #684 and #727: the SCM provider contract's twelve methods, all six pipeline phases, every trigger, the worker-safe projection (`WORKER_SAFE_KEYS`) and the non-secret transport slice all keep reading `project.repo` / `.baseBranch` / `.branchPrefix` / `.scm` exactly as before. That is also the boundary's teeth: a scoped config carries **no list**, so nothing downstream of the scoping call can act on a repository other than the one its work names.

**The boundary is why a PM provider is *handed* the repository list rather than reading it** (issue #686 phase 1). A repository entry declares an optional `pmRoutingToken` — the opaque provider-native id a board card carries to claim it (a Jira component id, a Linear label id, a Trello label id; GitHub Projects needs none) — and `PMProvider.resolveItemRepository(id, candidates)` answers which entry a card belongs to. The candidates are an **argument**, built by the caller from the record, precisely because the provider is constructed from a scoped `ProjectConfig` that carries no list: re-opening the boundary so a provider could look the mapping up itself is the alternative, and it is the one thing this split exists to prevent. Uniqueness of the tokens within a project is a `ProjectRecordSchema` refinement, so `swarm config apply` rejects two entries claiming one token rather than leaving a card ambiguous at dispatch. Phase 2 acts on the answer — see "Repository routing for a board card" below.

The repository lookups mirror the split (`src/db/repositories/projectsRepository.ts`). `findProjectByRepoFromDb` matches *any* entry by jsonb containment and scopes to the **matched** one, so a delivery from repository B runs against repository B's settings; the id/board/container lookups and `listAllProjectsFromDb` scope to the **default** (first) entry. A board lookup keeps doing so on purpose: it answers "whose board is this?", which is what authenticates a delivery, and *which repository the card runs against* is then decided separately (below) and named on the job, so the by-id read scopes to it. Dropping the `repo` column took its `UNIQUE` index with it, so a write-seam guard (`assertRepositoriesUnclaimed`, raising `ProjectRepositoryConflictError`) refuses a repository another project already owns, and the read orders by `id` so it can never resolve arbitrarily.

### How the repository travels: ingress → job → run → assignment (phase 2)

**Phase 1's one-entry cap is gone.** It existed only because a second repository was not routable — a webhook from repository B would have resolved the project and then run the phase against repository A. Phase 2 closes that by deciding the repository **once**, at the top of the worker's composition root, and letting the existing scoped-config shape carry it the rest of the way. Phase 3 still owes the dashboard a list editor, so a second entry is authored in `swarm.config.json` plus `swarm config apply` for now.

Four hops, and the load-bearing property is that only the first one is new:

1. **Ingress** resolves the project from the delivery's repo (`findProjectByRepoFromDb`), already scoped to the **matched** entry by phase 1. The normalized `ScmEvent.repoFullName` on the durable job is the record of which repository the delivery was for.
2. **The job answers for itself.** `repositoryForJob(job)` (`src/queue/jobs.ts`) is one `switch` exhaustive over the envelope's discriminator: an `scm` job → its event's `repoFullName`, a `merge-automation` job → its own `repo` (which stopped being observability-only here), a `pm` job → the repository its card routed to (`Repository routing`, below — `undefined` only on a row written before that, which means the **default** entry). Exhaustive rather than a lookup with a fallback, so a fourth job type must decide instead of silently inheriting the default. A dispatch row written before either phase answers exactly as it did then.
3. **`processJob` scopes the project to it** (`findProjectByIdFromDb(job.projectId, repositoryForJob(job))`, `src/worker/consumer.ts`) — the seam the whole phase turns on. Everything downstream reads `project.repo` / `.baseBranch` / `.branchPrefix` / `.scm` off that one value and so needed **no change**: the trigger context, all six phases, the SCM provider calls, the worktree and task branch, the review ledger, the delivery ids, the run row's `repository` column, and the `TaskAssignment` the router pushes to a federated worker (`pushAndAwaitResult`, `src/router/dispatcher.ts`, reads the same scoped project — it never re-reads by id). A job naming a repository the project no longer owns throws out of the read (`requireProjectRepository`) and **fails the dispatch** rather than falling back: running a phase against the wrong repository would push a branch, open a pull request, and post reviews in a repository nobody asked for.
4. **The run row is the durable answer afterwards.** `runs.repository` (issue #683, below) is written from that same scoped project, so every control-plane action that starts from a *run* rescopes from it instead of from the project's default: Force re-review (`src/dispatch/force-re-review.ts` — its ledger key, cap override, and synthetic event's `repoFullName`), Reset & restart and the runs API's deferred-termination settlement (`src/dispatch/run-reset.ts`, `src/api/routers/runs.ts` — the `GitWorktreeManager` is built from the entry whose branch settings the run actually used), and startup reconciliation's legacy merge-follow-up import (`src/dispatch/reconciler.ts`, which warns and skips rather than throwing — it is a best-effort one-time import). The queue read model resolves `QueuedRun.repo` through `repositoryForJob` too (`toQueuedRun`, `src/queue/queued-runs.ts`), so the PR link a waiting row renders and the repository its phase will run in cannot drift.

The by-id read is where the choice is stated: `findProjectByIdFromDb(id, repo?)` scopes to the named entry, or to the default when none is named. Its two failure answers are deliberately different kinds of "no" — an unknown **project** is `undefined`, because a caller reading a project id off a durable row must cope with deletion, while a project that exists but does not own `repo` **throws**, naming the project and the repositories it does own.

**One gap this phase surfaces and deliberately does not close: worker checkout roots.** A worker host resolves **one** `repoRoot` per project and declares the one repository its checkout actually is (issues #687–#689), so a run for a non-default repository fails loudly at provisioning — refused up front by the assignment's repository check (`src/transport/assignment-execution.ts`) or by `assertRepoIdentity` (`src/worker/git-worktree-manager.ts`) — rather than working in the wrong tree. That is safe, not silent, but it does mean a second repository is not yet *usable* end to end; per-repository checkout roots are host-local execution state and are a follow-up. Because provisioning is where a non-default repository stops, the DB-free delivery API's own project reads (`src/router/worker-delivery.ts`, which derives the review-ledger key and the server-side SCM delivery provider from the authenticated project's **default** entry) are unreachable for one and are deliberately left for that same follow-up.

### Repository routing for a board card (issue #686 phase 2)

Hop 2 above used to answer `undefined` for every `pm` job, which is what pinned board-driven Planning and Implementation to the project's default entry. Phase 2 routes the card instead, and the decision is made **at ingress** — in `handlePmEvent` (`src/router/webhook-receiver.ts`), after authentication, the status-field filter and loop prevention, so a delivery that is dropped pays for no board read (the same ordering, and the same reason, as `resolveChecksPullRequest` on the SCM side). The answer is recorded on the envelope as `PmWebhookJob.repository`, so a redelivery, a dependency recheck, and an operator's "Retry now" re-parse the stored payload and route identically rather than re-asking the board.

That choice is what makes the change small: `repositoryForJob` is already the one place a job states its repository and `processJob` already scopes the project from it, so **no** consumer, phase, trigger, run-row, or read-model code changes — the worktree, branch, PR, `runs.repository` and `QueuedRun.repo` all name the routed repository because they always named `repositoryForJob`'s answer.

Three properties are load-bearing:

- **A project owning one repository short-circuits.** `resolveCardRepository` (`src/pm/repository-routing.ts`) answers from the record when there is exactly one entry, and takes a provider **factory** rather than a provider so nothing is constructed and no board read is made. With one repository there is nothing to choose; that is arithmetic, not a provider decision, and it is what keeps every existing Jira/Linear/Trello project working without declaring a token.
- **An unroutable card is refused, never defaulted.** `unrouted` (no entry claims it) and `ambiguous` (two do) each ack `202 { ok: true, ignored: true, reason }` with their own reason and a `logger.warn` naming the project, the card, and the repositories it could have gone to. Nothing is enqueued: with two repositories declared, "the first one" is a coin toss that pushes a branch and opens a pull request where nobody asked for one. A failed board read is the same refusal with a `500`, so the provider redelivers.
- **The worker's self-enqueued next phase carries the repository forward.** `selfEnqueueNextPhase` sets it from the run's own scoped project (`src/worker/consumer.ts`) rather than re-deriving it; without that, an auto-advanced Implementation would silently jump back to the default entry. That path needs no board read — the completed phase already ran in the answer.

A `pm` row written before this carries no `repository` and still means the default entry, so there is nothing to migrate.

## Provider abstraction

Three integrations, each following the manifest/registry pattern from `ai/CODING_STANDARDS.md`:

### Per-provider SCM credentials (issue #628)

A project's source-control credentials are **per provider**, not one shared pair. Before #628 `credentials.reviewer` / `credentials.webhookSecret` were a single role-keyed pair, so storing a GitLab reviewer token overwrote the GitHub one in place and a project could hold only one provider's secrets at a time. The shape is now `credentials.scm[<providerId>][<role>]` (`src/config/scm-credentials.ts`, composed into `CredentialsSchema` — `src/config/schema.ts`), with `SCMProviderManifest.credentialRoles` declaring each provider's own conventional reference name for the two roles the contract itself names (`SCM_CREDENTIAL_ROLES` = `reviewer` + `webhookSecret`, `src/scm/types.ts`) — the #497 PM precedent, minus its open role vocabulary, because every SCM provider needs exactly those two and only the *name* differs (`GITHUB_TOKEN_REVIEWER`, `BITBUCKET_TOKEN_REVIEWER`, `GITLAB_TOKEN_REVIEWER`, and their `*_WEBHOOK_SECRET` siblings; the conformance suite asserts no two providers share a key, which is the structural guard against the overwrite).

Resolution is `(project, providerId, role) → secret` — `resolveScmCredentialOrNull` / `requireScmCredential` (`src/config/provider.ts`) — and reads **only** the provider it was asked for. The callers each supply the provider the operation belongs to: `'github'` for the `getPersonaToken` pair (explicitly GitHub's), `'bitbucket'` / `'gitlab'` for their own folders' twins, the *delivering* manifest's id for the receiver's webhook-secret lookup (so a GitLab delivery is never checked against the project's GitHub secret), and `project.scm` for the one PM role that inherits an SCM credential. **Deliberately not a fallback chain**: switching the dashboard's provider selector leaves the previous provider's secrets stored — so switching back restores them, and evaluating a provider costs no re-entry — and those retained credentials are stored but never resolved. A missing credential for the provider asked for throws naming the project, provider, role, and that provider's conventional key, never another provider's secret.

Two decisions worth not re-deriving. **Presence is enforced at resolution, not at parse.** The schema validates only structure (every key a real provider id, every role one that provider declares), because the Source Control tab persists a provider switch on its own — so "provider selected, credentials not yet entered" is a state one click legitimately creates, and a parse-time presence error would leave `swarm config apply` rejecting a config the dashboard just made. **The PM side is switchable too since issue #642, and still keeps its parse-time presence rule**, because the two tabs write in opposite orders: the PM switch flow enters the incoming provider's credentials *before* the new `pm` member is saved (`validatePmCredentialRoles` rule 3, `src/config/schema.ts`), so a half-configured `pm.type` is never something a save produces — and `projects.update` refuses one outright rather than relying on a parse that path never performs. `requireScmCredential` is therefore the single enforcement point, firing when the credential is actually needed. **Migration copies reference names verbatim.** A SQL backfill (`src/db/migrations/0045_scm_credentials_per_provider.sql`) files each persisted project's legacy pair under `credentials.scm[scm_type ?? 'github']`, and `adoptLegacyScmCredentials` does the same at parse time for a `swarm.config.json`; neither rewrites the name to the manifest's `envVarKey`, because the secret sits in `project_credentials` under the old key and renaming the reference without moving the row would break resolution outright. A project created since issue #290 keeps the neutral `SCM_TOKEN_REVIEWER` / `SCM_WEBHOOK_SECRET`, so a reference diverging from its manifest's conventional key is the **common** case — anything that *displays* a credential key must show the reference the role resolves through, never `envVarKey`. There is no runtime fallback from a per-provider reference back to the legacy pair, which is what stops a newly selected GitLab from silently receiving the GitHub secret.

**The API and the Source Control tab followed in issue #632**, which is what makes the shape visible to an operator. `projects.credentials.list`/`set`/`delete` (`src/api/routers/credentials.ts`) are now addressed by `(providerId, role)` exactly as the PM family is: `list` takes an optional provider id (defaulting to the one the project runs on) and answers `{ providerId, providerLabel, providerRegistered, roles: [{ role, envVarKey, referenceKey, isConfigured, maskedValue }] }` built from the manifest's `credentialRoles`, and the writes name a role while the **server** resolves the store key. That is the fix, not a refactor: the pre-#632 `set` took the key itself, so the browser saving GitLab's reviewer token wrote it over GitHub's row. A provider id nothing runtime-ready is registered for answers `providerRegistered: false` rather than throwing, because the tab's provider catalogue is a hand-kept browser list that can name one this installation lacks. The tab queries per selected provider (the id is part of the query key, so a switch refetches), renders `referenceKey`, and takes every field label from per-provider copy in `dashboard/src/lib/credentials.ts` — `getScmProviderCopy(...).roleLabels`, beside the descriptions that already lived there — because "Reviewer PAT" is GitHub's word for a Bitbucket app password and a GitLab access token. Phase 1's interim legacy mirror in `projects.create` is gone with it; the deprecated legacy keys stay in `CredentialsSchema` for the adoption path alone.

### Instance-level default SCM credentials (issues #769, #778)

An **instance-level default** is the value an installation records once for a source-control role that one account normally serves across every project — every runtime-ready provider's `reviewer`, since issue #778 made the reviewer identity an installation-wide *requirement* rather than the GitHub-only convenience #769 shipped. It is a third credential tier: `project_credentials` holds a project's own secret (resolved through a reference key), `worker_scm_credentials` a worker operator's own identity, and `instance_scm_credentials` (`src/db/schema/instanceScmCredentials.ts`) one row per `(provider_id, role)` slot with no project and no worker at all. The write surface is `settings.credentials.{list,set,delete}` (`src/api/routers/instanceCredentials.ts`), driven by General Settings → **Credentials**.

Four decisions worth not re-deriving:

- **Its own table, not a key in the `app_settings` blob.** `settings.get` returns that whole object to any authenticated caller, so a secret there would be echoed back verbatim. A separate table also lets the read path select the *key columns only* (`listConfiguredInstanceScmCredentials`) and never decrypt anything merely to mask it — unlike `project_credentials`' own `resolveAllProjectCredentials` + `maskCredential` pair, which has a reference-key indirection to resolve first.
- **Keyed by `(provider_id, role)`, not by a reference key.** The indirection in `project_credentials` exists because a *project* names its own reference (`SCM_TOKEN_REVIEWER` vs `GITHUB_TOKEN_REVIEWER` — see "Per-provider SCM credentials" above). An instance default has no project and therefore no reference to honour: it is the value for one slot. `provider_id`/`role` are `text` rather than pg enums, like `projects.scm_type`, so a fourth provider opting in needs no migration.
- **A new AAD that names the slot**, `instance:scm:<providerId>:<role>` (`instanceScmCredentialAad`), not a bare `'instance'` constant. This preserves the property `project_credentials` gets from using `projectId` as AAD: a ciphertext moved into another slot's row fails GCM authentication instead of resolving as that provider's secret. Same primitive (`src/db/crypto.ts`), no second encryption scheme.
- **Eligibility is manifest data**, `ScmCredentialRoleSpec.instanceDefault`, mirroring `PmCredentialRoleSpec.inheritsSharedCredential`: the admin surface offers exactly the roles a provider says are installation-wide (`listInstanceDefaultScmRoles`, filtered to *runtime-ready* manifests for `runtimeReadyScmProvider`'s reason), so a fourth provider opts in without an edit to shared code. `webhookSecret` can never opt in — it is tied to one project's own webhook endpoint, so an installation-wide value would be wrong rather than merely redundant — and the conformance suite asserts that structurally. Issue #769 paired that with a tripwire pinning the eligible set to exactly `[{ github, reviewer }]`, because opting a reviewer role in was then a decision made in that provider's own issue; #778 made it installation-wide policy instead, so the suite now asserts the **rule** — every runtime-ready manifest's `reviewer` spec declares it — and a fourth provider inherits the assertion rather than editing a list.

**`resolveScmCredentialOrNull`'s no-fallback rule is untouched, and the creation-time copy does not reintroduce the risk it exists to prevent.** Nothing reads this table at resolution time: a project with no credential of its own still throws naming the project, provider, role and that provider's conventional key. The rule exists because a *runtime* fallback would hand a newly selected GitLab the GitHub secret — the resolved value would silently stop matching the provider the project actually names. Copying a default into a **new** project's own `project_credentials` row at creation is a different thing: after the copy the project's stored credential is its own, attributed to one provider, visible and editable on its Source Control tab, and resolution still reads only that row. Nothing becomes ambient, and nothing changes for an existing project.

That copy is phase 2/2 — `requireInstanceScmDefaults` + `seedInstanceScmCredentials` in `projects.create` (`src/api/routers/projects.ts`), split into a required resolve and a best-effort write by issue #778. These properties are what keep it a *copy* rather than a tier, and a *requirement* rather than a convenience:

- **The project's own provider, and only opted-in roles.** It walks `listInstanceDefaultScmRoles()` filtered to `project.scm` — never a block per registered provider, which is the fallback shape `adoptLegacyScmCredentials` refuses for the same reason. `webhookSecret` can never opt in, so it is never seeded — and since issue #778 a Bitbucket or GitLab project is seeded its own provider's reviewer default, where before it was seeded nothing at all.
- **The destination key is the one the project resolves the role through**, `scmCredentialReferenceFor(...) ?? envVarKey` — the same `scmReferenceKeyFor` rule `projects.credentials.set` writes under (`src/api/routers/credentials.ts`), shared rather than re-derived, so a seeded value and a hand-entered one land on the same row.
- **Re-encrypted under the project's AAD.** The instance value is decrypted under `instance:scm:<providerId>:<role>` and re-encrypted under `projectId` by `writeProjectCredential`, leaving the project row self-contained: rotating or clearing the instance default afterwards cannot reach it, and cannot break it.
- **Required *before* creation, and fail-closed** (issue #778). `requireInstanceScmDefaults` resolves each eligible role ahead of `createProjectWithMemberInDb` and refuses with `PRECONDITION_FAILED` when nothing is stored — naming the provider, the role and General Settings → Credentials, since whoever creates a project is usually not the `instanceAdmin` who can fix it. Nothing is written, and the config is not even assembled, so a request that would both be refused and conflict on id reports the refusal (the more actionable of the two; the conflict arrives on the retry). A lookup *failure* propagates the same way rather than leaving #769's created-but-unseeded project: we cannot claim the credential exists, and re-running `create` after the DB recovers is clean precisely because nothing was written. It throws on the first missing role rather than aggregating — every provider declares at most one eligible role. A provider declaring none is not checked at all, so the AC's hypothetical-removed-role case creates exactly as before.
- **The resolved secret is threaded through, so the row is read once.** The required check hands `seedInstanceScmCredentials` the value it already resolved instead of a boolean; a second read at write time would reopen the window where the default is cleared in between, recreating the silent degradation #778 removes.
- **Only the *write* is best-effort, and it stays outside the creation transaction.** The project and its membership are already committed atomically and `writeProjectCredential` takes no transaction handle, and the value demonstrably exists by then — so a failure here is a transient encryption/DB fault, logged as a `logger.warn` with creation still successful rather than a client-visible error (the recovery is the Source Control tab), while re-running `create` would only earn a `CONFLICT`. That is legitimately best-effort; *not having the credential at all* is what stopped being.
- **The dashboard's requirement alone.** `swarm config apply` / `applyConfig` (`src/config/apply.ts`) is untouched and keeps its deliberately different philosophy: a missing referenced secret there is warned-and-skipped, because applying a config file before every secret is exported is a legitimate partial state for that path.

Nothing re-seeds afterwards: `projects.update`, `swarm config apply` and the Source Control tab's own `projects.credentials.set` are untouched, and bulk-applying a default to already-created projects is deliberately not offered.

Authorization is enforced in the router, not on the screen: every procedure, `list` included, is `instanceAdmin`-only (a local `FORBIDDEN` guard rather than `assertInstanceAdmin`, whose copy describes a read-only installation *view*). That is the deliberate difference from the `agents` tab, whose admin-only-ness is a screen-level visibility boundary with `settings.get`/`update` unchanged — a secret must not be writable, nor its configured state readable, by any authenticated user just because a tab is hidden.

### Per-worker operator SCM credential (issue #765)

The **worker operator's own** account credential — the identity every commit, push, pull request and implementer-side comment a phase makes is authored as, on every worker for every phase since issue #551 made it universal — is a durable, encrypted, per-`(worker, scmProvider)` row in `worker_scm_credentials` (`src/db/schema/workerScmCredentials.ts`). It used to be one process env var, `SWARM_OPERATOR_GH_TOKEN`, read unconditionally at worker startup: no validation, no rotation flow, nothing stopping a duplicate `.env` key from silently shadowing the intended value, and hardcoded to GitHub regardless of `project.scm`.

Four decisions worth not re-deriving:

- **Keyed by `(workerId, scmProviderId)`, so a worker enrolled across providers holds one credential per provider.** `scm_provider_id` is `text` and not a pg enum, for the same reason `projects.scm_type` is: `ScmProviderIdSchema` is the value list's source of truth and a fourth provider must not need a migration. The unique index on the pair is the upsert target, which makes a rotation an update rather than a second row; the FK to `workers` is `ON DELETE CASCADE`.
- **Encrypted with the existing primitive, bound to both parts.** `src/db/crypto.ts` (AES-256-GCM, opt-in on `CREDENTIAL_MASTER_KEY`) with `workerScmCredentialAad(workerId, scmProviderId)` = `<workerId>:<scmProviderId>` — so a ciphertext copied onto another worker's row, or onto the same worker's other provider row, fails GCM authentication instead of resolving. No second encryption scheme.
- **Resolved control-plane side at dispatch, and carried on the assignment frame.** The worker is DB-free by design (ADR-003 §2) and cannot read the store; the control plane is also the only side that knows *which* worker was selected and *which* provider the project targets, so it is the only side that can fail with both names before the run starts. `src/router/dispatcher.ts` resolves `requireProjectSCMProviderId(project)` (a registry lookup with the same three throws as `requireProjectSCMProvider` — never `project.scm ?? 'github'`) and then `requireWorkerScmCredential` (`src/identity/worker-scm-credential.ts`), re-raising a miss as an `AgentRunError` of kind **`error`** so the shared settle path records a run-row reason and posts the usual board/PR failure comment. The kind matters and is easy to get wrong: `pushAndAwaitResult`'s two other pre-push throws are `aborted` and `DeliveryDeferredError`, and `handlePhaseFailure` (`src/worker/consumer.ts`) treats **both as deferrable** — correctly, since both describe a transient race. An unset credential is not transient, so deferring it would retry until the budget ran out and only then surface the one message naming the worker and the provider; `'error'` is the kind `isDeferrable` excludes, the same reason `adaptResultToPhaseRun` uses it for a worker's terminal frame. A consequence worth having: rotation takes effect on the **next dispatch**, with no worker restart — which is what phase 2/3's dashboard form needs.
- **`TaskAssignment.operatorCredential` narrows the frame's secret boundary, deliberately.** `src/transport/assignment.ts` is still the single place the *project* secret boundary is enforced (it derives `NonSecretProjectConfig` itself), and no project credential reaches a worker. The new field is the **worker's own** identity, delivered to the one machine that must perform the commit and the push, over TLS on a socket already authenticated by that worker's own `SWARM_WORKER_CREDENTIAL` — so the older claim that this credential is "never sent over the transport" is superseded for this one credential and nothing else. It is required on `BuildTaskAssignmentInput` (so the control plane cannot assemble a frame without having resolved one) and optional on the wire; `TRANSPORT_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is **not** bumped, following the same convention as `repository` and `boardItemId` — a bump rejects every frame from a mismatched daemon rather than only the affected ones. The skew that leaves is one-directional and loud: a new worker settles a credential-less frame `failed` with an explicit reason, an old worker ignores the field. Rollout order is therefore store → router → workers (`docs/onboarding-worker.md`).

The first write surface is `swarm workers set-scm-credential <worker-id> <scm-provider-id>` (`src/cli/commands/workers.ts`), which validates against `ScmProviderIdSchema` rather than the registry — the closed value list, so validity does not depend on which provider modules the CLI process imported — checks the worker exists, and reads the secret without echo through `src/cli/_shared/secret-input.ts` (never argv).

**The second is the dashboard (issue #766, phase 2/3):** an **Operator source-control credential** card on `/workers/<id>`, over `workers.scmCredentials` (`src/api/routers/workerScmCredentials.ts`, nested into `workersRouter`). Four decisions there:

- **Strict worker ownership on every procedure, the read included.** An `instanceAdmin` who does not own the machine gets the same `NOT_FOUND` a stranger does — the rule `workers.rename`/`setConsent`/`updateConstraints` already apply to the owner's own calls about their own machine, extended to `list` on `instanceCredentials.ts`'s precedent that configured/not-configured state is itself protected. An administrator's path to someone else's worker stays the CLI. (The two guards moved to `src/api/worker-access.ts` so the nested router can use them without importing back from `routers/workers.ts`.)
- **The providers offered are the ones a dispatch would ask for**, not a fixed GitHub field: `listWorkerScmProviders` (`src/identity/worker-scm-credential.ts`) walks the worker's enrollments — *every* status, since a `pending` one is a machine being enrolled — and resolves each project through the dispatcher's own `requireProjectSCMProviderId`, never `project.scm ?? 'github'`. A project whose provider does not resolve is skipped with a debug log rather than surfaced as a slot; the fix there is that project's `scm`. The list is then unioned with the providers a value is already stored for, so a credential outlives the enrollment that motivated it and stays rotatable.
- **Verified against the provider before it is stored.** `set` calls `verifyScmCredentialSecret` (`src/api/scm-verification.ts` — the provider-id → identity-lookup mapping extracted from `routers/scm.ts`, so the two write paths cannot drift) and rejects a value resolving to no account as `BAD_REQUEST` with nothing written, rather than storing it and failing at dispatch time. The identity lookups swallow a transport failure to `null`, so an unreachable provider reads as an invalid credential — inherited from the `scm.verify…` procedures, and the right side to fail on for a write.
- **Write-only.** `list` returns presence plus `updatedAt` and never a value or a masked echo of one, so the card offers **Replace** and no reveal anywhere; the login `set` returns is resolved from the value just pasted, is not persisted, and is never read back. And because resolution is per dispatch, a saved value applies to the next phase with no worker restart — the copy states that rather than a mechanism being added for it.

**Phase 3/3 (#767) is the hand-off, not a third write surface.** `swarm workers register` states in its success output and in `USAGE` that registration issues the *connection* credential alone — the machine has no SCM identity and every dispatch to it fails until an operator credential is stored — and names both write surfaces: the owner's card at `/workers/<worker-id>`, and `set-scm-credential` for a machine someone else owns (the dashboard form is strictly the owner's, so an admin bootstrapping another person's machine needs the CLI path). It deliberately gained **no** token flag and no prompt: the provider a worker ends up on is decided by its enrollments and is unknown at registration time, so prompting for one provider's secret in a generic registration command would hard-code GitHub in exactly the way `ai/RULES.md` §2 forbids. The reminder is printed *before* the credential so the credential stays the last line, which is the "copy the last line" affordance `docs/onboarding-worker.md` relies on.

**`swarm workers register-and-enroll` (issue #786) *is* a third write surface, and it is consistent with #767 rather than a reversal.** It composes `registerWorker` + `writeWorkerScmCredential` + `enrollWorker` into the one command that takes an operator from "no worker" to "enrolled, with the exact line that starts it", and it may prompt for one provider's secret precisely because it is handed the target project and therefore **resolves** the provider through `requireProjectSCMProviderId` — the condition #767 recorded as missing at registration time, not a licence to guess. It writes through the same `writeWorkerScmCredential`, so there is no second validation policy, and — unlike the dashboard form — it does **not** verify the value against the provider first: the CLI path never has. Two things about it are decisions rather than mechanics. Its **ordering** is the design: the CLI list, the owner, the project, the provider resolution and the secret are all resolved *before* `registerWorker`, so no pre-condition failure can orphan a worker row or lose a one-time credential. And a failure *after* registration deliberately diverges from `set-scm-credential`'s standalone behaviour by printing the leftover command(s) plus the worker credential once — a registered worker whose credential was never shown is only recoverable by `workers remove` + `workers register`. The credential is still printed exactly once per invocation, on exactly one of the two outcome paths.
