Nothing semantic is relaxed by it: every rule named above still fails closed, and a slot whose type carries meaning (the verdict, a severity, a category, an `F<n>` id, a document path, a command line) is normalized nowhere. A hand-off that fails that validation gets **one repair pass** — the agent is re-run in the same worktree with the validator's own complaint and the field contract (`buildReviewHandoffRepairPrompt`), resuming its session where one is addressable so it still holds the diff — before the run fails; otherwise the agent never learns why, and the queue re-runs the whole review up to three times over the same mis-shaped JSON. **The pass does not always resume** (issue #865): the shared `repairSessionId`/`acceptsAssignedSessionId` pair (`src/pipeline/resume.ts`, `src/harness/agent-cli.ts`) offers SWARM's *assigned* id only to a CLI that accepts one — `claude`'s `--session-id` — because `codex exec resume <assigned>` names a thread that harness never created and exits 1 on "no rollout found for thread id" in ~200 ms without reaching the model. On a self-minting CLI that reported no id the pass therefore runs **fresh** against the same checkout, and the run's failure message leads with the original validator complaint and appends which of the two happened (or that the pass never ran at all), the original error travelling on `cause`. Because that fresh turn is a first turn, `buildReviewHandoffRepairPrompt` carries `pipelinePhaseGuard()` and `GH_IDENTITY_GUARD` itself instead of inheriting them from the review prompt, and points at `review_handoff.json` on disk for the findings and verdict to preserve rather than at what the agent remembers — the same two adaptations `buildMigrationJournalRepairPrompt` got for the migration-journal repair pass, which resolves its session through the same helper (minus the identity guard, which that token-free pass has never carried). The rendered header names the pass from the ledger ordinal below (`pass 2 of 3`, marked as the final permitted verdict at the cap), derived as `getPriorSubmittedReview`'s ordinal + 1 rather than counted by the agent, whose count a retry would drift; the pass's *name* follows whether it actually is a re-review, so a full pass at ordinal 2 (a push after an approval that didn't merge) reads "Review", not "First re-review". **The verdict vocabulary is two, not three:** `comment` was removed in issue #470 — it mirrored `gh pr review`'s third event flag rather than any SWARM state, and it cleared no review gate, dispatched no Respond-to-review run under the default `skipOnMinors`, consumed a verdict slot anyway, and set no `manual-intervention-required` signal, so a PR that received one looked reviewed and was silently terminal. A reviewer that cannot reach a verdict now fails its run (which retries) instead. This is SWARM's *outbound* vocabulary only — `ScmReviewState` still observes an inbound `commented` review, which humans submit routinely. A durable, restart-safe ledger (`review_verdicts`, one row per `(project, repository, PR, head SHA)`, issue #235) caps every PR at **three** submitted verdicts — an initial review plus at most two **re-reviews** (`REVIEW_VERDICT_CAP`, the single place the number is defined): the `pr-review` trigger reserves a slot before dispatching (reusing a same-head retry's existing slot rather than charging it again) and refuses one past the cap; the phase marks its reservation `submitted` once GitHub confirms the review (idempotently, so a crash between delivery and this write is repaired by a retry without a duplicate submission). **The cap is the *automatic* ceiling, and an operator can deliberately step past it once** ("Force re-review", issue #511): `grantReviewCapOverride` records the override on the capped record's own row (`cap_override_granted_at`), `reserveReviewVerdict` spends one such grant inside the same advisory-locked transaction that creates the slot it pays for — so a grant can never license two reviews — and `isCapReachingRequestChanges` compares `>=` rather than `===`, so the forced pass at ordinal 4 stops the cycle again exactly as the pass it continued did. Nothing SWARM does on its own produces an ordinal above the cap. **The re-review is scoped, not a fresh full pass (issue #328):** when the phase finds a prior `request-changes` verdict already submitted for this PR (`getPriorSubmittedReview`), it builds the re-review variant of the prompt (`buildReviewPrompt(..., isReReview=true)`), which tells the reviewer to verify *only* whether the previously requested changes were implemented correctly — approve when they were, otherwise request changes with a strong, specific fix instruction — and **not** to surface newly-noticed pre-existing issues the first review missed (the only admissible new findings are regressions the fix commits themselves introduced). This keeps each permitted re-review spent on confirming the fix rather than restarting the cycle over unrelated work. Only a submitted `approve` requests a merge (`pipeline.respondToReview.autoMerge`, issue #235) — the worker's Review success path persists one durable merge dispatch per approving run (`requestMergeAutomationIfEligible`, `src/worker/consumer.ts` → `src/worker/merge-automation.ts`, issue #292), and the dispatch's executor performs the direct PAT merge through the provider-neutral `ScmMergeProvider`, retrying transient `not-ready` outcomes — **and, since issue #923, a `provider-error`** — on one shared bounded backoff (15s doubling to 5min, six retries). Every outcome lands on the Review run's `review_merge_*` columns; a terminal refusal (`not-eligible`, `policy-blocked`, `unsupported`) or a spent budget (`retry-exhausted` for a pull request that never became ready, `provider-error-exhausted` for a forge that kept failing) is visible there and on the dispatch record but never fails the completed Review. **A redelivery of an already-reviewed run is recorded as the success it was, and still requests that merge (issue #815).** A Review that rate-limits *after* submitting its verdict is deferred and retried, and the retry re-evaluates its original event: the trigger correctly declines, because the ledger slot for that PR/head is already `submitted`. `processJob`'s shared no-trigger branch (`settleNoTriggerDelivery`) used to write `failed` over every such run with one generic message, conflating "already done" with "nothing was ever done" — so an approved, mergeable PR read as an errored no-op *and* silently lost its merge automation, the success path having never run (seen live on PR #809). A `runId`-bearing redelivery now reads the ledger back (`getSubmittedReviewSlot`, guarded on the run row's own `phase === 'review'` and matching PR), finalizes the run `completed` with the ledger's verdict/ordinal/automation outcome, and re-requests the merge for a submitted `approve` — idempotent on `merge:<reviewRunId>`, so a further redelivery mints no second dispatch. The *dispatch* still completes as `no-trigger` (that delivery genuinely matched nothing); only the run's terminal status changes. Review is the only phase this answers for, because it is the only one with a durable record of its own completion — the per-phase findings for the other four are in `settleNoTriggerDelivery`'s own comment, of which Resolve-conflicts has the same class of bug and no ledger to fix it with. **Every *other* `no-trigger` settle hands back what its own trigger claimed (issue #856).** The Review trigger takes the PR+SHA dedup claim and the durable verdict reservation in the router, before a run row exists, while every hand-back used to be keyed on `job.runId` — so a dispatch deferred for worker capacity that then re-evaluated to `no-trigger` returned neither, and the leaked `pending` row made every later Review of that PR `blocked` at any head (one pending slot per PR *backed by an active dispatch* — see #857 below), leaving it silently unreviewable for life (`rover#116`). The settle now releases both on the not-already-submitted branch, gated on the **dispatch row's own recorded phase** (`recordDispatchResolution`) rather than on a run row, so it covers the no-run case: `review` hands back both, `respond-to-ci` hands back only the shared dedup claim it never reserved against, and any other phase — or a delivery that resolved nothing, such as a sibling event the dedup claim dropped — hands back nothing, because releasing a claim or abandoning a slot that belongs to an in-flight Review is this same bug mirrored. **And the reservation now outlives no dispatch at all (issue #857).** #856 fixed one missed hand-back; the fragility was the class — a `pending` row recorded no owner and no expiry, so "a Review is in flight" and "the dispatch that reserved this died five weeks ago" read identically, for ever, and every future drop path inherited the same trap. A reservation now records the dispatch whose trigger evaluation took it (`review_verdicts.dispatch_id`, written inside the same advisory-locked transaction that inserts the row, and re-pointed when a fresh dispatch reuses a `pending` slot), and `reserveReviewVerdict` — the one reader a leak can harm — honours a blocking `pending` row only while that dispatch is still in `ACTIVE_DISPATCH_STATES`; otherwise it abandons the row and reserves. Putting the rule in the reader rather than adding a hand-back to each terminal settle is what makes it un-forgettable: a missed hand-back degrades to a delay. A Review that is genuinely queued, deferred, leased, running or inside its retry budget is never cleared out from under itself, a `submitted` slot is never touched, and the cap is untouched — a recovered *ordinal* was never a spent verdict, and an operator's override (#511) is evaluated afterwards, so recovery neither spends nor is paid for by it. A null `dispatch_id` reads as "nothing backs this" (a dispatch never recorded, or one the FK's `set null` removed), which is also how the migration settles the five rows that had already leaked. Recovery is lazy by design — it happens at the next reservation attempt, not on a timer, because a legitimately deferred Review can wait hours and "old" is not "abandoned"; a PR that receives no further `checks`/`opened` event keeps its relic, visibly, in the Stalled section (#840/#847).
4. **Respond to review** — by default, the reviewer persona's `changes_requested` review → the implementer addresses the batch and writes a structured response → SWARM commits/pushes any fix and posts the response idempotently. `pipeline.respondToReview.skipOnMinors` defaults to true so approvals and comment-only reviews do not consume a separate agent run; set it false to respond to every reviewer-persona verdict. (SWARM's own Review no longer submits a comment-only verdict at all — issue #470, §3 above — so that clause now covers only a human's comment review arriving as the reviewer persona. Because an approval is skipped by default, a Review that approves with `minor`/`nit` findings states in its rendered body that nothing downstream will act on them, rather than leaving them reading as queued work.) The trigger resolves the submitted review's ledger record (by review id, falling back to PR/head for the narrow webhook race before the Review phase has stored it) and, on the safety cap's **last permitted** `changes_requested` verdict, stops here instead of dispatching — that PR needs a human, and the outcome is recorded as `manual-intervention-required` rather than starting an unbounded further cycle; a lookup failure for a `changes_requested` event fails the same way (closed) rather than risk reopening the cycle on a persistence outage. The one way past that stop is an operator's explicit **Force re-review** (`src/dispatch/force-re-review.ts`, issue #511), whose synthetic review event carries `forcedReReview` — the only flag that stands the cap gate down here, and with it the reviewer-persona gate, since a forced event is SWARM's own ledger record replayed by an operator rather than an inbound webhook whose author still has to be established. Respond-to-review's own outcomes (`fixed`/`pushed-back`/`no-findings`) never request a merge themselves — addressing feedback isn't a fresh approval. Like Implementation, it reports progress on the board as a status *report*, not a trigger: the card moves to "In progress" while the implementer works and back to "In review" once it has responded (best-effort — a card it can't resolve or move is logged and skipped, never failing the response). Neither status starts a phase, so bouncing the card can't re-fire Review; only a new fix commit does.

**Reliable follow-up Review after a `fixed` push (issue #241).** A `fixed` outcome whose commit was actually pushed and differs from the reviewed head reliably enqueues exactly one follow-up Review for the new SHA — reusing the same Review trigger, aggregate-check routing (`decideAggregateCheckOutcome`), reviewer persona, PR/head dispatch dedup, and durable review-verdict ledger a real `checks` webhook would drive, via a synthetic `checks` `completed` event (`src/pipeline/follow-up-review.ts`) carrying the PR number/branch/repo and new head. Unlike `selfEnqueueNextPhase`'s best-effort PM hand-off, this is part of the phase's own deterministic delivery: it runs after the push/comment delivery steps and before the delivery-progress checkpoint (`DeliveryProgress.followUpEnqueued`) is saved, so a queueing failure raises `DeliveryDeferredError` (preserving the worktree for a resumed retry) instead of silently dropping the follow-up, and the enqueued job's id is a deterministic hash of `(project, PR, new head)` so a crash between a successful enqueue and that checkpoint write can't duplicate the job. `pushed-back`, `no-findings`, a failed/unpushed response, and an unchanged head (the pushed commit somehow matching what was reviewed) enqueue nothing. A follow-up Review that approves may still request the same approval-only merge described above; Respond-to-review itself still never requests it.
5. **Respond to CI** — a PR's check suite completes with a failing check → worktree on the PR's task branch → Claude Code (implementer persona) inspects the failing checks, fixes the build surgically and pushes (or reports no code change was warranted) — mirrors Cascade's `respond-to-ci` agent. A per-PR fix-attempt cap stops a never-sticking fix from looping (each fix commit is a new SHA, so it re-triggers CI). **A `no-fix` run hands the pull request back to Review rather than ending the line** (issue #841). Reporting that the red is not this PR's fault pushes nothing, so no further `check_suite` event is ever produced and the `pr-review` trigger — which re-enters only on a *green* `checks` event — left the PR parked in **In review** forever (observed live on `SmartTechBrewery/rover` PR #92, unblocked only by a manual empty commit). The phase now reports its outcome up to the composition root (`PhaseRunResult.ciOutcome`, carried over the transport on `TaskExecutionResult.ciOutcome` — optional and additive, so no `TRANSPORT_PROTOCOL_VERSION` bump), and `processJob` schedules exactly one synthetic recovery dispatch for the same (project, PR, head SHA) beside `selfEnqueueNextPhase`/`requestMergeAutomationIfEligible`, where queue work belongs (`scheduleCiNoFixRecovery`, `src/dispatch/ci-no-fix-recovery.ts`). It **hands the PR+SHA review-dispatch claim over rather than releasing it** — the finished Respond-to-CI dispatch still holds that claim, and it is refreshed (`refreshReviewDispatchClaim`, the same hold `retainContinuationDispatchClaim` uses for a pending continuation) to a TTL sized to outlast the handler's own `state-pending` recheck chain, then the `checks`/`completed` event carrying `ciNoFixRecovery` is enqueued, exactly as the `fixed` Respond-to-review follow-up does (`src/pipeline/follow-up-review.ts`). **The claim is held continuously from the fix to the recovery, not only at the hand-over.** Its ordinary five-minute TTL is shorter than an agent run, so `pr-review` extends it at the moment it dispatches the fix to `ciFixClaimTtlSec(project)` — **this project's effective Respond-to-CI wall clock plus margin**, read as the same `agents.respondToCi.timeoutMs ?? SWARM_AGENT_TIMEOUT_MS` the worker applies when it invokes the agent (`resolveAgentTimeoutMs`, `src/worker/agent-timeout.ts` — split out of `consumer.ts`, which re-exports it, so a trigger can size a lease against the wall clock without pulling the worker's composition root onto its import graph). Without that the slot would fall free mid-run and the "hand-over" would be a re-take of a slot someone else could already hold; and *deriving* it rather than fixing it is what keeps a supported configuration inside the lease — `timeoutMs` is valid to 45 minutes, so a constant sized for the 30-minute default lapsed ~10 minutes into the tail of a legitimately configured run. Owning the slot for the whole window is what makes the hand-back safe against a *delayed* sibling check event: the aggregate decision waits for every suite to complete, so a two-suite PR can have one suite decide the aggregate while the other's webhook is still in flight, and a delayed sibling landing on a free slot would carry no marker — it would route the already-adjudicated red back to Respond-to-CI, spend a second fix attempt, and leave the recovery to fail its own claim and settle `no-trigger`, whereupon the deterministic delivery id absorbs the second run's recovery as a repeat and no Review ever runs. Held instead, the slot is closed to that event, and the marker is also what tells `pr-review` to *reuse* the held claim rather than re-claim and drop itself. The lease is a lease, not a lock: a Redis restart can still leave the slot free, and there the per-PR fix-attempt cap — not the claim — is what bounds a duplicate fix. A concurrency or worker-eligibility deferral does re-shorten the TTL to the continuation lease (`retainContinuationDispatchClaim`), and that is deliberate rather than a gap: those are *pre-run* waits, so no agent is running to outlive the shorter lease, that TTL is sized to outlast the wait, and the prioritized retry re-enters `dispatchRespondToCi` and re-extends the full lease before the run starts. Best-effort: a scheduling failure is logged, never thrown, so it cannot turn an already-succeeded phase into a failed job. **Bounded by construction** — one recovery per PR+head (the deterministic dispatch dedup key), one Review per head (the transferred PR+SHA claim plus the verdict-ledger reservation), and if a later push goes red again the per-PR fix-attempt cap applies as before, at which point the durable give-up row and comment below are what an operator finds. Behaviour on `fixed` is unchanged.

### Trigger wiring (SWARM-53)

The phases are self-contained orchestrators; the trigger registry (`src/triggers/`) is what connects inbound events to them. `registerBuiltInTriggers` (`src/triggers/builtins.ts`) registers four handlers under `src/triggers/handlers/`:

- **`pm-status-changed`** — one handler for both PM-driven phases. It names no provider (issue #297): `matches` delegates the "is this worth waking the pipeline for?" gate to `requireProjectPMAdapter(project).isStatusChange` — the one place that answers it, shared with the router's ingress filter — and `handle` re-reads the item authoritatively through the `PMProvider` the worker injected on the context (`ctx.pm`), never trusting the webhook's Status value (`docs/github-projects-v2-api.md` §5). Per `resolvePipelinePhaseForStatusKey(workItem.statusKey)` it starts Planning (→ Planning) or Implementation (→ ToDo), or returns `null` for any other status — including "In progress", which Implementation itself moves an item *to* as a status report, not a trigger. The Board view's drag-and-drop actually fires GitHub's `reordered` action (no `changes.field_value` at all — a real delivery confirmed this), not `edited`, so the adapter maps it to the neutral `moved` and accepts it alongside `updated`; a Redis-backed dedup (`pm-status-dedup.ts`) stops the resulting harmless within-column reorders from re-dispatching the same phase.
  - **A board move retires the phase an earlier move left queued (issue #909).** The card's current column is the single source of truth for which board-driven phase is queued for it, so `handle` now also *removes* work: before any of the gates below, it calls `retireSupersededBoardPhases` (`src/dispatch/board-phase-retirement.ts`), which cancels every **waiting** board-driven dispatch for the card's task whose phase is not the one this column asks for, and settles that dispatch's run row in the **same transaction** (`retireWaitingBoardDispatch`). **The run settle spans every `UNSETTLED_RUN_STATUSES` — `running` as well as the `deferred`/`checkpointed` pair — and is half of a two-sided guard,** because a waiting dispatch paired with a *running* run is a real state rather than an inconsistency: `deferBeforeRun` (`src/worker/consumer.ts`) creates the run, hands its dispatch back to `pending`, and only then writes `deferred` on the run, so a board move landing between those last two steps finds exactly that pairing. Settling only the retry-pending pair would leave the row `running` for the worker to finish writing `deferred` over, and `listDeferredRunsWithoutActiveDispatch` — the startup backfill — would re-dispatch the phase the card had just retired. The other side is that deferral's own settle, which passes `CompleteRunInput.fromStatus: 'running'` (the atomic guard `resetRunToRunning` already had), so a retirement that got there first makes the late `deferred` write a no-op instead of resurrecting the run. Neither side ever rewrites a `completed`/`failed` run: one that settled itself keeps its own outcome. Planning → ToDo leaves only Implementation queued; ToDo → Planning leaves only Planning; a column that starts no phase at all (Backlog, In review, Done) retires and enqueues nothing — the same rule, not a second one. Before this a board move only ever *added* work, so a card moved twice accumulated one queued phase per move and issue #761 then made them queue behind each other; the only cure was Terminate on the stale run. The key is `(project_id, task_id, phase, state)` — what the dispatch record already normalizes, never a payload path (ai/RULES.md §2) — and four exemptions carry the rest of the rule: only `WAITING_DISPATCH_STATES`, so stopping a `leased`/`running` phase stays Terminate's job; only `BOARD_DRIVEN_PHASES`, so a `review` sharing the bare `task-<pr>` id is never swept up by a card's move; never on `resumePmPhase`, a deferred PM phase resuming from its original event; never on an unchanged status (the existing within-column reorder gate); never on a status a *phase reports about itself* (`PM_PHASE_REPORTED_STATUS_KEYS` — `inProgress`, which Implementation moves its own card to, and retiring on which would kill that very phase's rate-limit retry); and never on a dispatch whose trigger has not run yet, whose `phase` is still null. It **fails open** — logged and counted as zero — because the failure direction is the pre-#909 behaviour, whereas throwing would fail a board dispatch that is otherwise perfectly good.
  - **The Planning gate is the `planned` label, and nothing else (issue #737).** A card entering Planning starts a Planning run unless it already carries `planned`; **to re-plan, an operator removes `planned` and moves the card Backlog → Planning.** A deferred phase resuming from its original event (`resumePmPhase`) is exempt. That replaced three concepts, one of which could not fire: a gate keyed on the preplan contract embedded in a split child's description plus its `swarm:split-child` label, an operator override label `swarm:replan` that invalidated the contract (**removed — nothing reads it, nothing creates it**), and a `preplan-invalidated` handler registered here to observe those label/body edits in place. The last one required a `work-item` event, which only GitHub's `issues` event maps to (`src/integrations/scm/github/webhook.ts`) — and the repository webhook subscribes to `check_suite`, `issue_comment`, `pull_request` and `pull_request_review`, never `issues` — so none of its three cases could happen; it is deleted rather than left registered and unreachable, and no `issues` subscription is needed to replace it. The preplan contract itself stays, demoted from gate to **content**: a run that *is* dispatched still reuses a split child's embedded plan rather than spending an agent (`evaluatePreplan`, `src/pipeline/preplan.ts`), and its `descriptionHash` still refuses a plan written against since-edited scope, because "should this dispatch happen" and "is the plan this dispatch found still about the same work" are different questions. A split child costs no agent run because the split labels it `planned` **before** moving it to Planning — the move is the event that dispatches, so labelling after it would race every child into the dispatch the label exists to stop.
- **`pr-review`** — the PR-lifecycle handler. Starts Review on a non-draft, **same-repo**, **SWARM-managed** PR opening or its checks passing, and routes a *failing* check suite to Respond-to-CI. A `review` disposition also reserves the durable review-verdict safety-cap slot (see Pipeline phases §3) after the dispatch dedup claim; `respond-to-ci` never consumes one. On a `checks` completion it re-queries *every* check on the head SHA (`SCMProvider.getAggregateCheckStatus`) rather than trusting the single event's own conclusion — a provider fires one event per workflow — and decides via `aggregate-check-decision.ts`: review if all complete and none failed, **respond-to-ci** if a check failed, or **defer** if a check is still running, scheduling a coalesced recheck (`scheduleCoalescedDispatch`) that re-queries fresh state. **A defer draws on one of two budgets, classified by why the handler could not decide** (issue #720): a *state-pending* defer — the provider answered but not finally (a check still running, no checks registered, `mergeable` still `null`) — spends `recheckAttempt` on a fixed ~30s cadence, capped at 20; a *read-failed* defer — the read never answered, because the provider threw (unreachable, 5xx, rate-limited, no resolvable reviewer token) or the run-history lookup threw — spends its own `readFailureRecheckAttempt`, capped at 15 on a capped exponential backoff (30s doubling to 5 min, ~62 min of outage outlasted). A read-failed defer carries `recheckAttempt` through untouched and a defer that *did* get an answer clears the read-failure count, so a source-control outage can no longer draw down the allowance reserved for CI that is still running — the failure mode that dropped a synthetic follow-up Review (`src/pipeline/follow-up-review.ts`), which has no later webhook to fall back on, when 17 of 20 attempts went to a DNS failure. Both caps stay bounded: for a webhook-driven review the fresh `checks` webhook every completing suite emits re-triggers anyway, but for that synthetic follow-up exhausting a budget really does abandon the review, which is why the reasons no longer share one. **A closed pull request ends a chain before either budget is touched** (issue #772): the mergeability gate reads the contract's `PullRequestDetails.state` — the same neutral `open`/`closed` pair `CommitPullRequest.state` carries, normalized by every provider — and skips a closed or merged pull request outright, because no provider ever reports a final `mergeable` for one. Without it a chain still in flight when the PR merged polled to its cap and reported a `failed` run for work that had already finished (PR #768: a stray `checks` chain kept polling for ~16 minutes after the PR was reviewed, approved and merged). That is a plain skip with no durable trace and no comment — the give-up records below are for a provider that failed to answer, not for a question that stopped mattering. **A terminal give-up leaves a durable trace** (issue #742): whichever path exhausts its budget writes one `failed` Review run (`createFailedRun`, `src/db/repositories/runsRepository.ts`) naming the pull request, head SHA, exhausted budget, attempts spent and last read, and carrying this event's payload so the abandonment is retryable from the dashboard. It goes to SWARM's own Postgres rather than the provider precisely because the give-up is usually *caused* by that provider being unreachable — in the incident behind issue #720 the give-up comment failed against the same DNS failure and the abandoned review left no trace anywhere, settling as an ordinary `no-trigger`. The pull-request comment (`scmCommentReviewGaveUp`, worded per budget) stays a best-effort extra on the mergeability path; the aggregate-check path, which posts none, is covered by the record alone. Fork PRs are dropped from the Review path (their head SHA is unreachable for the detached review checkout). A **work-item origin gate** (`isSwarmManagedPullRequest`, `src/triggers/swarm-managed-pr.ts`, issue #397) reviews only PRs SWARM manages, so a human- or third-party PR never burns a review: the PR's head branch must be exact `<branchPrefix><taskId>` (digits) **and** SWARM must hold an `implementation` run row for that work item in that project (status-agnostic — Implementation opens the PR from inside its own still-running process). It replaced an author-persona gate, which stopped carrying the signal once the implementer credential became the worker operator's own GitHub account (ADR-004 §3): a federated PR is authored by a plain user, and the control plane cannot even resolve another operator's identity. The gate is evaluated **once** for both entry events, inside the mergeability check that already fetches the PR — so it still runs *before* the `checks` path's heavier aggregate query, and that path no longer needs an author PR read at all. A failed run-history lookup degrades to the same bounded mergeability recheck as a failed PR fetch rather than dropping a legitimate review — both on the read-failure budget above, since neither read answered. The respond-to-ci dispatch reuses the same per-SHA dedup slot (review and respond-to-ci are mutually exclusive for a commit) and adds a per-PR fix-attempt cap (`respond-to-ci-attempts.ts`) so a fix that never makes CI green can't loop forever. **That cap leaves a durable trace too** (issue #838): the event that *first* crosses it (`claim.attempt === MAX_FIX_ATTEMPTS + 1`) writes one `failed` `respond-to-ci` run under task id `<pr>-ci` naming the pull request, head SHA, spent budget and failing checks, saying the pull request now needs a human, and carrying the same counter-free payload so a dashboard "Retry now" re-enters the handler — plus a best-effort pull-request comment (`scmCommentCiFixGaveUp`), an extra on top of the row exactly as on the Review side. Every later red `checks` event on a capped PR keeps the plain warn-and-drop, so a capped pull request is noticed once per cycle rather than once per check suite. **One narrow bypass routes a still-red head to Review instead** (issue #841): a `checks` event carrying `ciNoFixRecovery` — set only by SWARM's own `no-fix` recovery (Pipeline phases §5), never by an inbound webhook — means this head's red was already adjudicated by SWARM's Respond-to-CI agent as not the pull request's fault, so `resolveAggregateCheckReview` returns a `review` disposition rather than routing back to the phase that just declined, and no fix attempt is spent. Reviewing without a green `checks` event is precedented by `pipeline.review.checks: 'if-present'` (issue #274); this narrows it to that one case. Every other gate still applies unchanged — the closed-PR skip, the work-item origin gate, the draft/fork skip, the mergeability/conflicts route, the `state-pending` defer, the PR+SHA dispatch dedup and the durable review-verdict reservation all run exactly as they do for any other event. **A second judgement sits beside it, about a red the base branch already explains** (issue #873). On an aggregate that is *already* red — never on the green path, so the healthy case adds no provider call — `resolveAggregateCheckReview` reads the health of **the base branch this pull request actually targets** — `PullRequestDetails.baseBranch`, carried out of the mergeability gate's existing PR read rather than re-derived from `project.baseBranch`, because a retargeted pull request builds against the base it now names and a red project base neither explains its failures nor may withhold its fix — via `readBaseBranchHealth(project, provider, baseBranch)` (`src/dispatch/base-branch-health.ts`, the detection half of issue #872, whose branch is a parameter for exactly this reason and to which the periodic sweep passes `project.baseBranch`), and, when that base is red at its current head with checks whose **names intersect** this pull request's failing ones, resolves a `blocked-by-base` disposition instead of `respond-to-ci`: nothing is dispatched, no fix attempt is claimed, no review-verdict slot is reserved and the PR+SHA dedup claim is deliberately *not* taken (it is returned before all three), so the slot stays free for the Review a re-run against a fixed base is meant to produce. What it does instead is one structured `info` log and one best-effort comment through the **delivery seam**, keyed `deliveryIdentity(['base-blocked', repo, pr, headSha, baseBranch, baseHeadSha])` — once per (pull-request head, base branch, base head) triple rather than once per check event, since a red pull request emits one `checks` event per workflow and a base head that advances is a new fact. It **fails open in both directions**: a base that could not be read (`unknown`) and a red base whose failures are disjoint from this pull request's both route to Respond-to-CI exactly as before, because withholding a legitimate fix over an unreadable base is worse than spending one attempt on a red the base did explain, and without the intersection every self-broken pull request would hide behind a coincidentally red base. `readBaseBranchHealth` reads the base's *current* head while these checks were built against the base as of their own run — the check-name intersection is what makes that approximation robust. A disabled `pipeline.respondToCi` silences the comment too: a project that turned the CI-fix phase off has opted out of SWARM speaking about its failing checks at all. **A blocked pull request is not re-dispatched when the base recovers** — nothing here waits, the pull request is left where it is, and what unblocks it is the fresh head and fresh CI run issue #874's base-update path produces; until then a human re-running CI works as it does today. **A pull request that is ready and unreviewed re-dispatches its own Review (issue #862).** In practice `checks completed` is the *only* event that ever starts a Review for a green pull request — a mergeable PR on `pull-request updated` is deliberately skipped, and that event never fires twice for one head — so every way a Review can die after it left the pull request green, mergeable and permanently unreviewed with nothing scheduled: `rover#116` (a leaked `pending` ledger row blocked the dispatch, so no run row ever existed — issue #856) and `swarm#860` (the run failed terminally — issue #861) reached the identical dead end from unrelated causes, both cleared only by a human. `src/dispatch/unreviewed-pr-recovery.ts` breaks it with a periodic sweep on the router (its own 15-minute interval beside the reconciler's, `startControlPlaneDispatch`), chosen over #841's terminal-failure hand-back precisely because that shape needs a run to hand back *from* — which neither `rover#116` nor the largest unenumerated failure of all, a `checks` webhook lost while the control plane was offline, has. Per repository it lists the open pull requests (`listConflictCandidates`, the existing seam — so, on GitHub, read under the implementer persona, i.e. the router's own `SWARM_OPERATOR_GH_TOKEN`, exactly as the `resolve-conflicts` fan-out reads it; a router without it degrades to a logged warn and no recovery) and gates each candidate cheapest-first: open, `mergeable === true`, work-item origin, no active dispatch for the pull request (`hasActiveDispatchForPullRequest` — all of `ACTIVE_DISPATCH_STATES` and every phase, so an in-flight Review, a writing phase holding the head (#850), a capacity-blocked dispatch and a bounded retry all read as *something is still due*), the review-verdict ledger, and only then one aggregate-checks read fed to the same `decideAggregateCheckOutcome` under the project's own policy. It **constructs trigger input and nothing else** — one synthetic `checks`/`completed` event carrying *no* marker, so the handler takes the PR+SHA claim itself and a race with a real webhook resolves to one Review — and every gate above is bounded three ways: `MAX_RECOVERY_ATTEMPTS` (3) deterministic per-attempt dedup keys per (project, repository, PR, head), where an attempt is only ever *spent* on a dispatch `isDeadDispatch` (`src/dispatch/dead-dispatch.ts`, extracted from `force-re-review.ts` so the two recoveries share one judgement) calls dead; the PR+SHA claim; and the ledger itself, so the #235 cap and #511's override are never circumvented and no extra verdict is ever burned. **The boundary with #856/#857 is deliberate:** any `pending` slot reads as `review-in-flight`, so a *leaked* reservation still blocks recovery — telling a live reservation from a leaked one is their fix, and disturbing a live Review is what criterion 5 forbids. **#856/#857 free the slot; this sweep supplies the re-dispatch neither of them provides.**
- **`resolve-conflicts`** — a side-car handler for merged pull requests. It lists the open same-repository PRs targeting the advanced base and keeps the ones the **work-item origin gate** claims (`src/triggers/swarm-managed-pr.ts` — the same gate `pr-review` uses, shared since issue #836 so a change cannot fix one trigger and leave the other behind), retries GitHub's asynchronous mergeability result with bounded coalesced jobs, and dispatches only confirmed conflicts. It used to filter on the PR's *author*, which skipped every federated PR — the implementer credential is the worker operator's own account (ADR-004 §3) — so conflict resolution never fired on a project served by remote workers. Ownership is decided before mergeability on the per-PR path, so a PR that is not ours spends no recheck; a run-history lookup failure defers on that same recheck budget rather than dropping the candidate, and in the fan-out an undecided candidate is kept rather than silently disappearing. Redis dedup keys each attempt by PR/head/base state; the implementer phase merges the base normally and checks the remote head before pushing. **A merged `src/db/migrations/` conflict gets a deterministic backstop, not just prompt guidance (issue #503/#508's own incident).** Drizzle's numbered `.sql` files and `meta/_journal.json` are generated artifacts with invariants a generic "preserve both sides' intent" merge does not know to hold — every journal entry must name a `.sql`/snapshot file that exists, and `when` must strictly increase across entries, or `PgDialect.migrate` silently *skips* an out-of-order entry rather than erroring on it. Confirmed live: three resolve-conflicts merges into one long-lived branch left the journal naming a `.sql` file that was never committed and gave the branch's own renumbered migration a `when` earlier than the entry `main` had already moved past — reaching review as two brand-new blocking findings unrelated to anything the implementer had actually been asked to fix, on every pass a further merge happened. The prompt now tells the agent to resolve a migrations-folder conflict by keeping `main`'s migrations untouched and running `npx drizzle-kit generate` for whatever this branch's schema still needs, rather than hand-editing the journal/snapshot — and `runResolveConflictsPhase` still runs `validateMigrationJournal` (`src/db/migration-journal.ts`) against the merged worktree before any commit/push, giving the same agent session **one** repair pass with the validator's own complaint (mirroring `readReviewSubmission`/`repairReviewHandoff`'s shape in `src/pipeline/review.ts`) and failing the phase — nothing delivered — if the journal is still inconsistent after that. **The guard's subject is the directory, not just the journal (issue #907).** A repository with no `src/db/migrations/` at all has nothing to validate and passes untouched — the validator answers clean before it looks for a journal — because the guard's path is this repo's own hardcoded layout, and reporting its absence as a violation no agent could repair failed the whole phase, after one wasted repair pass, on every project without that folder (three of the five live ones, each left with a permanently conflicted PR). A migrations directory that *exists* with a missing or inconsistent journal beside it is still a real inconsistency and still fails every check it did before. **A second deterministic backstop settles the index the delivery gate actually reads (issue #844).** The agent verifies its merge by scanning the files for conflict markers; `validatePreparedTree` asks the index (`git diff --name-only --diff-filter=U`) — different questions, and only the second one gates delivery, so on PR #98 both files were resolved correctly, neither was `git add`-ed, and delivery refused a tree that was by content perfectly deliverable. `settleMergeResolution` (`src/pipeline/merge-resolution.ts`) now runs right after the migration guard, inside the same pre-commit window: it enumerates the unmerged paths with **that same command** — using the identical question is what makes the two definitions unable to drift — and stages each one whose working-tree file carries no `<<<<<<<`/`>>>>>>>`/`|||||||` marker, logging what it staged at `warn` so the agent's drift stays attributable. It keys on those three markers only, never on `=======`, which is ordinary Markdown underlining (both incident files were Markdown) and would refuse a fine delivery; and it deliberately leaves a genuine ambiguity — markers still present, the working-tree file deleted, a binary conflict — unmerged, so it reaches issue #839's terminal refusal naming the paths rather than being silently decided. The prompt states the same index question, but the backstop is the guarantee. **And the phase reads the agent's own verification rather than only accepting a pass (issue #924).** `ConflictHandoffSchema`'s verification entries carry `passed`, `pre-existing-failure` or `failed`, with a required `detail` on the two failing outcomes — the same move `ReviewVerificationSchema` made for Review (#470/#861), for the same reason: an agent that ran the suite, found failures and *proved* on a pristine unmerged head that they predate the merge had no slot for that result, so its only options were to write `"passed"` or to fail hand-off validation, which discards the whole run with the resolution already done (confirmed live, twice in one day). `assertMergeVerified` (`src/pipeline/resolve-conflicts.ts`) reads the result immediately after `readHandoff` and before the migration repair pass: a `pre-existing-failure` is delivered and logged at `warn`, so a merge shipped with failures stays attributable, while a `failed` entry — the agent's claim that *this merge* broke it — throws there, naming the commands and the agent's own detail, with nothing committed, pushed or commented. A plain `Error`, not an `UnretryableDeliveryError`, because a fresh attempt resolves the merge from scratch and can genuinely produce a tree that builds. **The gate runs a second time when the migration repair pass above actually ran**, because the hand-off it reads is a file `buildMigrationJournalRepairPrompt` explicitly tells that pass to rewrite when its fix changes `body` or `verification` — so `guardMigrationJournal` reports whether it attempted a repair, and on that path the phase re-reads the hand-off, re-gates it, and binds the commit, the push and the posted comment body to the re-read copy. Without it the refusal held only for hand-offs written before the repair pass: a pass that regenerated the migration, re-ran the suite and reported `failed` would have been committed, pushed and commented with the pre-repair body claiming success. The merge that needed no repair keeps exactly one gate and one file read.
- **`pr-review-submitted`** — starts Respond-to-review on a `pull_request_review` `submitted` with `changes_requested`, authored by the *reviewer* persona (`getPersonaForLogin`). `pipeline.respondToReview.skipOnMinors: false` restores dispatch for approve verdicts (and for an inbound `commented` one — a state SWARM's own Review stopped producing in issue #470, but which a human still submits). Stops instead of dispatching on the safety cap's last permitted `changes_requested` verdict (see Pipeline phases §4).

A matched handler returns a `TriggerResult` naming the phase plus its resolved inputs; the worker's `processJob` (`src/worker/consumer.ts`) dispatches on that phase and calls the matching `runXPhase`, resolving the project's `PMProvider` from the PM registry (`requireProjectPMProvider`) for the two board-driven phases and for the automation-label gate on the four SCM phases (which fails open if that resolution cannot be read). The review handler is guarded by a cross-process dedup claim (`src/triggers/review-dispatch-dedup.ts`, SWARM-62): a Redis `SET NX EX` keyed on `owner/repo:pr:headSha`, claimed before dispatch and normally left to expire by TTL (a dispatch that provably ran nothing hands it back instead — one the automation-label gate or the PR-scoped hold skipped, or one that re-evaluated to `no-trigger` *and* had itself resolved the `review`/`respond-to-ci` that took the claim, which since issue #856 is read off the dispatch row's recorded phase rather than requiring a run row; a *failed* run still leaves it to the TTL, and since issue #815 so does a `no-trigger` re-evaluation whose ledger slot for that PR/head is already `submitted` — that one declined *because* the review was delivered, so handing the claim back would let a sibling event post a duplicate), so a PR that opens *and* then passes checks (or emits several check-suite successes) is reviewed once per commit rather than once per event. It fails closed — a claim it can't obtain (duplicate, or Redis down) skips the dispatch — and the respond-to-ci path shares that same slot before its own attempt cap runs.

`processJob` also self-enqueues the hand-off from Planning's `autoAdvance` to Implementation, rather than waiting on GitHub to echo that move back as a webhook (`selfEnqueueNextPhase`, `src/worker/consumer.ts`). The router's loop-prevention gate (`PMRouterAdapter.isSelfAuthored`) correctly drops *every* board status-change webhook authored by a SWARM persona — but Planning moves its own card to "ToDo" using the `implementer` persona, the very identity that gate checks against, so the direct webhook for that move is dropped every time by design. Left as webhook-only, the pipeline only ever advanced when some other, differently-attributed event for the same item happened to arrive later — unbounded, and observed live leaving an item in "ToDo" for hours with Implementation never dispatching. Instead, once a PM-driven phase's own move lands on a status that starts the next phase (`resolvePipelinePhaseForStatusKey`, `src/pm/pipeline.ts`), the worker enqueues a synthetic `pm` job for the same item — its event built by the provider's own `synthesizeStateChange`, since assembling one needs the board mapping the adapter owns — directly onto the BullMQ queue (`enqueueJob`, no `deliveryId` — the same "synthetic/manual" carve-out `src/queue/producer.ts` already documents), which re-enters the normal trigger-match → authoritative-re-read → dedup path a real webhook would. Best-effort: a failure to self-enqueue is logged, not thrown, so it can't turn an already-succeeded phase into a failed job.

## Base-branch health (issue #872 phase 1)

**Nothing in SWARM watched the base branch until this landed.** A merge that broke `main` was only ever noticed *indirectly*, through some unrelated pull request whose CI then failed on the merge of its own head with the broken base — so the failure presented as belonging to a diff that had nothing to do with it, and a human had to infer the real cause from the wrong symptom. `src/dispatch/base-branch-health.ts` makes the base's own state a first-class observation instead.

**It changes no lifecycle.** Nothing is dispatched, no phase runs, and no merge decision, trigger disposition, or review verdict is affected — which is what makes it safe on its own, ahead of the two halves that *act* on the answer (per-pull-request attribution, issue #873; prevention, issue #874 — both landed since, the latter inside the merge lifecycle, see "Durable dispatch state machine" below). It is scheduled at the composition root beside the reconciler and the unreviewed-PR sweep (`startControlPlaneDispatch`, `src/router/dispatcher.ts`), on its own `unref()`'d interval cleared in `close()`, and it lives under `src/dispatch/` for the reason `unreviewed-pr-recovery.ts` does: it is router-scheduled work, not work a phase creates.

**Cadence: 5 minutes, coded, not configurable** (`BASE_BRANCH_HEALTH_SWEEP_INTERVAL_MS`, on `DISPATCH_CONSUMER_CONCURRENCY`'s precedent — detection latency is not an operator dial). Shorter than the unreviewed-PR sweep's 15 on both halves of the same trade-off: a healthy pass here is **two provider reads per repository** against that sweep's list read plus one read per candidate, and a base left red silently costs every open pull request a full round of CI. It is deliberately **not** the reconciler's five minutes, which is tunable via `SWARM_STALE_RUN_SWEEP_INTERVAL_MS` — the values coincide and neither should ever be changed because the other was.

**What it reads.** Per repository of every project (`listAllProjectRecordsFromDb` → `scopeProjectToRepository`, so a multi-repository project — issue #685 — is swept per repository, each against its own base branch), with the provider resolved through `requireProjectSCMProvider` and the repository skipped on a `debug` log when that throws — never a fallback. Then `readBaseBranchHealth(project, provider, project.baseBranch)`: `SCMProvider.getBranchHead(project, baseBranch)` for the head SHA, `getAggregateCheckStatus` for every check on it, and the **same** classifier a pull request's head is judged by — `decideAggregateCheckOutcome` (`src/triggers/handlers/aggregate-check-decision.ts`) under the project's own `pipeline.review.checks` policy — mapped onto `green` / `red` / `unsettled`, with a throw or a head-less answer becoming `unknown`. It is exported separately from the sweep, kept free of reporting side effects, and takes the branch as a **parameter** rather than reading `project.baseBranch`, precisely because issue #873 reuses it to decide whether a pull request's red CI is its own fault — and that question is about the base *that* pull request targets, which a retarget can move off the project's configured one. **All three reads pass the `implementer` persona explicitly**, because the contract methods disagree on their own defaults (`getAggregateCheckStatus` and `listPullRequestsForCommit` default to the *reviewer*, `listConflictCandidates` hard-codes the implementer) and the router holds only the operator credential. Only two gates apply: the provider must resolve, and that is all — a repository is swept regardless of `pipeline.review.enabled` (base health is not Review) and there is no automation-label gate (that label is per work item; this asks about a branch).

**How it reports, only on `red`.** One structured `error` log naming the project, repository, base branch, head SHA and failing check names, **every pass** while the base stays red so an ongoing incident stays loud; plus **one** comment on the pull request `listPullRequestsForCommit` returns for that head — the pull request whose merge produced it — warning that open pull requests build the merge of their head with this base. The comment goes through the **delivery seam** (`provider.deliveryProvider(project, 'implementer').postComment`), not `commentOnPullRequest`: its `<!-- swarm-delivery:… -->` marker is what makes the notice land exactly once per red head with no new durable state, where `commentOnPullRequest` would re-comment every five minutes. Its `deliveryId` is `deliveryIdentity(['base-red', project.repo, project.baseBranch, headSha])`, with `project.repo` in the hash for the reason `unreviewedPrRecoveryDeliveryId` states. The body's `SWARM_GENERATED_FOOTER` is load-bearing rather than decoration: ingress drops the resulting comment event through `isSwarmGeneratedBody`'s `SWARM_GENERATED_SIGNATURE` match, not through the delivery marker. Reporting on the merged pull request is a deliberate choice — SWARM has no operator alert channel, and the merged pull request is the most addressable artifact available, names the change that broke the base, and notifies its participants. **The base head need not come from a pull request at all** (a direct push, or a provider that has not indexed the commit): `listPullRequestsForCommit` then answers `[]`, an ordinary answer, and the pass degrades to log-only. Every lookup or post failure is caught and logged at `warn` — a report that cannot be delivered must not abort the pass.

## Task identity (issue #498)

**`taskId` is SCM-derived, and stays that way.** It names the worktree (`task-<id>`), pairs with the branch (`<branchPrefix><id>`) and with the pull request opened from that branch — all SCM artifacts. Deriving it from a PM key instead (a Jira `PROJ-123`, a Trello card id) would rename directories and branches without making anything more portable, and would break every existing worktree, branch, and run row for no gain. So the *shape* of a task id does not change when the board provider does.

What changed is **who derives it**. Two seams carry the card↔artifact relationship now, and neither is a URL pattern:

- **Card → task: `WorkItem.taskRef` + `WorkItem.taskRepository`** (`src/pm/types.ts`). The provider resolves its card's SCM artifact reference from its own linkage and hands it back on the item — GitHub Projects fills it from the backing Issue/PR's `number`, read in the same GraphQL round-trip that already yields `contentNumber`. Fields rather than a 16th `PMProvider` method, for the same reason as `statusKey`: the provider holds the linkage during its own board read. `undefined` means the card has no SCM artifact (a draft card, or a board with no SCM linkage at all), and an SCM-driven phase cannot run for it — `pm-status` logs and skips, exactly as it always has for a draft.
  - **The pair travels together, and the repository is the *card's* (issue #710).** A bare number names nothing, so `taskRepository` reports the `owner/repo` the linkage itself named — never the repository the provider's `ProjectConfig` happens to be scoped to. That is what lets a board-wide read answer per card: a board is project-wide, so one page can hold cards linked in several of the project's repositories, where matching them all against one scoped `repo` silently resolved nothing for the rest. GitHub Projects reports the backing Issue/PR's own `repository { nameWithOwner }` — the same authority `resolveItemRepository` routes on — rather than a declared token.
  - **Whether that repository is one the project owns is decided by the caller, not the provider.** A provider is built from a config *scoped to one repository*, which deliberately carries no repository list at all (`scopeProjectToRepository`; "Project record vs. scoped project config" above), so it cannot know. `src/triggers/handlers/pm-status.ts` — the one consumer — compares `taskRepository` against the run's own scoped `project.repo` through `repoSlugsMatch` (`src/scm/repo-slug.ts`, so casing and a `.git` suffix are noise, matching how ingress routed the card in the first place) and skips with both repositories logged when they disagree. So an honest miss stays a miss and a wrong-repository guess is impossible, and the *shared* code still holds no URL regex and no `pm.type` branch. It also closes a live defect: GitHub Projects used to hand `pm-status` a foreign card's issue number with no repository comparison at all, so a card for another repository sitting on a shared org board dispatched a phase in the wrong place.
  - **Linear answers it from a GitHub *issue* attachment** (issue #530's provider, `src/integrations/pm/linear/provider.ts`) — Linear's GitHub integration records the link as an issue attachment, so that URL's number *is* the SCM artifact reference and its `owner/repo` is the repository. Three deliberate consequences: a *pull-request* attachment does not qualify, since a PR link is an artifact of the task rather than its id; the Linear issue's **own `number`** is never used, because it would name an unrelated GitHub issue in a PR's closing keyword; and an unlinked card (including one this provider just created) leaves both fields unset and cannot start an SCM-driven phase, which is the honest answer rather than a guess. `findWorkItemForArtifact` is the same linkage read backwards, through `attachmentsForURL`. Shared code reads `workItem.taskRef`; the old `issueNumberFromUrl` regex over `WorkItem.url` in `src/triggers/handlers/shared.ts` is gone.
  - **Jira answers it from a GitHub *issue* remote link** (issue #490's provider, `src/integrations/pm/jira/provider.ts`), under the same three rules: a pull-request link doesn't qualify, the Jira issue **key** is never a task id, and an unlinked card leaves both fields unset. Two Jira-specific costs are visible rather than hidden: remote links are a separate round trip, so the pair is filled only on the reads that answer with a single card (a whole-board read leaves both unset — the one provider that meets the per-card rule by resolving nothing), and Jira indexes nothing on a remote link's URL, so `findWorkItemForArtifact` is a **capped scan** of the board's cards rather than a lookup.
  - **Trello answers it from a GitHub *issue* URL on the card** (issue #492's provider, `src/integrations/pm/trello/provider.ts`) — attachments first, then the description — under the same three rules, with the same `owner/repo` read off the matched URL.
  - **The one case a card can still be keyed against a repository the operator did not intend** is a card carrying GitHub issue links in *two* repositories: the first match wins, since only the caller knows which repositories the project owns and the provider cannot rank them without re-introducing the scope it was just taken off. Previously the scope silently picked; now the pair is reported and, if it is not the run's repository, refused and logged.
- **PR → card: `runs.work_item_id`** (`src/db/schema/runs.ts`). Every board-driven run persists the card it came from next to its `(project_id, task_id)`, so a later PR-driven phase recovers the card from SWARM's own durable link rather than guessing a URL: `findBoardItemIdForTask` (`src/db/repositories/runsRepository.ts`) returns the most recent run for that pair carrying a card. The task id it keys on comes from the PR branch (`issueNumberFromBranch` + the project's own `branchPrefix`), which is SWARM's naming convention, not a provider's.

That lookup is resolved **control-plane side**, where the DB is — by the assignment builder (`src/router/dispatcher.ts` → `boardItemId` on `TaskAssignment`), for every dispatch since issue #551 left one execution path — and injected into the phase, because a federated worker must not need a database (ADR-003 §2 / ADR-004 §3). `src/pipeline/respond-to-review.ts`'s `resolveBoardItemId` prefers the injected id and falls back to `PMProvider.findWorkItemByUrlSuffix('/issues/<n>')` only when there is none — a PR opened before this change, or a run row that was pruned. **That fallback is the last GitHub-shaped assumption in the card↔artifact mapping**, deliberately kept so pre-existing PRs keep reporting board status; it simply resolves nothing for a board whose item URLs have another shape, and the report is best-effort anyway.

Rejected alternative: stamping a `<!-- swarm:work-item id=… -->` marker into the PR body. It would need `PullRequestDetails` (`src/scm/types.ts`) to carry `body`, implemented by all three SCM providers — a wider blast radius for the same result — and it would still not help a PR created before the marker existed.

**Two sequential phases sharing one `taskId` is required, not a smell** (issue #759). Planning and Implementation both resolve their id from the same card (`src/triggers/handlers/pm-status.ts`, one handler serving both board-driven phases), because they work in the same `task-<id>` checkout on the same `issue-<n>` branch — giving Planning its own would put the plan somewhere Implementation does not read. The PR-driven phases suffix theirs (`<pr>-respond`, `<pr>-conflicts`, `<pr>-ci`) for the opposite reason: they run *alongside* a Review holding `task-<pr>`, so they need distinct checkouts. **That leaves what those four phases *share* — the pull request — with no key of its own, which is issue #850's subject:** three of them (Respond-to-review, Respond-to-CI, Resolve-conflicts) commit and push to the PR's head branch, so two at once meant the second one's push could not fast-forward and its whole agent run was discarded, and a Review could start against a head a writing phase was about to replace. The suffixed ids stay exactly as they are — they are right for the checkouts they name — and the dispatch layer holds the *artifact* instead, under its own key (`repository` + `pr_number` on the dispatch row, `pr-in-flight`, "Durable dispatch state machine" below). **Serialising the shared pair is therefore the dispatch layer's job, not the id's** — it admits one phase to `task-<id>` at a time, and it orders the pair (issue #761, below): the in-flight guard (`processJob`, `src/worker/consumer.ts`) keys on `(taskId, phase)` to tell a repeated delivery from the next phase, and makes the next phase *wait* for the checkout (`task-in-flight`, "Durable dispatch state machine" above) instead of dropping it. It had keyed on `taskId` alone, which made the ordinary progression "Planning finished → the operator moves the card to ToDo" indistinguishable from a duplicate webhook and dropped both — issue #754's Implementation dispatch was discarded 71 seconds before Planning finished, leaving the card in ToDo with both workers idle until a transition was produced by hand hours later.

**Ordering the pair is one-directional** (issue #761, which closed the caveat #759 left). #759's guard admitted one phase at a time but chose nothing between two deliveries that had *both* not started, so an Implementation could claim first and run without the plan it was dispatched to consume. The window is queue time *plus* execution time: on issue #754 a Planning dispatch sat queued for 6.5 minutes with nothing running, and the board gives an operator no sign that execution has not begun. **Implementation now defers while a non-terminal Planning dispatch exists for the same task; Planning never defers to Implementation.** That asymmetry is the point — a total order over two phases cannot be derived from the dispatch table at claim time (PR #760 tried over three passes; each ordering exposed its mirror), so nothing derives one. A yielding Implementation lands back in `pending`, which the executing read does not count, so no pair of dispatches can defer to each other and no interleaving can be produced by fixing one direction. A task with **no** Planning dispatch — the ordinary Backlog → ToDo path, which is most tasks — finds no hold and starts immediately: the rule is "do not overtake a Planning that exists", never "Implementation requires a plan".

The signal is the dispatch record's own `(task_id, phase, state)` (`findActivePlanningDispatchForTask`), not the `planned` label. The label's write is deliberately best-effort — `markSplitChildPlanned` swallows a failed `addLabel` so a refused label cannot abort a split mid-loop (issue #737) — so gating a second thing on it would let one swallowed failure both un-gate Planning and mis-order Implementation; and "a plan exists" is the wrong question anyway, since it would block the ordinary unplanned path. The dispatch row is normalized SWARM state that no provider payload is read for (`ai/RULES.md` §2), and the wait reuses #759's vocabulary rather than a new mechanism: the same `task-in-flight` deferral, visible as a `pending` dispatch plus a `deferred` run row with its reason stated, woken by Planning settling, with the reconciler's republish underneath. **The one residual window** is a Planning dispatch that has never been claimed, whose `task_id`/`phase` are still `NULL` — a queue hop rather than a wait. Closing it would need either a provider-shaped payload path or a new normalized column, and matching unresolved rows by `phase IS NULL` instead would make two duplicate Implementation deliveries defer to each other.

## Worktree lifecycle

Unchanged from `PROJECT.md` §4 — this part of the original spec is SWARM-specific and already correct:

- Main repo at `~/swarm/{project-name}/`, worktrees under `~/swarm/{project-name}/.swarm-workspaces/task-<id>/`.
- `git worktree add` per task; config/caches and the `cascade` sibling-checkout pointer (`.env`, root and nested package dependencies such as `node_modules` / `dashboard/node_modules`, `cascade`, build caches) grafted in via symlinks with **absolute** targets — a relative link would dangle at the worktree's `.swarm-workspaces/<name>/` depth (see `ai/RULES.md` §1).
- **The base is read through `origin/<baseBranch>`, not the local branch of that name.** Provisioning fetches `origin` first so the task branch starts from the current base, but a fetch only advances `refs/remotes/origin/*` — it never moves the local `main`. Cutting from the bare branch name therefore started every task off whatever that host last *pulled*, which on a host nobody pulls on is silently many commits behind the real base and lands as merge conflicts the pipeline then spends a Resolve-conflicts phase on. `createFreshWorktree` resolves `refs/remotes/origin/<baseBranch>` and uses it when it exists, falling back to the literal value otherwise — the same preference `hasUnpushedWork` already applied. The fallback is load-bearing rather than defensive: `ProvisionOptions.baseBranch` is not always a branch (Review detaches at a head SHA), and `refs/remotes/origin/<sha>` never resolves. Fetch failures stay best-effort, so an offline host still provisions from whatever it has.
- `git worktree remove --force` on completion.
- **Provision-time collision reclamation (issue #367).** When a phase provisions `task-<id>` and a checkout is already there, it runs the shared reclaim gate (`src/worktree/reclaim.ts`) before doing anything destructive. The gate applies one ordered, fail-closed safety check — a live worktree lease, a resumable deferred/failed run or checkpointed continuation pinning the checkout, uncommitted changes, then unpushed local commits. Only a checkout that clears all four is *reclaimed*: the phase atomically holds the task lease across removing the stale checkout and re-provisioning a fresh one (releasing the lease on any failed provision), so two concurrent provisioners can't both remove it. A checkout protected by any check is left untouched and the run settles **terminally** as a `BlockedRecoveryError` — it is no longer treated as a transient `worktree-exists` deferral that retried against the same protected checkout forever — persisting `recovery.state = "blocked"` with the exact reason (`live-leased`, `resumable-owner`, `dirty`, `unpushed`) and posting an actionable message on the item/PR.
- **A lease is only honoured while it has a live owner (issue #427).** The lease key carries an opaque token, not an owner id, so a run that died — or completed — without releasing it left an orphan that wedged every later run for that task as `live-leased` until the 4h TTL expired, recoverable only by hand. The collision gate therefore checks *liveness in the database* before honouring a lease it could not claim (`src/worktree/lease-liveness.ts`): an owner is another `running` run (`hasLiveRunForTask` — the same query the reset path's `hasLiveOwner` resolver uses, so the two paths cannot drift), or another `leased`/`running` dispatch, for the same `(projectId, taskId)`. The asking attempt's own `runId` — threaded in through `ProvisionOptions.runId`, which `runAssignedPhase` passes to **every** phase and each phase forwards to `provision` — is excluded, since its run row is already `running` by the time it provisions, and a retry of a crashed run reuses that id so its own leftover lease reads as stale. With an owner, the run blocks as `live-leased` exactly as before. With none, the orphan lease is adopted by compare-and-set against the token actually observed (`takeOverWorktreeLease` — a provisioner that legitimately claimed it in between still wins) and the checkout behind it goes through the *same* ordered reclaim gate, so a dirty, unpushed, or resumable-pinned checkout is still retained and never force-removed. The token is read *before* liveness is evaluated, so a run that becomes the genuine owner after that check invalidates the compare-and-set instead of losing its checkout. Every uncertain outcome — no `runId`, a database or Redis error, a lost compare-and-set — reports "live" and blocks, so the fix only ever removes protection where the owner is provably gone. Liveness is deliberately not extended to `worker_sessions` heartbeats: a worker still writing after its run and dispatch have both been reaped as timed out has no database owner, and that window is accepted rather than closed because the reclaim gate behind this one still retains any dirty or unpushed checkout — only a provably clean, fully pushed one can be reclaimed there.
- **The DB-free runtime coordinates only its own host (issue #535).** `GitWorktreeManager` receives a `WorktreeRuntime`: the store-backed default keeps the Redis lease + Postgres liveness/pin/cancellation implementation above unchanged, while `runAssignmentDbFree` injects `host-local-runtime.ts`. The **retention sweep picks the same one** (`retentionWorktreeRuntime`, `src/worktree/retention.ts`, issue #551), keyed on the dispatch mode, so it reads the markers this host's worker actually writes rather than a store nothing sets. Its atomic lock directories and takeover guards serialize provisioners on that filesystem, its owner record checks this daemon's in-flight dispatches (or a live peer PID), and a preservation pin outside the checkout retains a resumable deferred/failed run without making the worktree dirty. The same reclaim gate still checks lease → pin → dirty → unpushed in that order. A different host cannot see or block on these files, and this path never creates an `ioredis` client. **Every artifact it writes carries an expiry**, because the Redis lease it replaces had a 4h TTL and that TTL was load-bearing (issue #427): a lock, a takeover guard, or a preservation pin stranded by a crash is reaped by the next provisioner rather than wedging the task until an operator deletes a file by hand. **An expiry only the next provisioner evaluates is not enough on its own** (issue #721): a task never dispatched again keeps its lapsed artifacts forever, and `preserve()`'s `<pin>.<uuid>.tmp` staging file — strandable by a crash between its `writeFileSync` and its `renameSync` — has no next reader at all, so the retention sweep applies the same TTLs proactively (`sweepStaleHostLocalState`, above), TTL-only because the sweeping process is not the daemon and can trust neither an in-flight set it cannot see nor a pid it did not write. Liveness (this daemon's in-flight set, or a peer PID) still answers sooner and more precisely than the clock, but **the recorded timestamp is checked before either liveness answer** (issue #717), so no liveness path is unbounded; it remains the backstop for what liveness cannot see — a recycled PID, a crash between two syscalls, a truncated write, an in-flight entry the daemon never cleaned up. That ordering is the fix for a recurrence of #427's failure class on the lease that replaced the Redis one: the same-process branch used to return first, and since the recorded PID is the *daemon's* — it outlives every phase it dispatches — virtually every lease on a worker host takes that branch, so one leaked in-flight entry reported "live" with no bound at all (task `702-conflicts`, wedged 8h42m, cleared only by restarting its daemon, because no control-plane action reaches a `.swarm-state` file on a worker host). The standing rule that follows is that a branch here may answer *sooner* than the expiry but never be *exempt* from it. The ordering costs nothing real: a configured per-phase timeout caps at 45 minutes (`AgentConfigSchema`), far inside the 4h lease TTL, and the Redis lease was never heartbeated either — it lapsed under a live phase too. Expiry never force-removes work: the reclaim gate behind it still refuses a dirty or unpushed checkout. `repoRoot` follows the same ownership rule: it is omitted from `TaskAssignment` and resolved from `SWARM_WORKER_REPO_ROOT` (default cwd) on the executor — and because that one path serves every project the worker is enrolled in, provisioning first asserts the checkout's `origin` actually resolves to the assigned `project.repo`, so a daemon launched in the wrong directory fails loudly instead of cutting task branches in an unrelated repository. That one path can also be handed to *two* daemons, which is why the daemon locks it for its whole life before handshaking (`src/worktree/checkout-lock.ts`, issue #689): same atomic-`mkdir` claim, same shared liveness primitives (`src/worktree/local-lock.ts`), but its own short, refreshed TTL — a process that lives for days cannot be bounded by a TTL sized for one agent run.
- **A checkout provisioning cannot have exactly as asked is adapted, not failed (issue #558).** Git enforces one checkout per branch and refuses `-b` for a branch that exists, and both rules turned ordinary leftovers into a phase that could not start at all. `createFreshWorktree` therefore resolves *how* to check out from what the repository actually holds: a task branch that survived `reapOrphanedBranch` (kept because `origin` has it, so a PR may depend on it) is **checked out** rather than cut again — the state a hand-removed preserved checkout leaves, which used to fail the next attempt with `a branch named '<x>' already exists` — and a branch another worktree already holds is checked out **detached** at `origin/<branch>`, so a *preserved* checkout kept for its own retry stops making every other phase that needs that branch unrunnable (`'<branch>' is already used by worktree at …`, which is how a Respond-to-review for an open PR failed on sight). Detaching costs the delivery nothing: commits are made on the detached HEAD and pushed as `<sha>:refs/heads/<branch>`, so the phase runs and pushes exactly as it would have, and the handle still names the branch it targets. **Issue #829 generalised that same authority rule to the plain existing-local-branch case**, which had no check at all: provisioning an existing branch (`createBranch: false` — Review, Respond-to-review, Respond-to-CI, Resolve-conflicts, an Implementation retry) whose local `refs/heads/<branch>` does not *contain* `origin/<branch>` is checked out detached at the remote tip too, so the remote is the authority for a branch a PR is open on in both sub-cases rather than one. It cost a Respond-to-review run: a never-pushed local `issue-825` sibling of the PR's real head was checked out as-is, the agent's own mandated `git pull --ff-only` correctly refused, and the phase died reporting a missing hand-off. "Contains" rather than "equals" is the deliberate carve-out — a local branch merely *ahead* of origin is not diverged, pushes as a fast-forward, and holds real unpushed work, so it is left alone; the probe asks the same `merge-base --is-ancestor` question `divergedRemoteHead` asks before classifying a rejected push, so the two cannot disagree about what "diverged" means. The lookups are skipped where they cannot apply (a create-path branch that survived no reap can be held by nothing), so an ordinary first provision runs the same git commands it always did.
- **A reset's discard intent travels to the worktree, not the other way round (issue #592).** "Reset & restart" (`resetRun`, `src/dispatch/run-reset.ts`) runs in the API server, so the `reconcileTerminatedWorktree` it performs settles only the control-plane host's filesystem — right for a checkout that host's own worker holds, a no-op otherwise. In a federated deployment the checkout is usually somewhere else, where that teardown reported `absent` and the replacement dispatch then failed on the exact `Worktree collision for task '<id>' … uncommitted changes` the reset was issued to clear. A reset therefore also puts `recoveryMode: 'discard'` on the replacement dispatch's payload — the fourth `RecoveryMode` (`src/queue/jobs.ts`), and the only one permitted to destroy protected work — which rides `TaskAssignment` to whichever worker holds the checkout and is honoured by `executeRecoveryGate` there: remove it unconditionally, then provision fresh. It is **one-shot**, dropped by `deriveRetryJobPayload` like `'checkpoint'`, so a later automatic deferral of the same run cannot destroy a checkout that deferral had just preserved for a resume. Since issue #744 *every* reset carries it: the provision-time reclaim gate's dirty/unpushed/pinned protections still govern every other path, but a reset is the deliberate "this checkout is worthless" action and no longer has a variant that leaves them in charge. No teardown request/result frame pair was added — `resetRun` has no request channel to the router (only Redis pub/sub and BullMQ), and worker affinity already routes the restart to the host holding the checkout.
- Stale `task-<id>` worktrees left behind by interrupted runs are reclaimed by a retention sweep running periodically in the background of the **API server** — the control-plane host process that owns it (see "Process responsibilities" above), since the worker is DB-free and executes phases only — or manually on-demand via `swarm worktrees prune`. The sweep preserves up to a configurable number of most-recently-active worktrees (defined by `worktreeRetention.maxWorktrees` in the project config, defaulting to 10), and runs the *same* reclaim gate on each prune candidate so it never removes a worktree that is leased (in-flight), pinned by a resumable deferred/failed run, has uncommitted changes (`git status`), or carries unpushed local commits.
- **The age-based abandoned sweep is the second entry point, asked for per machine (issue #951).** The retention sweep above is a *rank* — it never expresses "untouched for a long time" as an age, and its reclaim gate retains a dirty or unpushed checkout forever, which is correct minutes after a phase stops and wrong at ten days. `sweepAbandonedWorktrees` (`src/worktree/abandoned.ts`) is the second entry point: it removes every `task-<id>` checkout directly under one machine's `worktreeRoot` untouched for `worktreeRetention.abandonedAfterDays` (default 10) **including** the dirty and unpushed ones the retention sweep refuses, *recording* that state on each removal — logged at `warn` with the path, the qualifying age, and the work destroyed — rather than being stopped by it.
