"Untouched" is the newest mtime among the worktree directory and the checkout's own git metadata (`.git`, and `index`/`HEAD`/`logs/HEAD` in the per-worktree git dir), because a directory's mtime moves only on a *top-level* change and would otherwise call a checkout holding real nested work stale; the accepted consequence is that a spurious bump (a symlink graft, a build-cache write) costs a delayed delete and never a lost one, and a candidate nothing can be stat'ed for fails closed as `ignored`. **Liveness is never overridden by age, and it is *held* rather than read**: a leased or resumable-pinned checkout is exempt whatever its age, decided by `GitWorktreeManager.claimForSweep` over `evaluateWorktreeLiveness` — the first two ordered checks of the reclaim gate, extracted so the two sweeps cannot disagree about what "live" means. What the claim adds is the half an age-based sweep cannot do without: it *takes* the task lease, through the same compare-and-set gate a provision takes it through (orphan takeover included, or a ten-day-old checkout's lapsed lease would block the sweep forever), and the force-removal happens under it — `removeCheckoutUnderLease` rather than `cleanup`, which releases the lease first. A read alone is true only at the instant it is taken, and a provisioner arriving in the gap would have had its fresh checkout deleted out from under a running phase. Both sweeps resolve their candidates through the same `matchTaskWorktrees` (`src/worktree/task-worktrees.ts`) and force-remove through the same idempotent path, so neither can fight the other over a checkout. That matcher admits only a **direct** `task-<id>` child of the root: a sweep addresses a checkout by task id alone, and the removal force-removes the path `GitWorktreeManager.worktreePath()` maps that id back to — so a nested namesake (`…/archive/task-123`) qualifying on age would have been "removed" by destroying the direct `task-123` checkout instead, whatever its own age or unpushed work. `.swarm-state` stays `pruneStaleWorktrees`' alone; every artifact there is TTL-bounded at 4h/24h, so none survives to ten days, and removing one would only take the liveness answer away from the gate. **The control plane asks one machine for it** (issue #955, the section "The abandoned-worktree sweep signal" below) **and asks the whole installation for it once a week on its own clock** (issue #956, "The weekly fleet schedule" below). That completes the shape: the **host keeps the schedule and records what came back**, and **each machine inspects and deletes its own checkouts** — which is the only division that works, since `worktreeRoot` is resolved against a host-local `SWARM_WORKER_REPO_ROOT` the control plane never sees and liveness is answered by the daemon's own in-flight set.

### The abandoned-worktree sweep signal (issue #955)

Phase 2 gave `sweepAbandonedWorktrees` the same signal path issue #933 gave `applyUpdateTarget`, and it is a deliberate copy of it — every piece below names the bridge it was copied from, and the deviations are what the two mechanisms genuinely differ about.

**Asking.** `workers.requestWorktreeSweep({ workerId })` (`src/api/routers/workers.ts`) is **strictly owner-only**, like `requestUpdate`: removing directories from somebody's hardware is the machine operator's call, so an `instanceAdmin` who does not own it gets the same `NOT_FOUND` a stranger does. It carries **no draining precondition**, which is the deviation that matters. An update replaces the code under a running daemon and therefore requires a drained machine; a sweep disturbs no in-flight run, because a checkout a phase still holds reads as *leased* and is skipped by construction. Requiring a drain would also make phase 3's unattended weekly sweep impossible, so `requestWorktreeSweep` (`src/db/repositories/workersRepository.ts`) is `requestWorkerUpdate` **minus** the `draining_since IS NOT NULL` predicate, and there is no `in-pool` disposition to word. There is no host-level opt-in either: `SWARM_WORKER_SELF_UPDATE` exists because a self-update rewrites the operator's own install root, while a sweep removes only `task-<id>` checkouts under a project's own `worktreeRoot` behind the liveness gate above, so `worktreeRetention.abandonedAfterDays` is the whole of the opt-out. `swarm workers sweep-worktrees <worker-id>` drives it.

**Recording and pushing.** The request lands on the `workers` row in five columns splitting the same way the `update_*` block does — the request outstanding (`worktree_sweep_request_id`, `worktree_sweep_requested_at`) and the last outcome reported (`worktree_sweep_status`, `worktree_sweep_reported_at`, `worktree_sweep_result`) — read back as one `Worker.worktreeSweep`. **Only the most recent sweep per machine is retained**: with the weekly cadence of issue #956 that is precisely "last week's", which is the question #951 asks the record to answer, so it is not a history table and says so in its own column comment. Retained against the *next question*, not only against the next machine: asking overwrites the request pair alone and leaves the reported outcome standing until a new report replaces it (issue #956), so `worktree_sweep_reported_at` may predate `worktree_sweep_requested_at` — and comparing the two is how a reader tells "this outcome answers the outstanding request" from "this outcome answers an earlier one". Clearing the outcome at ask time instead would make the weekly fan-out blank every machine that had not answered the latest ask, which is the ordinary overnight state of a laptop and the permanent state of a retired one. The mutation publishes the worker id on `swarm:worktree-sweep` (`src/queue/worker-sweeps.ts`) and `src/router/worktree-sweep-dispatch.ts` turns that into the `worktree-sweep` frame, because only the router holds worker sockets. The frame is built **from the row plus the machine's approved enrollments** — `listEnrollmentsForWorker` → `findProjectByIdFromDb` per project → `{ projectId, worktreeRoot, abandonedAfterDays }` — never from the notification's payload, so a push days later asks about the projects the machine is enrolled in *now*. Approval (`status === 'active'`) is the gate rather than `isRoutable`: sharing consent governs whether a project may be given *work* here, and a sweep gives it none — a machine whose owner stopped offering it is exactly where abandoned checkouts pile up. A machine with no approved enrollment is logged and left alone rather than pushed an empty frame. `resendPendingWorktreeSweepToWorker`, hooked into `handleWorkerStreamOpen` beside `resendPendingWorkerUpdateToWorker`, states the request again on the next connection — which matters more here than for an update, since a sweep can be asked of a machine that simply happens to be offline.

**The frame.** `WorktreeSweepSchema` (`src/transport/protocol.ts`) carries a request id and, per project, an id, the project's **relative** `worktreeRoot` and an age in days — and nothing that could name a directory on its own: the machine resolves that root against its own `SWARM_WORKER_REPO_ROOT`, which is host-local and never travels. Additive, so `TRANSPORT_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is deliberately **not** bumped, on `TaskCancelSchema`'s reasoning.

**Acting.** `src/transport/worktree-sweep.ts` sweeps each named project serially, each in its own `try`, against a `GitWorktreeManager` over `createHostLocalWorktreeRuntime` whose `isOwnerLive` is the daemon's **own in-flight set** — which is what makes a checkout a phase here currently holds read as leased and be skipped, and is the whole of this feature's safety. That set carries the answer in both directions: a running sweep **adds its own owner id** to it, because the lease it holds across a removal is written with this daemon's pid and a provisioner in this same process resolves such a lease through `isOwnerLive` — unregistered, it would read as an orphan and be taken over mid-removal (cross-process, the pid check already answers). The daemon's busy flag is republished on both edges of that entry, so a machine reads busy while it sweeps, which is the right answer for what that flag guards. A project that throws is counted into `failedCount` and its text folded into the report's `message`, never aborting the rest. It keeps `createWorkerUpdateHandler`'s bookkeeping, because the same reconnect re-push exists: a `requestId` already handled is never re-swept (re-sweeping would mean re-removing) but a report that never landed **is re-sent** (the `takeUndelivered` move), at most one sweep runs at a time with a second request held rather than dropped, and a shutdown abandons the attempt silently and forgets it, so the re-push sweeps from scratch. It **never exits the process** — unlike an applied update, nothing about the running daemon changed.

**Reporting.** `POST /worker/delivery/worktree-sweep-report` (`src/router/worker-delivery.ts`), a route rather than a stream frame for the reason the update and quota reports both state. The row written is the one the *credential* resolved to; the body names no worker. The report carries the paths removed, each with its qualifying age and the uncommitted/unpushed work the removal destroyed — recorded because an age-based sweep destroys work by design and that must not be readable only in one machine's log — plus `removedCount` as the true total beside a capped `removed` list. A report whose id the row has moved on from answers `recorded: false`, which is a success. No rollout is advanced: a sweep has no bad-build risk to stage against, so none of `src/api/worker-update-rollout.ts` is involved.

### The weekly fleet schedule (issue #956)

Phase 3 puts the **host's clock** in front of phase 2's per-machine request, so the whole installation sweeps itself with no operator action, and adds the readout that answers "what did the fleet delete last week?" from one machine. It builds nothing new below the request: every machine asked here gets the same row write, the same publish, the same frame and the same daemon handler phase 2 built.

**The schedule.** `startHostMaintenance` (`src/api/maintenance.ts`) gains `runFleetWorktreeSweepIfDue` as a second, independent chore beside the hourly `maxWorktrees` retention sweep, which is untouched. **The setting is a *due* interval, not a timer period**: the timer ticks hourly and cheaply, and `SWARM_WORKTREE_ABANDONED_SWEEP_INTERVAL_MS` (default `604800000`, 7d, a positive integer or the API server's startup throws) is compared against a durable marker. That is what makes the cadence hold across restarts — an API server restarted daily must not fan out daily — and what makes "the following week" true for a machine that was offline: the tick is cheap, the marker decides. The whole body is in a `try`, like every other chore there, so a failure never stops the API server, and the marker records that the fleet **was asked**: it is written *after* the fan-out, so one that threw outright is retried on the next tick rather than skipped for a week, and it is skipped entirely (with a `warn`) when a non-empty fleet produced no entries at all — the fan-out swallows each machine's failure by design, so a database hiccup that hit every per-machine write would otherwise advance the marker a full interval on the strength of a failure. A genuinely empty fleet is not that case and does advance it.

**The marker** is `maintenance.lastFleetWorktreeSweepAt` in the `app_settings` blob (`src/config/app-settings.ts`), whose header already states the blob is extensible so future keys drop in without a migration. It is the first key there that is **not** a setting — machine-written state — so it is deliberately absent from the settings tRPC router's vocabulary and from the dashboard; it rides `settings.update`'s whole-blob write untouched because both dashboard writers merge onto the settings they loaded. An absent marker means due, which is what makes a fresh installation (and an existing one the first time this ships) sweep once and then settle into the cadence.

**The fan-out.** `fanOutWorktreeSweep` (`src/api/worktree-sweep-fanout.ts`) is modelled on `src/api/worker-update-fanout.ts` and carries over its two load-bearing properties verbatim: **one machine's state never refuses the whole call** — every machine gets a disposition and the call succeeds, which matters more here since the caller is a timer nobody is watching — and **input order is preserved**. The dispositions reduce to three, because this request has neither of the two things an update's five turn on: `requested` (recorded, live session, the push is on its way), `queued-offline` (recorded, no live session, re-stated on the machine's next connection), and `already-asked` (an unanswered request is outstanding and is left exactly as it is, request id and all — re-asking would mint a new id for a sweep the machine may be running right now). One `randomUUID()` per machine, never one shared across the fan-out, since `recordWorktreeSweepReport`'s `(worker_id, worktree_sweep_request_id)` match depends on it; sequential rather than `Promise.all`, for the predictable publish order the update fan-out gives its reason for. A machine deregistered mid-fan-out, or one whose write throws, is logged and left out of the report rather than given a fourth disposition — neither reached a durable state one of the three describes, and leaving it out is what keeps one machine's failure from costing the rest of the fleet its sweep. It takes an already-resolved worker list, so authorization stays the caller's; here the caller is the maintenance loop, which passes `listAllWorkers()`.

**No waves, staging or halting.** Issue #951 says outright that `src/api/worker-update-rollout.ts` is more than this needs, and it is right: a sweep has no bad-build risk to stage against and takes no machine out of the dispatch pool, so there is nothing for a wave to bound. There is no per-project or per-machine cadence override either — one installation-wide interval.

**The readout.** `workers.listSweeps` (`src/api/routers/workers.ts`) answers every machine's last recorded sweep and any outstanding request, and `swarm workers sweeps` prints one block per machine with each removed path, its age, and whether it held uncommitted or unpushed work; a machine that has never reported prints "never swept". It exists because `sweep-worktrees` reads a machine's sweep only by asking that machine for another one, which is not something to do to a fleet nobody asked to sweep: once sweeps are requested by a schedule rather than by the operator reading them, there has to be a way to read one without asking for one. `never swept` means the machine has never reported a sweep and nothing else — an unanswered ask leaves the record standing, so the last sweep and the outstanding request print together. Installation-wide, so an instance administrator's on issue #647's terms, and refused outright rather than narrowed to the caller's own machines. It is kept off the roster rows deliberately: one report carries up to 200 removed paths. No dashboard surface.

## Host-local install updates (issue #920)

`src/worker/self-update.ts` is the mechanism that moves **one machine's SWARM install root** to a requested build, or leaves it exactly as it was and says why. Since issue #933 it is no longer a leaf — the whole signal path around it is below — and since issue #934 it also owns the record an applied build is *judged* on; since issue #935 it also decides whether the update is safe for the **machine**, which is the one "when" that belongs to the install root rather than to a daemon. What it still never decides is when *this daemon* may act. There is no dashboard control either; the CLI is the operator surface. The running process is unaffected whatever this module does, because its modules were loaded at startup and it never exits.

What it operates on is the **install root**, `swarmInstallRoot()` (`src/lib/build-identity.ts`) — never `process.cwd()` and never `SWARM_WORKER_REPO_ROOT`. That distinction is the same one build identity already turns on: on the control-plane host several daemons serve several *project* repositories out of one npm-linked SWARM checkout, so `cwd` names a project and the env var names a project's checkout, and only the module's own `import.meta.url` names the install being updated.

`applyUpdateTarget({ target })` returns a structured `UpdateOutcome` — `already-current` / `applied` / `refused` / `failed` — and **never throws**, on the same contract `resolveBuildIdentity` keeps next door. Every step before the apply is a guard rather than a feature, because this is a code-distribution channel:

- The target is **data with a grammar**, `WorkerUpdateTargetSchema` — declared beside `WorkerBuildSchema` in `src/lib/build-identity.ts` rather than in the worker, so the frame that later carries it imports the definition instead of re-declaring it, exactly as `HandshakeRequestSchema.build` does for the build a daemon *is* on. It admits a branch, a tag, or a commit id and nothing else: a ref starts with an alphanumeric (so it can never be read as a git option), and the character class admits no colon, whitespace, scheme, `~`, `^`, `@`, `?`, `*`, `\`, or quote — which excludes a URL, a refspec, a revision expression and a shell fragment *by construction* rather than by a reviewer noticing. It is validated before a single subprocess runs.
- The fetch is `git fetch <remote>` with **no refspec and no URL**, so the only code that can arrive is whatever the install root's own already-configured remote already fetches. Nothing a request carries can redirect it.
- The target must be **reachable from the branch the install tracks** (`merge-base --is-ancestor` against `refs/remotes/<remote>/<branch>`). That is the authorization that matters: a machine can only be moved to code already on the branch it follows, never onto a side branch somebody pushed.
- A **dirty** install root is refused before anything is fetched — it is an operator's working copy, and an update must never overwrite one — and a target already checked out short-circuits as `already-current` with nothing installed and nothing built.

The apply is `git checkout --detach <commit>` → `npm ci` → `npm run build`, every command an `execFile` argv array with a bounded timeout, `cwd` at the install root, and its output captured as a bounded tail that rides its own field rather than being folded into the operator-facing `reason`. A failed install or build returns the install root to the commit it was on and rebuilds it there; a rollback that itself fails reports `rolledBack: false`, the one outcome an operator must be told about. `npm run build:dashboard` is deliberately **not** run: the dashboard bundle is served by the API server on the control-plane host, and a worker machine has no reason to build it.

State lives at `~/.swarm/install-updates/<sha256(realpath(installRoot))>/state.json` through `checkoutStateDir` (`src/worktree/checkout-key.ts`, derived once in `src/worktree/install-lock.ts` and re-exported by the worker so the state file, the update lock and the participant records cannot key the same install root differently) — the same per-checkout convention the checkout lock and the worker credential cache use, reused rather than re-derived, and outside every checkout for the reason stated there. It records the tracked branch and its remote, because an applied update leaves HEAD **detached** so git has no upstream to answer with on the next run, and the last known good commit, written *before* the checkout so a process killed mid-apply still leaves a readable record of what to go back to. A detached HEAD with nothing recorded is refused naming the remedy, rather than guessing a branch; an unparseable state file is refused rather than silently losing the commit to fall back to. While HEAD is attached those two halves are asked of git **by field** — `git for-each-ref --format='%(upstream:remotename)%0a%(upstream:remoteref)'` on the branch `symbolic-ref` names — never split out of the abbreviated `<remote>/<branch>` form, because a remote name may itself contain a slash (`team/origin` is a remote `git remote add` accepts) and splitting at the first one read it as remote `team` tracking `origin/main`, then fetched a remote that does not exist. A branch whose upstream is a local sibling (`remotename` `.`) has no remote to fetch and is treated as no upstream at all, falling back to the recorded pair. Since issue #934 it also records the **pending verification** — the applied build, the one it replaced, and how many starts in a row have failed since — and `lastKnownGood` consequently stays on the build that was running until a daemon on the new one has handshaked. The field is defaulted rather than required, so a state file written before that issue parses as "nothing to verify" instead of being refused as unreadable, which would block updates on every machine that has ever had one.

Nothing here decides when *this daemon* may act — the signal path below does. What it does decide, since issue #935, is whether the **machine** may be moved at all: the lock and the busy-peer refusal described under "Several daemons on one install root".

### The update signal and the restart it triggers (issue #933)

The "when" is one lifecycle across three processes, and every piece of it copies a bridge that already exists.

**Asking.** `workers.requestUpdate({ workerId, target })` (`src/api/routers/workers.ts`) is **strictly owner-only**, exactly like `setDraining` — an `instanceAdmin` who does not own the machine gets the same `NOT_FOUND` a stranger does — which matters more here than anywhere else in that router, since this is the one mutation whose effect is to run *different code* on somebody's hardware. It **refuses unless the machine is already draining** (issue #919), naming `swarm workers drain <id>` as the remedy: the daemon waits for its in-flight phases to finish before applying anything, and only draining stops new work arriving into that wait. **That precondition is a predicate of the durable write, not a read the handler makes first** (issue #921): `requestWorkerUpdate` (`src/db/repositories/workersRepository.ts`) carries `draining_since IS NOT NULL` in its own `WHERE` and answers a four-way `WorkerUpdateRequestOutcome` — `requested` / `in-pool` / `no-project` (issue #971) / `not-found` — so the eligibility test and the write are one statement and a concurrent `swarm workers undrain` cannot land between them. The API layer words the refusal (a `CONFLICT` here, an `in-pool` disposition for the fleet form below); it does not decide it, which is what keeps one race-safe copy of the policy rather than one per caller. The target is validated with the shared `WorkerUpdateTargetSchema` at the API, at the service seam, and again on the machine — it ends up handed to `git` on an unattended host, so it is checked at every seam it crosses rather than once at the edge. `swarm workers update <worker-id> <ref>` drives it.

**Recording and pushing.** The request lands on the `workers` row in seven columns that split cleanly in two — the request outstanding (`update_request_id`, `update_target`, `update_requested_at`, and `update_requested_by_user_id` since issue #922) and the last outcome reported (`update_status`, `update_message`, `update_reported_at`) — read back as one `Worker.update` value.

**Since issue #972 the delivery is a durable queued unit, not a pub/sub announcement.** `requestWorkerUpdate`'s one transaction now writes three things — the row, the `runs` row that records the update (issue #971), and a `worker-update` dispatch — for the same reason #971 gave for the run: a recorded request with no dispatch is a request nothing will ever deliver. The `swarm:worker-update` Redis channel and `src/queue/worker-updates.ts` are **gone**; the mutation publishes an ordinary dispatch wake-up instead, after the durable write and swallowing its own failures, because `reconcileDispatchesPeriodically` re-publishes anything a crash window loses. The queue's only consumer is the router (`startControlPlaneDispatch`), which is also the process holding worker sockets, so the unit that must push the frame is dequeued exactly where the socket is.

Three properties follow, and each answers something the announcement could not. It is **durable**: a control-plane restart between the request and the machine acting on it loses nothing. It **outranks waiting work at both layers it passes through**: `priorityFor` (`src/queue/producer.ts`) returns `WORKER_UPDATE_JOB_PRIORITY = -10` for it, every claim-ordering read sorts `priority ASC` over a column that is a plain `integer` with no lower bound, and the BullMQ wake-up that carries it is added `lifo` so it is the next job the control-plane consumer claims rather than the last (below) — so a machine asked to update while its project's queue is full is served first rather than last, expressed in the queue's own terms rather than by a second scheduler. And it leaves a **record** when it cannot be dispatched: an offline machine's dispatch sits `retry-scheduled` on the `worker-eligibility` wait reason with a sentence saying so, in the same place every other undispatchable unit's is. Nothing is preempted to make room — the executor (`src/worker/worker-update-dispatch.ts`) resolves no trigger, claims no task, takes no project slot and touches no run but its own.

**The negative priority reaches BullMQ as `lifo`, not as a priority.** A BullMQ worker `RPOPLPUSH`es from the plain `wait` list first and only falls through to the prioritized ZSET when `wait` is empty; a job added with *any* priority — a negative one included — goes to that ZSET, so passing `-10` would rank the wake-up *behind* every unset-priority wake-up. Passing nothing is not enough either: an option-less add `LPUSH`es onto `wait`, the end `RPOPLPUSH` does not take from, so the wake-up would still queue behind every default job already waiting — the ordinary case whenever the control-plane consumer's slots are busy. `lifo: true` is the remaining half and the only tier BullMQ offers *above* the default: `addStandardJob` `RPUSH`es instead, so the wake-up is claimed next. The producer therefore passes `priority` only when the rank is a demotion and `lifo` when it is a promotion (`bullMqOrderingOptions`), and the ordering that decides which unit a worker claims still lives in the dispatch table, where the negative is honoured directly. One bound on that: BullMQ's `promoteDelayedJobs` `LPUSH`es everything it moves out of the delayed set whatever its `lifo`, so a *delayed* wake-up loses the guarantee — which costs this nothing, since an update's only delayed wake-up is the executor's timed backstop for an offline machine and the reconnect that ends that wait republishes it undelayed.

The frame itself is still built **from the row**, never from the dispatch's payload, so a wake-up arriving late, twice, or after a re-target cannot push a build the row has moved off; what the dispatch contributes is the `requestId` it was created for, matched against the row's own (`pushPendingWorkerUpdate` → `pushed` / `superseded` / `not-connected`, settling the dispatch as `worker-update-pushed`, `superseded`, or a scheduled retry). A re-target supersedes the previous request's dispatch beside its run, in the same transaction. A machine with no socket here leaves the request waiting; `resendPendingWorkerUpdateToWorker`, hooked into `handleWorkerStreamOpen` beside `resendRunCancellations`, now **wakes that dispatch** rather than pushing directly, so there is one delivery path rather than two. For this feature that reconnect is the *ordinary* path rather than an edge, since an operator typically asks while the machine is drained and idle.

**The upgrade across that change is served by the reconnect too, not by a backfill.** A request recorded by a control plane older than #972 lives on the row alone, and the hook that used to push it directly now looks for a dispatch it does not have — so `promoteWorkerUpdateDispatchForWorker`, finding nothing to wake, calls `adoptOutstandingWorkerUpdateRequest` (`src/db/repositories/workersRepository.ts`), which writes that request's missing dispatch — linked to the `runs` row it already has, or a new one if it predates #971 too — and publishes its wake-up on the same outbox order as a fresh request. It is **idempotent on the dispatch's dedup key, the request id**: a request that already has a dispatch in any state writes nothing and answers `undefined`, which is every request recorded since the issue, so the ordinary path ends at the first read. A deployment-time backfill was the alternative and lost: it would have to be sequenced against the rollout by hand, and still could not repair a request the old build recorded while the rollout was in progress.

**The frame.** `WorkerUpdateSchema` (`src/transport/protocol.ts`) carries a target ref and a request id and nothing else — no command, script, URL, or path. Additive, so `TRANSPORT_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is deliberately **not** bumped: an unrecognised cloud→worker frame is a logged no-op on an older daemon (`parseControlFrame`), where a bump would reject every frame from every already-deployed worker.

**Acting.** `src/transport/worker-update.ts` is the daemon's decision layer: act only if this host opted in (`SWARM_WORKER_SELF_UPDATE=true`, reported `declined` otherwise), re-validate the target, wait until the in-flight assignment set is empty — **no run is cancelled, deferred or failed by an update** — then call `applyUpdateTarget`. It waits for *its own* runs only, and what the mechanism does about a peer daemon sharing the install root depends on what that peer is doing (see "Several daemons on one install root"): one **mid-phase** is refused rather than waited for, because nothing here can drain another machine role and a wait on one would not terminate; one **updating** *is* waited for, because its finish is exactly what makes this daemon's own restart safe (issue #973). A shutdown abandons the attempt silently, because the request is durable and the next connection is pushed it again. At most **one** update runs at a time — two `applyUpdateTarget` calls on one install root would interleave a checkout with a build — so a re-target arriving mid-apply is **held**, not dropped, and taken up as soon as the one in flight ends without restarting the daemon; dropping it would strand it, since a machine that stays up after a `failed` or `refused` gets no further push (the notification fired once, and the only other re-push is on a new connection). Only the newest held request survives, and when the update in flight *does* apply it is left for the restarted daemon's reconnect re-push instead. On `applied` — and, since issue #973, on `adopted` — the daemon reports, releases its session and exits 0 through the same graceful teardown a SIGTERM takes (session released, quota reporting stopped, checkout lock dropped), so launchd `KeepAlive` / systemd `Restart=always` restarts it into the new build — whose identity then reaches the control plane on the next handshake through the existing `build` field (issue #918), with no extra wiring. The restart is decided from the *reported* status rather than from the mechanism's outcome, which is the whole of the difference: a report the control plane rejected — an older router that has never heard `adopted`, the skew the "control plane first, workers after" rollout order already covers — still restarts the daemon rather than stranding it on stale code, and the run it leaves behind is settled by the control plane's own stale sweep. Every other outcome is reported and the machine **keeps taking work** on the build it has.

**Reporting.** `POST /worker/delivery/update-report` (`src/router/worker-delivery.ts`), a route rather than a stream frame, for the reason the CLI-quota frame already states: `handleWorkerStreamFrame` closes the socket with `WS_CLOSE.MALFORMED_FRAME` on a frame it cannot parse, so a newer worker against an older router would reconnect-loop, where an unserved route is a 404 the daemon survives. That matters more here than for quota, because **this feature's rollout is control plane first, workers after** — a daemon freshly restarted into a newer build talking to an older router is the expected skew. The row written is the one the *credential* resolved to; the frame carries no worker id to honour or ignore. The outcome is recorded whether or not it closes the pending request: a report whose id the row has moved on from answers `recorded: false`, so a superseded request's answer never un-pends the one an operator has since made. `update_target` is not cleared by a report, since an outcome naming no build answers nothing.

**An update is a run (issue #971).** Moving a machine to a new build is a unit of work executed by a worker, so it is recorded as one: a `runs` row of `kind = 'worker-update'`, created by the same write that records the request and settled by the same write that records the report — each end is one transaction, because a recorded request with no run is invisible and a run with no request is a row nothing will ever settle. The row names the machine (`worker_id` + `worker_user_id`, which `runs.list` already resolves into `workerName` and `runs.getById` into `attribution`, **plus `maintenance_machine`** — the machine's display name as it stood when it was asked) and the build (`maintenance_target`, denormalized because `workers.update_target` is overwritten by the next request); its `phase` is `worker-update`, its `maintenance_request_id` is the key the report settles it by, and a partial unique index keeps that id naming at most one run. Both denormalizations exist for the same class of reason — the live source is overwritten or removed while the run is a record of what already happened: `workers.update_target` by the next request, and `worker_id` by the machine being retired (`ON DELETE SET NULL`), which is precisely when "what did that machine last do?" is asked, so `runs.list` and `runs.getById` fall back to `maintenance_machine` for the name and the live worker row still wins where it resolves. A re-target settles the previous request's run `failed` as superseded, since its own report will answer `recorded: false` and never close it. A machine that never answers is settled by the ordinary stale sweep after `WORKER_UPDATE_RUN_TIMEOUT_MS` (6h, written into the row's own `timeout_ms`), and a machine that answers later *corrects* the row — the report is authoritative for the request it names, so `settleWorkerUpdateRun` is deliberately unguarded on the run's current status. `applied`/`adopted`/`already-current` complete it; `declined`/`refused`/`failed` fail it carrying the machine's own prose as `error`, which is where the run detail page already shows a failure. `adopted` completes it for the same reason `applied` does: the machine that run names ends up on the build it was asked for, and which daemon on it paid for the fetch is a distinction the *status* carries rather than a difference in whether the unit of work succeeded.

What it deliberately is **not**: it names no pull request and no board item and produces none, writes no `job_payload`, no `agent_session_id` (there is no session to resume) and no `repository`/`task_id` — those two columns dropped `NOT NULL` for exactly this, so the type-checker rather than each reader's memory is what keeps a maintenance run out of retry, reset, stalled detection, the review ledger and continuation pinning. Since issue #972 it does have a dispatch — the durable unit that delivers the request — but an **agent-less** one that resolves no trigger, provisions no worktree and takes no project slot, so it still cannot count against a project's concurrency. **`kind` is the discriminator** — never `phase`, and never a null coordinate: every pipeline-scoped reader asks for `kind = 'pipeline'` positively, so a second maintenance kind is excluded from all of them without any being edited again.

**Scoped to the worker's project.** Every working worker belongs to exactly one project, so the run has a project to hang off and appears in that project's list like anything else; the machine's *oldest* enrollment is what names it, deliberately unfiltered by enrollment status (the run records what happened to a machine, it does not route work). The boundary that comes with it, stated so nobody rediscovers it as a bug: **a worker enrolled in no project cannot be updated this way**. It is refused, never silently skipped, on all three surfaces — `PRECONDITION_FAILED` from `workers.requestUpdate`, a `no-project` disposition from the fan-out, and a `skipped` member in a staged rollout (which must settle rather than wait, since advancing the rollout would never change the answer).

**Reading it back.** Three durable readouts now, each answering a different question. `swarm workers list` marks `update <ref> pending` while a request is outstanding and `update <ref> <outcome>` once answered — the outcome column exists because a machine that declined or failed carries on looking entirely normal otherwise — and the state is on both roster read models. Beside it, the runs list carries one row per machine asked, with the history surviving each later request: an operator can see when a machine was last updated and what happened, after the fact, in the same place as every other run. Both runs surfaces mark that row as maintenance rather than letting it read as pipeline work (issue #974) — a caution `Maintenance` pill keyed on `kind`, never on `phase` or a null coordinate, on the *kind* axis so it cannot conflate with the status badge beside it — and the run's own page swaps the pipeline `running`/`failed` callouts for maintenance-shaped ones naming the machine, the build, the machine's own failure prose and the `swarm workers update` that asks again, offering none of the recovery controls `requirePipelineRun` already refuses. Third, the machine's **own page** carries an `Update history` card (issue #977) — the "when did *this* machine last update, and did it work?" question, which the global runs list can only answer by being filtered for one machine. It is the same `runs` rows, read per machine (`listWorkerUpdateRunsForWorker`: `kind = 'worker-update'` AND `worker_id`, newest first, bounded at ten — `idx_runs_worker_id` already covers the predicate, so no migration), surfaced as `DashboardWorkerDetailView.updateHistory` and rendered with the *shared* `RunStatusBadge`, so an outcome reads identically wherever it appears. So the three divide cleanly: `swarm workers list` answers "what is this machine's latest outcome?", the machine's page "what has been asked of this machine?", and the runs list "what has SWARM been doing?" — and only the latter two survive the next request, because only they are `runs`. Two boundaries the per-machine read takes on deliberately: it is keyed on `worker_id`, the **live** link, so it needs no `maintenance_machine` fallback (a retired machine has no page, which is exactly why the *list* keeps that fallback); and an entry is withheld when its project is outside a restricted viewer's scope, the same rule the Active job card applies — so a viewer who reached the page through another enrollment they can access, but cannot access the oldest one the run hangs off, sees no history rather than a run `/runs/<id>` would refuse them. Re-issuing the mutation overwrites an unanswered request; there is no cancel.

### Asking the whole fleet (issue #921)

`workers.requestUpdateForMine({ target })` asks the **same** thing of every machine the caller owns, in one action. Nothing about the per-worker lifecycle moves: each machine that is asked gets the same `requestWorkerUpdate` row write, the same durable `worker-update` dispatch and wake-up, the same frame and the same daemon decision layer as above. What is new is the selection and a vocabulary for the machines it deliberately does not ask.

**Owner-scoped, deliberately.** The set is `listWorkersForOwner(ctx.user.id)` and nothing else — `requestUpdate`'s strictly-owner-only rule inherited rather than restated, which matters because this is still the one mutation whose effect is to run different code on somebody's hardware. Whether an installation administrator may signal machines they do not own was issue #922's question, answered by a *second procedure over the same fan-out* (below) rather than by widening this one; the name it was given for its scope (`requestUpdateForMine`, mirroring `listMine`) is what left the admin-facing name free, and an `instanceAdmin` calling this one still gets their own machines and nobody else's.

**One machine's state never refuses the whole call.** `requestUpdate` throws `CONFLICT` for a machine still in the dispatch pool, which is right for a call about one machine and wrong for a fleet: one un-drained machine out of twelve would abort the request and tell the operator nothing about the other eleven. So `fanOutWorkerUpdate` (`src/api/worker-update-fanout.ts` — control-plane policy beside `worker-access.ts`, not a router) throws for no machine's state and answers a **disposition** per machine instead: `requested` (recorded, live session, push on its way), `queued-offline` (recorded; no live session, so `resendPendingWorkerUpdateToWorker` states it again on the next connection), `in-pool` (not asked — not draining; the CLI names `swarm workers drain <id>`, the remedy `requestUpdate`'s own refusal names), `already-asked` (an unanswered request for this same target, left exactly as it is) and `answered` (an outcome already reported for this exact target, returned rather than the machine re-asked). Only the first two write and publish. The draining precondition is **unrelaxed**, which is why this phase cannot turn a fleet-wide update into a fleet-wide outage: it asks only machines an operator has already taken out of the pool, and it cannot drain anything itself. A fleet action reads its machines once and writes to them one at a time, so `drainingSince` on the list it was handed is stale the moment another session undrains one — which is exactly why the enforcing copy of the check is the row write's own predicate (above) and the `drainingSince` test inside `fanOutWorkerUpdate` is only an optimisation that saves a pointless write and session lookup. A machine undrained mid-fan-out is declined by the write and reported `in-pool` like any other, rather than queued for a restart it is no longer drained for.

**Re-running is the readout.** An `applied` machine is never sent back through an apply it has already done, and a machine with an outstanding request keeps its `requestId` — so no second push, and the daemon's own dedup is never relied on to paper over a duplicate the control plane could have avoided. A pending request for a *different* target is overwritten and reported as `requested`, which is `requestUpdate`'s documented re-issue semantics and the behaviour an operator moving a fleet to one build wants. Retrying a single `failed` machine stays the per-worker command; the fan-out grows no `--retry`.

Two invariants the module is shaped around. Connectivity is `getLiveSessionForWorker` — the definition the rosters and the dispatch gate already use — never `isWorkerConnected`, whose map is local to one router process and answers nothing from the API server; it decides only the *word* reported, since the request is recorded and published either way. And the request id is minted **per machine**, never once for the fan-out: a request id names one request to one machine, which is what `recordWorkerUpdateReport`'s `(worker_id, update_request_id)` match depends on.

The procedure is still served, and is the **unstaged** form: ask every machine that is already drained, once, and report. What `swarm workers update --all <ref>` drives is the staged rollout below (issue #940). There is still no dashboard control.

### Asking the installation, and who may (issue #922)

`workers.requestUpdateForInstallation({ target })` is the administrator-facing form: the **same** `fanOutWorkerUpdate` over a different selection — `listAllWorkers()` where phase 1 hands over `listWorkersForOwner` — and nothing else is duplicated. The fan-out takes an already-resolved worker list precisely so this could be a selection rather than a second copy of the policy.

**The authorization question, and the answer.** The codebase held both stances and neither generalises on its own: issue #800 made `set-scm-credential`, `remove`, `consent` and `update-enrollment` **strictly the machine owner's** (an `instanceAdmin` gets the same `NOT_FOUND` a stranger does), while issue #647 made the unfiltered roster an **administrator's view**. The deciding difference is that each of #800's four *takes something of the owner's and keeps it* — a credential the administrator would then hold, the machine's existence, the owner's consent to share it, the constraints their machine runs under — whereas a request takes nothing and decides nothing. Both switches that decide whether a machine moves stay the owner's, and neither needs the administrator's cooperation: the **host opt-in** (`SWARM_WORKER_SELF_UPDATE`, read from the machine's own environment and never from the wire — unset it and restart, and the daemon reports `declined` to everything), and the **drain**, which is still strictly the owner's (issue #919) and is deliberately *not* widened here. The fan-out's unrelaxed draining precondition is what makes the second one bite: a machine its owner has not drained is reported `in-pool` and untouched, so an administrator can neither move an unasked-for machine nor take the installation's capacity down. The rule is written down for operators in `docs/onboarding-worker.md` beside #800's, which is where it belongs — the code was the wrong place for a decision this size.

**Refused outright, never narrowed.** A non-`instanceAdmin` gets `FORBIDDEN` naming `swarm workers update --all` — `FORBIDDEN` rather than the owner-scoped commands' `NOT_FOUND` on #647's own reasoning (the caller named no worker id, so there is no existence to hide). It never falls back to the caller's own machines: an installation-wide action that silently became an owner-scoped one would be read as the whole installation.

**Reporting an opt-out needs a pre-request read.** A recorded request resets `update_status` to NULL, so the outcome a machine had last reported is destroyed by asking it again — and `declined` is the *only* evidence the control plane ever gets that a host has not opted in. `WorkerUpdateFanoutEntry.lastReportedStatus` therefore carries the status off the caller's own snapshot, before the write, and the router derives `optedOut` from it. It is a report annotation and **not** a disposition: a machine that declined is still asked again, because its owner may have opted in since.

**Auditing: `workers.update_requested_by_user_id`** (migration `0064_worker_update_requester.sql`) is the seventh `update_*` column and the one that stopped being inferable once the requester and the owner could be different people. It belongs to the *request* half — written with `update_target`/`update_requested_at`, reset by the next request, untouched by a report — and `requestWorkerUpdate` takes it as a required argument at every seam, so no caller can record an unattributed request. `ON DELETE SET NULL`, not the owner column's `CASCADE`: a machine outlives whoever asked it to update. Beside it, the API server logs one `installation-wide worker update requested` line per call carrying the requester, the build and every machine's disposition — the row is one machine's history, the log line is the fleet action.

`swarm workers request-update <ref>` is the operator surface, a separate subcommand rather than a flag on `update` because it is a different *authorization*, not a different scope of the same one. Lines are grouped by owner (the selection is sorted before the fan-out, which preserves input order), and exit 0 whatever the dispositions say. It drains nothing and stages nothing; the staged rollout below stays each owner's own.

### Staging the fleet in waves that halt on a bad build (issue #940)

Phase 1 could only ask machines an operator had already drained by hand. That is what kept it from taking a fleet's capacity down — and also what kept it from being a rollout. **A rollout owns the drains**: `worker_update_rollouts` + `worker_update_rollout_members` (`src/db/schema/workerUpdateRollouts.ts`) name the machines to move and the order to move them in, and one advance drains at most `wave_size` of them, waits for them to go idle, signals them through the *same* `fanOutWorkerUpdate`, verifies each one came back on the new build, returns it to the dispatch pool, and only then lets the next wave start. Nothing about the per-machine lifecycle moves: same row write, same publish, same frame, same daemon decision layer.

**Advancing is what the operator asks for, and one way to do it.** `workers.startFleetUpdate({ target, waveSize? })` starts a rollout, and **calling it again for the same target advances it** rather than refusing — `swarm workers update --all <ref>` re-run is the advance, exactly as re-running `swarm workers drain` is already the supported way to poll a drain, and for the same reason: the useful answer is not "done" but "where is it now". A *different* target while one is in progress is `CONFLICT` naming `workers.fleetUpdateStatus` (`swarm workers update --status`), because two rollouts over the same machines would drain and undrain each other's members. **One `in_progress` rollout per owner is a partial unique index**, not a read-then-insert: two calls landing at the same instant would both find nothing and both insert, so the loser's `23505` is caught and re-resolved against whichever won. `halted`/`completed` rows are exempt and accumulate as history, which is what makes "start a new one" the way past a halt. Since issue #941 the operator's call is the manual nudge and the readout rather than the only path forward — the sub-section below is what actually finishes a rollout.

**The advance is one locked pass in six steps** (`src/api/worker-update-rollout.ts` — control-plane policy beside `worker-update-fanout.ts`, not a router; only the vocabulary is a domain fact and stays in `src/identity/worker-update-rollout.ts`, so the schema and repository can import it without `src/identity` or `src/db` ever importing `src/api`). `advanceUnderRolloutLock` takes the rollout row `FOR UPDATE` and reads the members *under* that lock, so two advances cannot both decide the same wave has settled and both drain the next one. Then: **settle** every `signalled` member from its own `workers` row — `applied` and `adopted` move on to verification (`isRestartingUpdateStatus`, so the two restarting words are asked for rather than named here), `already-current` settles at once (nothing restarted, so there is nothing to come back from), `failed`/`refused`/`declined` settles badly and halts with the machine's message recorded verbatim; **verify** every `verifying` member; **return** each well-settled member to the pool; **stand down** everything uncommitted once halted; **advance** a new wave only when nothing is in flight; **complete** when every member has settled. Each rule is a pure function returning a verdict, so the state machine reads as a table and the class beneath it only reads the world, applies verdicts and writes.

**The come-back verdict is two facts, not one.** A bumped `worker_sessions.fencing_token` is the exact "a new daemon process took the lease" signal and answers *came back*; it does not answer *on the new build*, because a machine that returned **itself** to its last known good build (issue #934) bumps the token too, and accepting that would march a whole fleet through a build none of them end up running. So the commit the machine declares at handshake (issue #918) must also have moved off `build_commit_at_signal` — and where neither side declared a build, the lease stands alone rather than a member being failed over a fact nobody recorded. Both facts are recorded **at signal time**, before the fan-out writes, since afterwards they are already the facts of a machine on its way out. Silence is the third verdict and has to be: a build that cannot start says nothing at all, so `COME_BACK_WINDOW_MS` (10 minutes, measured from the machine's own `applied` report and therefore *after* its bounded fetch/install/build) halts the rollout rather than waiting forever.

**What a halt leaves behind.** No further machine is drained or signalled; every member still `queued` or drained-but-unsignalled is settled `skipped` and put back in the pool, so the untouched majority of the fleet keeps taking work; members already in flight go on settling on later advances, because their answer is still worth recording. The machine that **failed** is deliberately left drained — it is exactly the one an operator has to look at before it is given work again, which is verbatim what issue #933's single-machine form already leaves them to do. `halted` is terminal: there is no resume and no cancel, the same stance #933 takes for a single request.

**Two rules about drains the module is shaped around.** `drained_by_rollout` is decided once, when a member leaves `queued`, from the snapshot taken before the pass drained anything — so a machine the operator had drained for their own reasons is left exactly as they left it, and only one the rollout took out of the pool is put back. And the drain is **re-asserted on every pass** over the wave, for three reasons at once: it is idempotent (`coalesce` keeps the first instant), it returns the row *after* this pass's own drain — which is what the fan-out reads its eligibility from, so handing it the pre-drain snapshot would have it report every machine the rollout just drained as `in-pool` — and it puts back a machine another session undrained mid-wave.

`swarm workers update --all <ref> [--wave N]` and `swarm workers update --status` are the operator surfaces: the whole member table plus a tally every run, exit 0 whatever it says, because it is a report rather than a pass/fail. The default wave is **one** machine, deliberately — the default has to be the safe one — and `--wave N` is uncapped, since an operator asking for their whole fleet at once has chosen a big-bang with their eyes open. Still no dashboard control, and nothing installation-wide: the set is `listWorkersForOwner(ctx.user.id)` and nothing wider, so issue #922's administrator scope is not answered here by accident.

### Advancing a rollout with nobody watching (issue #941)

A rollout started once now completes, or halts, without the operator running anything again — which is what makes issue #921's "one operator action moves every worker to a target build" literally true. Phase 2's `advanceRollout` is unchanged and is still the only thing that decides anything; what this phase adds is *when* it runs (`src/router/worker-rollout-advance.ts` — the trigger module, in the router because that is the process holding worker sockets, serving the delivery routes and already hosting the control plane's other timers).

**Three triggers, which between them cover every way a rollout can move.** A machine's **update report** landing on `POST /worker/delivery/update-report` is the answer step 1 reads off the `workers` row, so it arriving is the moment that answer became readable; the hook is added *after* the existing `recordWorkerUpdateReport` call rather than in place of it, and is deliberately not gated on `recorded` — the outcome is written either way, and an advance decides everything from durable state. A machine's **handshake** opening a `/worker/stream` socket is the event the come-back verdict waits for and the only one it has: by the time `handleWorkerStreamOpen` runs, the daemon has already taken its fenced lease and declared the build it is running, which are exactly the two facts that verdict is reached against. And a **periodic tick** covers the two moves no event announces — a machine that applied and never came back (silence is the verdict, and silence sends nothing) and a wave whose members were still mid-phase when they were drained, since a machine going idle is not something the control plane is told. `ROLLOUT_ADVANCE_TICK_MS` is one minute and coded rather than configurable, like `COME_BACK_WINDOW_MS` and `MAX_FAILED_STARTS`: against a ten-minute come-back window it is a rounding error, and against a phase that runs for many minutes it is the shortest wait worth having.

**Every hook is fire-and-forget and caught**, on exactly the contract `resendPendingWorkerUpdateToWorker` already keeps on the socket-open path: they return `void`, so the report route and the handshake stay synchronous, and every failure is logged rather than raised — a rollout must never be able to fail a machine's report or refuse its connection. A missed advance costs latency and nothing else, since the next event or the next tick re-decides the whole thing from durable state. Both per-machine hooks are injected through their module's existing `deps` object, exactly as `recordWorkerUpdateReport` and `resendPendingWorkerUpdate` are.

**The rollout is resolved through the machine's owner, not its member row.** An owner has at most one `in_progress` rollout (the partial unique index decides that) and a machine has exactly one owner, so `getWorker` → `findInProgressRolloutForOwner` is the same answer by an indexed lookup, where a `worker_id` search over `worker_update_rollout_members` would be a scan whose cost grows with the rollout history — on a path that runs at every handshake. The one case the two differ is a machine enrolled after the rollout started, which is not a member; advancing on its event is still right, because an advance only ever reads the rollout's own durable state and a spare trigger is a no-op.

**Nothing new serializes the triggers**, because the rollout row's own `FOR UPDATE` lock already does: two hooks firing at the same instant take it in turn and the second decides against the state the first left, so a wave cannot be drained or signalled twice. That lock was written for this phase — phase 2 had one caller. The tick itself runs one sweep at a time for a weaker reason: a sweep that outran the interval would only queue a second set of callers behind those same locks.

Out of scope, deliberately and unchanged: the rollout's own policy — wave size, the come-back verdict, the halt rule and the undrain rule are all phase 2's and are only *triggered* here — plus a dashboard control and issue #922's installation-wide scope.

### Returning to the last known good build (issue #934)

An update the machine applies is **not trusted until a daemon running it has handshaked once**, because the channel that delivered it is the same channel a bad build takes away. Once the new code cannot reach the control plane there is no socket to push a corrective update down — the control plane sees a worker that went away and nothing more — so this is the one part of the feature that has to be recovered by the machine itself, and it is not optional.

The record is on disk before the build it judges ever runs: `pendingVerification` names the applied commit, the one it replaced, a **failed-start counter**, and — since issue #973 — how many peer daemons adopted that build. `src/transport/build-verification.ts` is the daemon's side of it, at three moments. **Process start** — the first thing the process does, before it reads its environment, locks its checkout or opens the transport — counts this start; counting at *start* rather than on an observed failure is what makes a build that dies before reaching any code of its own recoverable, since the count is already durable by the time it dies. **That is why `src/transport/connect-entry.ts` is a bootstrap rather than the daemon.** ESM evaluates a module's static imports before its own body, so a counter at the top of the daemon's module runs *after* the whole worker dependency graph — every integration, the harness, the executor — has been evaluated, and a build whose bad code throws at import time would never be counted at all: `failedStarts` stays put, the supervisor restarts the same build, and the machine crash-loops forever on precisely the failure this mechanism exists to recover from. The entrypoint therefore statically imports only the verification and state mechanism plus the logger (node builtins and `zod` below that), and reaches the daemon (`src/transport/worker-main.ts`) through a **dynamic** `import()` that cannot run until the count is on disk. `tests/unit/transport/build-verification.test.ts` pins the entrypoint's import list and launches the real entrypoint with a `worker-main.ts` that throws during ESM loading, which is the only way to assert a property of a process rather than of a call. **The first session** promotes the build: `lastKnownGood` becomes the new commit and the record is cleared, so the counter only ever measures *consecutive* failures, and the promotion is guarded because `onSession` fires on every reconnect. **A handshake the control plane rejects outright** — `isFatalHandshakeRejection` (`src/transport/worker-client.ts`): a protocol mismatch, a capability rejection, or a refused credential — returns at once rather than waiting the counter out, because those are exactly the "this build cannot serve this control plane" cases and no restart changes them. A *session conflict* is deliberately not one of them: that is about the other daemon, and returning over it would discard a perfectly good update.

The return itself is the apply's three steps aimed at `lastKnownGood` (`git checkout --detach` → `npm ci` → `npm run build`, the same runner and the same bounded output tail), followed by clearing the record and exiting **0** so launchd `KeepAlive` / systemd `Restart=always` starts the daemon on the good build. `MAX_FAILED_STARTS` is 3 and is a coded constant, not configuration: a larger number only lengthens an outage on a machine nobody can reach. The budget it is measured against is that constant **plus the peers that adopted this build**, because the counter is a fact about the install root while the failure it describes is one daemon's: the state directory is keyed on the install root alone, so on a shared one every peer's own healthy restart lands on the same counter, and three daemons coming up cleanly would otherwise spend a three-start budget between them and roll the machine back off a build that was working (issue #973). Each adopter records itself as it takes its licence to restart, so it buys exactly the one start it is about to make; on a machine with one daemon nothing adopts and the budget is the constant it always was. A return that *itself* fails keeps the record, logs an error naming the install root and the step, and exits non-zero — the machine is down rather than crash-looping quietly, and a later start attempts the return again, because clearing the record instead would send it back into the build that could not start.

Nothing is reported from the daemon. The control plane learns which build a machine came back on exactly the way it learns every other one, from the `build` its next handshake declares (issue #918) — so a machine that returned itself shows its previous commit again on `/workers`, with the `update <ref> applied` the row already recorded beside it.

### Several daemons on one install root (issue #935)

The control-plane host runs four daemons serving four *project* repositories out of **one** npm-linked SWARM checkout (`ai/LOCAL_CONFIG.md`), so the install root is shared where the checkout (`SWARM_WORKER_REPO_ROOT`) never is — which is why the checkout gets a lock that *refuses a second daemon at startup* and the install root gets something else entirely. Two questions, two records, both under the install root's own state directory and both host-local by construction, exactly as the checkout lock is (`src/worktree/install-lock.ts`):

- **Two daemons updating at once** would interleave a `git checkout` with an `npm ci`. `applyUpdateTarget` therefore takes a machine-local exclusive lock — `update-lock/owner.json`, the same atomic `mkdirSync` claim, pid-liveness-first reclaim rule and typed `…HeldError` as `src/worktree/checkout-lock.ts`, over the same `src/worktree/local-lock.ts` predicates — after it has validated the target and read the tree, and **before it fetches**; it is released in a `finally` on every path. Reclaiming a *lapsed* lock goes one step further than the checkout lock's single retry, because read-remove-create is three steps and two daemons that read the same dead record both pass the reclaim test — the second one's remove deletes the first one's fresh claim and both come away holding the install root. The replacement therefore runs inside the atomic takeover guard `src/worktree/host-local-runtime.ts` uses one scope down (`update-lock.takeover/holder.json`, itself reclaimable after five minutes so a daemon that died inside one wedges nothing) and **re-reads the owner under it**: the observation that sent a daemon there predates the guard, and only what it sees while holding it may be removed. It refreshes itself on an unref'd interval rather than asking the daemon to, because it is held across a multi-minute `npm ci` by a caller with no loop of its own. A daemon that cannot take it **waits for the holder** (issue #973, `INSTALL_LOCK_FOLLOW_WAIT_MS` — derived from this module's own step timeouts, since one fetch, one checkout and two `npm` steps is the longest apply it can run) rather than giving up on it: holding that lock *afterwards* is the only proof the holder's fetch and build are over, because the holder releases it in a `finally` on every path. **What the wait proves is only that somebody was in here**, never what they did, so the adoption is gated on the install root's own record rather than on the wait: `pendingVerification` naming HEAD is the one thing that says a *completed* apply put the tree where it is, since the holder writes `applying` with `pendingVerification: null` before it checks anything out and only a successful `applyCommit` fills it in. It is necessarily recent, too — an update request arrives over a socket the daemon handshaked on, and a handshake is what clears that record. On that evidence, and only then, the follower resolves the target off the refs the holder's own fetch already updated, with **no network of its own**, and answers `already-current` — two facts rather than one: HEAD equals the target *and* this machine finished putting it there. A completed apply that landed something *else* (a peer asked for a different ref) is `refused` naming the commit the install root is actually on, and so is HEAD sitting on neither the build this machine last proved nor one a daemon here finished applying — that is the half-written tree a holder that died between its `git checkout` and its `npm ci` leaves behind, and it is exactly what a peer must not restart onto. Everything else — a holder that refused, one whose own `git fetch` failed, one returning the machine to its last known good build — landed nothing and moved no ref, so the follower **does the ordinary fetching update for itself**. Adopting on the wait alone was the bug worth naming: a follower resolving `refs/remotes/<remote>/<target>` off refs nobody refreshed finds HEAD, calls it the target, and settles a rollout member as done on a build the machine is not running. The one case this cannot tell apart is a peer whose apply *failed* and rolled the tree back, which on disk is the same picture as a peer that returned the machine to its last known good build; both fall through, because one wasted rebuild behind a broken build is cheaper than refusing a daemon that could have applied, and the lock serializes them. A daemon that waits the whole window out and still cannot get in refuses with the holder's own message, naming that daemon. The old 5s `INSTALL_LOCK_WAIT_MS` survives as `returnToLastKnownGood`'s alone, where a wait only lengthens an outage. `returnToLastKnownGood` takes the same lock, since it moves the same tree; a conflict there is a `failed` with stage `lock`, which leaves the machine down and retrying on its next start rather than rebuilding under somebody else's update. Everything it acts on is read *after* that lock is held, for the same reason the lock exists: a peer that lands a newer build while this daemon queues leaves the pre-lock reading describing an install root that no longer exists, and acting on it would check a superseded `lastKnownGood` back out and erase the new build's pending-verification record. A post-lock state that no longer names the build this daemon set out to abandon is `nothing-pending` — someone else's newer build is not this one's to roll back.
- **One daemon updating while another is mid-phase** is not a lock question at all — the peer running a phase holds nothing and is not asking permission — so every daemon writes a **participant record** while it runs: `participants/<pid>.json`, carrying a `busy` flag flipped where `worker-main.ts` fires and settles a dispatch (derived from the same `inFlight` set the update's own idle wait reads), annotated with the worker id at handshake so a refusal names something an operator can drain, refreshed on the *same timer as the checkout lock* (hence the shared TTL) and dropped on every exit path. With any **other** live participant busy, the update is `refused` — **just before the checkout** since issue #973, rather than before the fetch: a fetch writes remote-tracking refs and swaps no code, and putting the guard where code is actually replaced is what lets a daemon that only needs to *notice* the machine is already on the target through, which is the whole of a peer's own update. It names that worker. The check is a **snapshot, not a reservation** — nothing stops an undrained peer starting a phase a moment later — so draining the other daemons on the machine stays the operator's act (`swarm workers drain`), which is what the refusal says. A dead participant's record blocks nothing: liveness is the fast path, the refreshed timestamp the backstop, and a record that answers neither is removed as it is read, since nothing else sweeps that directory. An unreadable-but-fresh record counts as busy, on `local-lock.ts`'s own rule that an artifact this process cannot identify is occupied rather than free.

**Every daemon on the machine ends up on the new build (issue #973).** Making `already-current` mean "the install root is on the target *and* this machine finished putting it there" is what lets the daemon above it act on one: `src/transport/worker-update.ts` compares that commit against `build` — the daemon's *own* startup commit, resolved in `worker-main.ts` before the transport connects and therefore the one thing a peer's checkout cannot have moved since — and splits the answer in two. Equal means the process is running those files: `already-current`, the literal no-op it always claimed to be. Unequal means a peer moved them while this process went on executing the modules it loaded at start: **`adopted`**, and the same exit-0 restart path `applied` takes. Both are successes and both are wire-visible, which is AC 5's "distinguishable" — `applied` is the daemon that paid for the fetch, `adopted` one that restarted onto a peer's — and `adopted` is the one word this issue adds to `WORKER_UPDATE_STATUSES`. It maps onto no `UpdateOutcome` member by design: the mechanism answers about the install *root*, and only a daemon can tell the two readings apart. A `build` this daemon could not identify biases to `adopted`, because the failure being fixed is a daemon that did *not* restart; `dirty` is deliberately not consulted, since it would make a machine genuinely on the target report `adopted`, come back on the same commit, and be read by the rollout's come-back verdict as "came back still on the build it was asked to move off". The readers of the vocabulary ask `isRestartingUpdateStatus` (`src/identity/worker-update-rollout.ts`) rather than naming `applied`: a rollout member moves to `verifying` on either, and `settleWorkerUpdateRun` completes the run on either.

What is deliberately **not** here: any cross-machine coordination (the directory is keyed on a local realpath), any control-plane view of participants or grouping of workers by install root, any leader election — the existing lock already decides who fetches and who merely restarts — and any draining or restarting of a peer *from another daemon's process* (no signals, no pid killing). The restart is the supervisor's job on both paths, exactly as it already was for `applied`. One boundary is worth stating plainly rather than leaving to be rediscovered: **a daemon comes over on its own update request.** Nothing here manufactures one for a daemon the operator or the rollout did not ask, so `swarm workers update <worker-id> <ref>` moves one daemon, while `update --all`, `request-update` and the staged rollout ask every machine and are what bring a shared install root's daemons all the way over (`docs/onboarding-worker.md`, `docs/configuration.md`).

## Harness (agent-CLI execution engine)

Once the worker has a provisioned worktree, it hands off to the harness (`src/harness/agent-cli.ts`, `runAgentCli`) to actually run the agent. The harness is deliberately narrow: it spawns `claude` or `antigravity` (Node's `child_process.spawn`, no subprocess library) with the worktree as CWD, streams stdout/stderr line-by-line (optional callbacks + `logger.debug`) while accumulating the full output, and resolves with `{ exitCode, signal, stdout, stderr, durationMs, timedOut }`. A non-zero exit is a normal outcome the caller inspects — only a spawn failure (e.g. the CLI isn't installed) throws. `timeoutMs` and an `AbortSignal` both kill the run (SIGTERM, escalating to SIGKILL). Prompt construction, persona/token selection, and the queue→worktree→harness→cleanup lifecycle live in the worker (SWARM-17), not here.

**Live output (issue #356).** Claude runs in print mode with `--output-format stream-json --verbose`, not `json`: the single-document format is buffered until the process exits, so a run's log stayed empty for its whole duration and got one event at termination. `src/harness/claude-stream.ts` decodes those NDJSON records incrementally into readable lines (assistant progress, `Tool started/completed/failed: <name>`, and a one-line rendering of a failed terminal `result` record) and forwards them through the same `onStdout` callback every CLI uses — tool inputs, tool results, and thinking are never rendered, and unparseable protocol lines are dropped rather than leaked. The terminal `result` record still supplies the run's final text, session id, and usage (`src/harness/usage.ts`), and its rendered error line is what failure classification reads, so a streamed 429 stays a deferred, resumable rate limit. **The control plane performs that write, for every run** (issue #544). The worker streams its lines up as `stream-log` frames — batched every 100ms by the transport executor's own wrapper (`createAssignmentRunAgent`, `src/transport/assignment-execution.ts`), flushed on every settle path, and, for Claude only, carrying a "still running" heartbeat after 30s of silence so a working run is distinguishable from a hung one — and the control plane appends them to `run_output_events` (`src/router/stream-log-persistence.ts`), fire-and-forget but ordered per run. `src/worker/live-output.ts` is what remains of the retired in-process batcher: the heartbeat's cadence and its rendered line, shared so that one *user-visible* string has one definition. The worker never persists locally, so every worker — the control-plane host's own included — produces identical output.

**Capability-aware target routing (issue #346).** A phase's agent config holds an *ordered* list of `targets` (`agents.<phase>.targets`, `src/config/schema.ts`). **#339's eligibility gate is what walks that list against a real host's capabilities** — the CLIs an enrolled worker declares — selecting a worker and a target together and binding the target to an authenticated worker execution claim. `selectTarget` (`src/worker/target-selection.ts`) is the pure ordered walk the gate's decision is adapted back into (`targetSelectionFor`, `src/worker/consumer.ts`); a dispatch the gate selected no worker for resolves **no availability set at all** and so keeps the phase's highest-priority target. Nothing is routed against the control plane's own installed CLIs: `processJob` runs there and executes nothing, so the `cli_quotas` snapshot — which describes a worker's own machine rather than the host that dispatches — has no routing consumer (issue #703, re-keyed onto the worker by issue #823). `agentOverrideFor` (`src/worker/consumer.ts`) feeds the resolved `cli`/`model`/`reasoning` into the unchanged four-tier model chain. A per-run pinned retry (`cliOverride`/`modelOverride`/`reasoningOverride`) bypasses routing and is evaluated as one exact target.

**Model + reasoning mapping (issue #180).** The harness takes a *logical* `model` plus a normalized `reasoning` level and turns them into per-CLI launch args through `resolveModelLaunch` (`src/harness/models.ts`), the one place provider-specific reasoning semantics live: Claude gets `--model <alias> --effort <level>`, Codex gets `--model <id> -c model_reasoning_effort="<level>"`, and Antigravity — which has no reasoning flag — gets the reasoning folded back into the combined `agy models` slug it passes to `--model` (e.g. logical `gemini-3.6-flash` + `high` → `gemini-3.6-flash-high`). A config that stored a combined string directly — a current slug, or a retired pre-1.1.5 display string (`"Gemini 3.6 Flash (High)"`, issue #409) — is decomposed and re-resolved to today's slug. A selection naming a model the provider has since **retired** resolves to that model's live replacement with the substitution logged, on either CLI it has happened on: antigravity's `gemini-3.5-flash` (issue #892), which `agy` rejects as an unknown `--model`, and Codex's `gpt-5.4`/`gpt-5.4-mini` (issue #893), which the API answers with a `400 … not supported`. Both retirement lists sit beside the catalog (`RETIRED_ANTIGRAVITY_MODELS`, `RETIRED_CODEX_MODELS`) precisely because deleting an entry would make a config still storing it fail `isKnownModel` and stop parsing. The normalized level enum is a **union** across the CLIs rather than any one CLI's own list — `ultra` is a Codex level claude's `--effort` has no equivalent for — so which levels are real is per *model* (`reasoningChoices`), never per CLI. A stored target that names a value only one non-default catalog can claim is pinned to that CLI at the config boundary (`pinnedCliForModel` via `AgentTargetSchema`, `src/config/schema.ts`) rather than left to the phase's coded default CLI — that default is `claude` for every phase, so an unpinned target took an antigravity value, or a retired Codex id, to `claude --model`. Omitting reasoning preserves each CLI's own default (no flag for Claude/Codex; the model's default variant for Antigravity). `src/harness/models.ts` is the versioned capability catalog — logical models, their supported reasoning levels, and known defaults — cached in code, never discovered by invoking a model.

### Durable dispatch state machine (issue #284, ADR-002)

Every attempt to start or resume a pipeline phase is one row in the Postgres
`dispatches` table — the single source of truth for pending/in-flight
orchestration state (`docs/decisions/ADR-002-durable-dispatch-state-machine.md`).
