BullMQ jobs are pure wake-ups carrying a `dispatchId`; the worker acts only
after atomically claiming the dispatch (`pending`/`retry-scheduled` → `leased`),
so a cancelled, completed, or superseded dispatch refuses every late delivery —
redelivery, delayed retry, slot release, or reconciliation. The lifecycle is
`pending → leased → running → completed/failed`, with `retry-scheduled` for
deferred attempts (the derived retry payload is persisted on the row *before*
any queue work, so a crash never loses retry intent) and `cancelled` for
user/operator cancellation. Wake-up job ids are deterministic per
(dispatch, wake sequence), so the reconciler's re-publish is a queue no-op when
the wake-up already exists. **A board move is a third way a waiting dispatch goes
terminal** (issue #909), alongside operator cancellation and coalesce-key
supersession: the card's new column retires the board-driven phase an earlier move
left queued, and — unlike either of those two — it settles that dispatch's
retry-pending run row `failed` in the **same transaction**, which is precisely what
keeps `backfillOrphanedDeferredRuns` from resurrecting it at the next router start
(see the `pm-status-changed` bullet under "Trigger wiring"). Project-capacity waits are `pending` dispatches with
wait reason `project-capacity`, woken by slot releases under the
continuation-priority policy — there is no separate Redis registry. **A second
event-woken `pending` wait sequences the phases of one task** (issues #759 and
#761): a dispatch whose task has an *earlier* phase that has not settled waits as
`task-in-flight` and is woken by that phase settling
(`promoteTaskInFlightWaits`, from `processJob`'s `finally` — every settle path,
success or not), rather than being discarded. The *same* phase arriving twice is
still completed `skipped-duplicate` — **unless the in-process holder's own dispatch
has already settled** (issue #858), in which case the collision is not a repeated
delivery but a replacement dispatch racing a job the router has not given up on yet,
and it waits as `task-in-flight` too. So the two halves of that collision stay
distinguishable on the row; and the collision is read from the dispatch table as
well as from the worker's in-process map, so the verdict does not depend on which
worker the dispatch was routed to. Two durable reads answer it, under one wait
reason: `findExecutingDispatchForTask` (`leased`/`running`, excluding the asking
dispatch and `merge-automation`), which every phase waits behind, and
`findActivePlanningDispatchForTask` (any non-terminal state), which **only
Implementation** asks — so it cannot overtake a Planning dispatch that is merely
queued (issue #761, "Task identity" above). Nothing asks the mirror question, so
the second read can never produce a mutual defer. **A third such wait sequences the
phases of one pull request** (issue #850), which the task key cannot see because the
PR-driven phases carry distinct suffixed ids: `findExecutingWritingDispatchForPullRequest`
reads the dispatch row's own normalized `repository` + `pr_number` for a
`leased`/`running` dispatch in one of the three *branch-writing* phases
(`respond-to-review`, `respond-to-ci`, `resolve-conflicts` — `BRANCH_WRITING_PHASES`),
and the verdict is asymmetric. A **writing phase defers** as `pr-in-flight`, woken by
the holder settling (`promotePullRequestInFlightWaits`, from the same `finally`). A
**Review is dropped** — completed `skipped-pr-in-flight`, with its PR+SHA dedup claim
released and its verdict reservation abandoned so no ledger slot (issue #235) is spent
— because it is pinned to a head SHA and a deferred Review would wake to review the
commit the writer has already replaced; the re-review for the new head is already
covered by `scheduleFollowUpReview`, by that head's own `checks` event, or by
`ci-no-fix-recovery`. Only executing writers hold and a Review neither holds nor waits,
so no two dispatches of one pull request can defer to each other, and a null `phase`
deliberately does **not** match here (unlike the task read, whose caller may treat it as
"some other phase") — the verdict turns on *which* phase holds the PR, and assuming an
unrecorded one is a writer would defer a Review behind a Review. All three
pre-run waits share one deferral (`deferBeforeRun`): no attempt spent, the run
row visible while it waits, continuation dedup claims retained, and the
reconciler's republish underneath (a `pending` row is wakeable unless it waits on
project capacity). A worker
startup + periodic reconciler (`src/dispatch/reconciler.ts`) reclaims expired
leases (failing the dispatch and its still-`running` run together), re-publishes
lost wake-ups, and — once, at startup — imports legacy shapes (Redis
pending-continuation entries, deferred runs with no active dispatch, and
pre-#292 `not-ready` merge-follow-up intent) as durable dispatches.
Merge automation is the one agent-less dispatch kind (issue #292): a
`merge-automation` payload (dedup key `merge:<reviewRunId>`, linked to the
approving Review run) skips triggers, worktrees, and project slots entirely —
`processJob` routes its claimed wake-up straight to
`processMergeAutomationDispatch`, which merges via the `ScmMergeProvider` and
settles the dispatch itself (`completed` with a `merge-*` outcome, or
`retry-scheduled` while transiently `not-ready` or while the provider is
failing). **It never settles `failed` (issue #923).** A `provider-error` used to
fail the dispatch on its first occurrence, and since the dispatch is deduped on
`merge:<reviewRunId>` and the review-verdict ledger already holds a `submitted`
slot for that head, one transient 5xx from the forge stranded an approved, clean
pull request permanently — three of them in one GitHub incident, each merged by
hand. It now shares the bounded budget `not-ready` already owned, sharing the
dispatch's own `attempt` counter rather than adding a second one, and a spent
budget completes as `merge-retry-exhausted` while the Review run records
`provider-error-exhausted` — distinct from `not-ready`'s `retry-exhausted`, so
run history tells "the forge kept failing" apart from a refusal SWARM decided on
its own. Which provider errors deserve the budget is decided by the *bound*, not
by a predicate: the adapters' own classifiers already route every refusal they
can name to a terminal status (GitHub's 403 → `policy-blocked`, 405/409 →
`not-ready`, merge queue → `unsupported`), so what reaches this module is the
retryable residue, and sniffing status codes here would put provider-specific
shapes in shared worker code. A permanently broken credential therefore costs
seven attempts over ~13 minutes and then settles terminally, carrying the
provider's own last message — with the *first* failed attempt already persisting
`provider-error` onto the Review run, so the reason is on the dashboard from
second one.

**It is also where a stale approved head is re-verified (issue #874),** the
prevention half of #872. A `stale-base` merge outcome (the seam bullet above)
sends the dispatch down its own branch rather than to a merge: it calls
`updatePullRequestBranch(project, prNumber, job.approvedHeadSha)`, and on
`updated` re-writes its **own payload** — `approvedHeadSha` advanced to the
commit the provider produced, a new `baseUpdates` counter incremented — through
the ordinary `scheduleDispatchRetry({ jobPayload })`, which is how the advanced
head travels to the next attempt. Five things make that bounded and honest.
(1) The retry's `attempt` is **reset to 0**: the fresh CI is a new wait, not a
continuation of the old head's. (2) `MAX_BASE_UPDATES` (2, coded on
`MAX_MERGE_RETRIES`'s precedent) caps it; beyond that the dispatch completes
`merge-retry-exhausted` — the queue's existing vocabulary for a spent budget —
while the Review run records `stale-base`, so the two give-ups stay
distinguishable by outcome as well as by message. (3) Every attempt on a head
*this dispatch produced* (`baseUpdates > 0`) first reads that head's aggregate
checks and judges them with `decideAggregateCheckOutcome` under the project's own
`pipeline.review.checks` policy — the same classifier `pr-review` and the
base-branch sweep use — so "merges only once the fresh CI is green" is SWARM's
own guarantee rather than a property of the repository's branch protection;
pending defers on the ordinary backoff, and **red settles terminally** as
`merge-not-eligible`, which is the two-green-pull-requests case this whole issue
exists for. (4) A conflicting update settles `merge-not-eligible`, an
`unsupported` provider `merge-unsupported`, and a `provider-error` rides the
same shared bounded retry as the merge call's own (issue #923) rather than
settling; each of the two terminal ones plus the red head and the spent
allowance also posts one best-effort comment on the pull request, in
`scmCommentCiFixGaveUp`'s shape, so a refusal is visible where the work is and
not only on the dashboard. (5) Before scheduling the retry the dispatch **claims
the updated head's PR+SHA review-dispatch slot** (`claimReviewDispatch`, then
refreshed to 15 minutes so it outlasts CI): without it the head's own `checks
completed` event dispatches a Review, spending one of `REVIEW_VERDICT_CAP`'s
three verdicts per update on an unchanged diff, and a pull request bumped twice
would reach its merge unable to be reviewed at all. Nothing here is serialized —
the update happens at the moment one pull request's own merge is attempted, so
several stay in flight — and the outcome vocabulary needs no migration, since
`dispatches.outcome` and `runs.review_merge_outcome` are plain `text`. The one
schema-adjacent change is `updateReviewMergeOutcome`'s new `advancedFrom`: the
row's generation guard refuses a write whose approved head differs from the one
recorded, which is what protects a superseded review's leftover attempt and would
equally have silenced every write after an advance, so the single advancing write
names the head it replaces.

**A `merged` outcome also settles the split siblings this pull request absorbed
(issue #959),** which is where the Review phase's fold-in declaration above is
finally acted on. Right after `completeDispatch(…, 'merged')` — never before, and
never for any other outcome — the dispatch calls
`settleAbsorbedChildren` (`src/dispatch/absorbed-child-settle.ts`), which reads
the declarations recorded on *every* completed Review run of that pull request,
and for each declared card: resolves it through the provider-agnostic one-card
lookup (`findWorkItemByUrlSuffix`), posts a marker-idempotent audit comment naming
the pull request and quoting the reviewer's evidence, closes it through
`PMProvider.closeWorkItem` (issue #958), and retires whatever board-driven phase
was still queued or deferred for it via the existing
`retireSupersededBoardPhases`. That last step is what stops the race the issue
describes — a waiting Implementation dispatch is cancelled and its `deferred` run
row settled in the same transaction, with no new lifecycle — and closing the card
is what makes `WorkItemBlocker.open` false, so a dependent stops being blocked at
its next dependency re-check instead of burning its seven-day budget. **A
declaration alone settles nothing:** the card must be neither the merged pull
request itself nor the task it was written for, must carry `swarm:split-child` —
the mechanical proof SWARM created it, so a reviewer naming an unrelated issue
closes nothing — must **still** carry the project's `pipeline.automationLabel`,
since removing that label is the documented way to take an item off automation
(ai/RULES.md §5) and closing a card plus retiring its queued phases is a heavier
write than the comments SWARM posts on third-party cards, and must not already be
settled, which makes a re-run a no-op. **The own-task comparison is on task
identities, not artifact numbers:** a Review run's own `taskId` is the *pull
request's* number, while a card's `taskRef` is the issue number behind it — one
forge-wide sequence, two disjoint slices of it, so comparing the two could only
ever be false. The merge dispatch therefore decodes the pull request's backing
task from its head branch (`<branchPrefix><taskId>`, the derivation
`isSwarmManagedPullRequest` and `resolveBoardItemIdForPrBranch` already share) and
hands it in; `taskRef`/`taskRepository` are then compared through `repoSlugsMatch`,
never a URL shape, against that task *and* against the pull request's own number.
If that branch read fails, or the head branch encodes no task, the settle refuses
every entry rather than guessing — the guard's input is missing, and closing the
pull request's own card is the one mistake it exists to prevent. Every guard is a skip-with-warn and every
provider failure is that entry's alone: the merge has already happened and cannot
be undone by a board error, so nothing here can fail the dispatch. **Known
limitation:** a pull request merged by a human outside SWARM's merge automation
produces no merge dispatch, so its declarations are never acted on; the
declaration stays durable on the run row, so adding the `pull-request closed +
merged` ingress path later re-decides none of this policy.

The dashboard deliberately exposes three complementary read models (issues #313, #840):

- **Queue is dispatch-centric.** The Queue API/UI (`runs.queued`) reads every waiting
  dispatch from Postgres — state, wait reason, priority, and scheduled time — never a
  BullMQ snapshot. `swarm queue clear` cancels those canonical records first. A fresh
  dispatch with no `runId` is Queue-only because no persisted attempt exists yet.
  The *view* is narrower than the table, deliberately: `runs.queued` returns
  `{ items, noTrigger }`, partitioning out a board dispatch whose card status the
  enrichment's own board read proved maps to no pipeline phase (issue #570 — SWARM's
  own `In progress` status report echoing back, a card being filed, a column reorder).
  Nothing about the dispatch lifecycle changes: those rows are still created, claimed,
  and settled as no-triggers, and they stay observable as a counted, expandable group
  under the queue. The decision is the server's — the code that already read the board —
  so no client has to learn to hide them, and it keys on the proven case only, leaving
  the enrichment's fail-open behaviour intact.
- **Runs is run-centric.** The Runs list (`runs.list` / `listRunsFromDb`) reads persisted
  attempts by their normal lifecycle, but hides a retry-pending (`deferred` or `checkpointed`) attempt linked to a pending
  or retry-scheduled dispatch to avoid displaying a duplicate row (issues #279/#316).
  Retry-pending attempts with no waiting dispatch remain visible in Runs as history and for operator recovery.
- **Liveness is item-centric** (issue #840). `runs.stalled` folds *both* tables onto the
  unit an operator recognises — a pull request (`pr:<n>`, the four SCM-driven phases of one
  PR, which mint four different `task_id`s) or a board card — scoped by
  `(projectId, repository)`, and answers which of those units have no forward path right
  now. The whole decision is the pure `src/dispatch/item-liveness.ts`, fed by two bounded
  reads (`listTaskActivitySince`, `listActiveDispatchTaskRefs`); the procedure itself
  touches nothing but Postgres — no provider call, no cache, no enrichment.

  The classification is **generic by construction**: `stalled` is the *default*, and a unit
  steps back from it only for a hand-off SWARM actually recorded on a row — a non-terminal
  dispatch, an Implementation carrying `produced_pr_url`, a `merged` merge outcome recorded
  by **any** run in the unit (aggregated over the fold rather than read off its latest row:
  a merge is terminal for the pull request, and a later `respond-to-ci` or a failed
  re-review sharing the Review's own `task_id` used to hide it and report a merged PR as
  stalled for the rest of the lookback — issue #879), a
  `manual-intervention-required` review outcome, a completed Planning run on a project that
  does not auto-advance, or an `approve` whose merge either recorded an outcome or was never
  automated — or while it is still inside a two-hour grace window. So a stall from a cause
  nobody has seen yet falls straight through to `stalled`, and the four kinds of legitimate
  waiting do not register: capacity-blocked dispatches, dependency-gated items and bounded
  retries inside budget are all non-terminal dispatch rows, and waiting on CI is the
  `produced_pr_url` hand-off plus the grace window. Read the rule list that way — it is not
  an enumeration of failure modes, and it must not become one.

  **Where it surfaces (issue #847).** The report is rendered as a collapsible
  **Stalled** section above the Queued section on the two Runs surfaces an operator
  already looks at — the cross-project `/runs` screen and a project's own Runs panel
  (`dashboard/src/components/runs/stalled-items-section.tsx`) — so a stall is visible
  without opening the database. There is no `/stalled` route and no nav entry. The
  section renders *nothing at all* when the list is empty, carries exactly one
  per-row action (**Dismiss**, below — a row otherwise links out to the pull request
  or board card through the shared `WorkItemCell`, and retrying, cancelling or
  forcing a re-review stays the Runs table's job), and never notifies: it reports on
  render, so an item stays listed until it actually moves, is dismissed, or ages out
  of the lookback window, rather than re-announcing on a timer. The cross-project
  screen gates its query exactly as the
  queue's is gated — unscoped is instance-admin-only — so a member who has chosen no
  project never polls a guaranteed `FORBIDDEN`.

  **The pull-request link is provider-resolved, not derived client-side.** A stalled
  `pull-request` row carries `prUrl` — the URL the project's *own* SCM provider spells
  it with, filled server-side in `runs.stalled` through `SCMProvider.pullRequestUrl`
  (`src/scm/types.ts`) against the row's own `repository` (issue #683). The dashboard
  never assembles one: GitLab spells a pull request `/-/merge_requests/<n>` and
  Bitbucket `/pull-requests/<n>`, so deriving `github.com/<repo>/pull/<n>` in the
  client would send those projects' operators to an unrelated or nonexistent GitHub
  repository. `WorkItemCell` prefers that resolved URL and keeps the GitHub
  derivation only as the fallback for the runs list and the Workers screen, whose
  read models do not carry a resolved URL yet; a `work-item` row links to its board
  card as before, and a project resolving no runtime-ready provider simply carries no
  `prUrl` rather than failing the report.

  **An operator can force one row's age-out** (issue #880). `stalled` is the deliberate
  default when no *recorded* hand-off explains a unit's silence, so a pull request a human
  merged or a card a human closed is reported until the 30-day window expires — even when
  the operator can see at a glance that it is done. The Stalled table therefore carries a
  trailing **Dismiss** column, one button per row, calling `runs.dismissStalled`. That
  writes one row to `stalled_dismissals`, keyed on the **liveness unit** (a unit folds
  several runs, and the row the operator sees is the unit) and carrying the
  `last_activity_at` it was dismissed at; the classification gains a `dismissed` state that
  holds only while the unit's own activity has not advanced past that instant. So a unit
  that genuinely moves again is reported normally with nothing to un-set and no background
  job, the durable `runs` rows are never modified or deleted (the write touches neither that
  table nor `dispatches`), and the write is gated at project `member` — this router's
  uniform mutation gate, never weaker than the `contributor` the read it mirrors permits.
  The instant is the client's, **bounded at server time**: a stale one is harmless (it
  under-suppresses, and the row returns on the next poll), but a *future* one is refused
  outright rather than clamped, because no activity could ever advance past it and the
  unit's later stalled work would stay hidden for as long as that instant named — the
  reappearance the comparison exists for is a property of that bound, not of the caller.
  Dismissed items are *not* surfaced anywhere: the record names the unit, the instant, the
  wall-clock time and the user, which is where auditability lands, and a collapsed count or
  an undo would be its own feature with its own read.

  **"Stalled" is a computed view and never a persisted status.** There is no column, no new
  `dispatches.wait_reason` value, and no background sweep that writes one — including for a
  dismissal, whose record is its own row and whose suppression is a *comparison* the view
  re-derives on every read. The view is derived on every read, so a unit stays reported
  until it actually moves, is dismissed, or ages out of the lookback window, and nothing has
  to be un-set when it recovers. Note the name collision
  the read model deliberately does *not* reuse: `wait_reason: 'stalled'` already exists and
  means something else entirely — one BullMQ wake-up stalled, so the dispatch is waiting to
  be re-run. Such a dispatch is non-terminal, which makes its unit `active` here. One blind
  spot is
  deliberate and stated rather than fixed: a pull request that never produced a run of its
  own is invisible here, because closing that needs the provider's open-pull-request list —
  a different read with a different cost profile.

### Failure handling & rate-limit retries (issue #91)

A phase whose agent exits non-zero throws, and `processJob` turns that into a `phase-failed` `JobOutcome` rather than rethrowing — an agent run isn't idempotent, so a BullMQ retry storm is worse than surfacing the failure (the phase already logged the agent's stdout/stderr, and the `attempts: 3` default only ever fires for the infra throws that happen *before* the agent runs). The exceptions are transient failures: a **usage/session-limit** hit where the agent never did any work, an **aborted** run where the worker itself shuts down mid-phase, or a deterministic-delivery failure after the agent has already produced a valid checkpoint. Delivery deferrals preserve the worktree and expose the wrapped push/hook/API cause in the run rather than misreporting it as a worker shutdown.

**Not every delivery failure is deferrable, and delivery state is not only local (issue #558).** The progress sidecar that makes a delivery resumable lives on one worker's filesystem, while an interrupted dispatch goes back to the pool and may be re-dispatched to a machine holding none of it — so a resumed Implementation used to re-run the agent and produce a *second* implementation of the same task, on a branch whose remote already carried the first. Two rules close that: Implementation asks the SCM whether its task branch already has an open PR **before** running the agent (`findPullRequest`, the same answer on every host) and, finding one, adopts that delivery — no second agent run, no second commit — completing only the reporting the interrupted attempt still owed, with the PR-link comment carrying a per-delivery marker (`implementationDeliveryMarker`, mirroring Planning's) so a resumed attempt reuses its own comment instead of posting a duplicate. And a push that cannot fast-forward is classified as a terminal `DeliveryDivergedError` by `pushDeliveredBranch` (`src/scm/delivery.ts`) — the shared wrapper every pushing phase goes through — rather than a `DeliveryDeferredError`: only a *proven* divergence (the remote head is not an ancestor of the delivered commit) qualifies, so a transient rejection keeps its retry, while the unwinnable one settles at once with both SHAs named instead of spending four attempts on an identical rejection and holding the branch away from the phase that could unblock the PR. **Issue #839 turned that carve-out into a rule.** The terminal class is `UnretryableDeliveryError`, and `DeliveryDivergedError` is one member of it; every refusal `validatePreparedTree`/`commitPreparedTree` can raise (unresolved conflicts in the index, no working-tree changes at all, a tracked scratch artifact, nothing deliverable once hand-off artifacts are excluded) is another, because all four are raised through one `refuseDelivery(reason, remedy)` helper — so the next refusal added to either function is terminal without anyone remembering to classify it. That matters because a deferral resumes the preserved worktree *without* re-running the agent (`resumedDeliveryAgent`): nothing changes between attempts, so every retry re-validated byte-identical state and failed identically, burning the whole `MAX_RATE_LIMIT_RETRIES` budget and its backoff before settling failed. `shouldDeferDeliveryFailure` now asks whether the failure — or anything it wraps — is unretryable rather than naming one class, so the reason reaches the PR or board comment on the first attempt. The *message* is where the reason travels, not the class: a remote worker's terminal failure is rebuilt control-plane side as `AgentRunError{kind:'error'}`, so each refusal states what state was refused and what clears it. Genuinely transient failures — push races, API errors, rate limits, and any push rejection not proven to be a divergence — still defer and resume from the preserved worktree. `shouldDeferDeliveryFailure` is the single predicate all four *pushing* phases apply (Implementation, Resolve-conflicts, Respond-to-review, Respond-to-CI), so they cannot drift; Review is the fifth delivery-owning phase but applies a bare `hasDeliveryProgress` check, legitimately — it neither prepares a tree nor pushes, so it can raise none of the terminal class. Both rules live in the phase and the shared delivery seam, so a control-plane-delivered write and an operator-token one behave identically. **Issue #850 phase 2/2 moved the same question earlier**, for the two phases that write to a branch they did not create: `assertCheckoutHoldsHead` asks before the agent runs whether the checkout still holds the head the dispatch was pinned to — `'unchanged'`, or an advanced head the phase warns about and responds on (a Resolve-conflicts merge landing while the PR-scoped hold above held this dispatch), or an `UnretryableDeliveryError` when the commit was rewritten out of the branch, which costs no agent run at all — and `assertRemoteFastForwardable` asks `divergedRemoteHead`'s own question before `commitPreparedTree` rather than after the push, so the `DeliveryDivergedError` replaces the doomed commit instead of following it. One wording serves both raise sites (`divergedBranchMessage`), and both asserts fail **open** on a git error, leaving `pushDeliveredBranch`'s classification the backstop: the hold prevents the concurrency, and these only report what it cannot cover — a fail-open hold read, a human pushing mid-run, a rewritten branch.

`src/harness/agent-failure.ts` classifies a failed run (`classifyAgentFailure` → `rate-limit` | `capacity` | `auth` | `timeout` | `aborted` | `stalled` | `error`). Harness timeout and abort take precedence, followed by capacity and quota signals; a recognised auth banner then outranks only a trailing response-stall marker. This keeps a provider quota condition from being hidden by auth prose while ensuring a real login failure is not deferred as a generic stall. `auth` (issue #343) is the one recognised kind that is *not* a retry signal: it is classified so the run headline reads `… (authentication failed) …` instead of a bare `exited with code 1`, and is deliberately absent from the deferral list below — no wait restores a login, only a human re-`/login`. A quota signal is also trusted inside a CLI's *own* structural terminal failure record without the `resets …` co-occurrence free text needs, and since issue #963 Codex has one too — its `error` / `turn.failed` event, carried as `AgentCliResult.codexFailure` because Codex's `stdout` here is the normalized log text, which keeps only `agent_message` items: that is what makes a Codex quota hit classify identically whether or not the agent happened to speak before it was cut off, where previously it fell through to a terminal `error`. Its reset hint is Codex's own `try again at <time>` phrasing rather than `resets …`, kept for the headline but resolvable to an instant only if Codex ever names a timezone. On a `rate-limit`, `capacity`, `aborted`, or genuinely-interrupted `timeout` error, `processJob` returns a `phase-deferred` outcome with a computed delay instead of `phase-failed`, having already persisted the derived retry intent on the durable dispatch record and published its delayed wake-up, capped by retry budgets. The retry delay is floored above the review-dispatch-dedup TTL.

`capacity` is a transient **provider-capacity** signal, matched per CLI so one CLI's provider error isn't triggered by another merely quoting it: Codex's structured `error` / `turn.failed` events containing `selected model is at capacity` anywhere in captured output (or its textual terminal banner), and Claude/Claude Code's terminal `529 Overloaded` / `overloaded_error` banner (Anthropic's documented temporary-overload response — issue #229). It defers on a shorter capacity budget (`MAX_CAPACITY_RETRIES`, `MIN_RETRY_DELAY_MS` backoff) and retries **fresh, not resumed** — the provider rejected the request before the model did any work, so there is no partial reasoning or edit to continue. Only a bare status number never matches; human-readable provider banners use a conservative terminal-output window, so borrowed CI logs or reviewed code mentioning these numbers stay a plain `error`.

When Tier 1 session recovery cannot serve an involuntary stop, a parseable phase-matching `swarm_checkpoint.json` selects a Tier 2 continuation. Its run settles as retry-pending `checkpointed`, carries no session id, and the bounded continuation starts fresh from that checkpointed checkout.

A retry **reuses the originating `runs` row** rather than inserting a second one (issues #136 and #146): the `phase-deferred` outcome carries the run's `runId`, the dispatch settle path (`settleDispatchRetry`, `src/worker/consumer.ts`) persists it in the derived retry payload on the dispatch record, and on the next claim `processJob` resets that same row to `running` (clearing `completedAt`/`error`/`nextRetryAt` and the outcome columns) instead of creating a new one. A fresh webhook that intentionally reruns a deferred, checkpointed, or failed task/phase also claims and resets its latest terminal row, so automatic retries, manual retries, and user-triggered reruns all remain one logical dashboard run. A **manual "Retry now"** (the `runs.retryNow` tRPC mutation, offered for `deferred`, `checkpointed`, and `failed` runs) never flips the run row itself — the run turns `running` only when the worker actually claims the dispatch, so a failed enqueue can't strand a false `running` run (issue #284). It re-opens the run's active dispatch for an immediate attempt with `rateLimitRetryAttempt` reset to 0 (bypassing the automatic cap), folding any cli/model/reasoning overrides into the stored payload; a run with no active dispatch (terminally `failed`, or a legacy orphan) gets a fresh dispatch reconstructed from the row's persisted `jobPayload`. The conditional dispatch transition plus the one-active-dispatch-per-run unique index prevent concurrent automatic/manual retries from starting the same row twice. **"Reset & restart"** (the `runs.reset` mutation over `resetRun`, `src/dispatch/run-reset.ts`, issue #424) is the last-resort variant for a row "Retry now" cannot move: it additionally cancels the run's active dispatch, clears the Redis cancellation flag, tears down the checkout and its worktree lease (including a *stale* lease no live run owns), and clears the recovery record and captured session id before re-dispatching that same row from its stored `jobPayload` with a fresh agent session. **That replacement payload is composed as a first attempt, not as a retry** (issue #741): it is built by `reconstructResetJob` rather than the manual-retry builder, which strips every member of `RecoveryIntentSchema` — the session to re-enter, the delivery progress, the provisioned task branch, and any stored `recoveryMode` — by schema, so a latch added to the intent is dropped with no edit there, and sets only the reset's *own* mode (`'discard'`, on every reset since issue #744). The run row's stored payload is sanitised the same way, because that column is what a *second* reset and "Retry now"'s reconstruct-from-row path read; a failed sanitisation warns and continues, since the restart itself is already clean. Inheriting the intent is what made a run whose payload recorded `implementationBranchProvisioned` re-provision with `createBranch: false` against a branch that no longer existed on *every* reset, dying on `fatal: invalid reference: <branch>` while an ordinary fresh dispatch of the same task ran fine. The replacement dispatch is created last, so a partial reset leaves the run terminal-and-idle — the state a second reset retries from. **It never refuses and always discards** (issue #744): there is no `force` option, and a `running` row, a dispatch a worker just claimed, dirty/unpushed/pinned/stale-`live-leased` work, and a local teardown that throws are all handled rather than refused. **It also stops the agent that row already spawned** (issue #745) — the step that ended the two-step "Terminate, then Reset" dance. It reuses the delivery path that already exists rather than inventing one: the run-id-keyed cancellation `runs.terminate` records (`requestRunCancellation`, `src/queue/cancellation.ts`), which the router's bridge (`src/router/dispatch-cancellation.ts`, issue #549) turns into a pushed `task-cancel` frame to the worker actually executing the run — the only channel a DB-free worker has. The **wait** after it is the load-bearing half: `resetRun` polls the run row (500 ms, capped at 15 s — `AGENT_STOP_POLL_INTERVAL_MS`/`AGENT_STOP_WAIT_MS`, deliberately not configurable) until it leaves `running`, and only then clears the marker, tears the checkout down and creates the replacement dispatch, so the dying attempt's terminal result settles the *old* dispatch id rather than landing on top of the restart. The marker is cleared between the wait and the restart for the same reason it always was — earlier would let the worker's start-check miss the stop, later would kill the fresh attempt. A wait that expires proceeds anyway and says so (`agentStop: 'not-running' | 'stopped' | 'timed-out'`, reported by both surfaces): a reset that refused on an unconfirmed stop would strand the wedged run it exists to free. **What the replacement dispatch then meets is the router's own in-process claim on the task, and since issue #858 that claim no longer drops it.** An unreachable worker's job stays parked in the router until it gives up (`src/router/dispatch-cancellation.ts`, `src/router/transport-loss-reaper.ts`), and the in-process `inFlightPhaseByTask` entry lives until that job's `finally` — so for the gap between the two deadlines the router still held a claim on the task that `resetRun`, running in the API server or the CLI, can neither see nor clear, and the replacement was completed `skipped-duplicate` with the run settled `failed` on the restart Reset had just reported. The claim now records the dispatch it was taken for; a same-phase collision whose holder has already settled is deferred as `task-in-flight` and woken by that parked job settling, so the reported outcome and the run's fate agree. A holder that is genuinely still executing keeps refusing the claim exactly as before — the fix adds a wait, never a second attempt against one checkout. (One transient remains, deliberately unfixed: the reset reuses the `runs` row, so the parked job's own settle writes onto it while the replacement waits, and the row transits `deferred → failed → running` across the wake-up.) Only two answers are not a restart, and neither leaves a run un-reset: `run-not-found` (nothing to reset) and `already-resetting` (the one-active-dispatch-per-run unique index says a restart *is* happening). A run that cannot produce a dispatch at all — no stored payload, or a project that no longer exists — has its dispatch, flag and recovery record cleared just the same and is then settled `failed` with the stated reason, returned as `{ outcome: 'terminated', reason }` beside the ordinary `{ outcome: 'restarted', dispatchId }`. The dashboard surfaces it on the run-detail page for a `failed` (incl. recovery-`blocked`), `deferred`, or `checkpointed` run (issues #428 and #504): a confirmation modal names each step and states plainly what is destroyed — the checkout with any uncommitted and unpushed work in it, a dispatch a worker just claimed, and (for a `running` row) the agent process that is stopped first, taking its in-progress work with it — rather than offering an opt-in, and renders the mutation's per-step report — the agent stop, dispatch outcome, this host's checkout removed/retained (+ reason) or the error its teardown threw, the discard intent the replacement dispatch carries to the worker holding the checkout (issue #592), new dispatch id or the terminal reason — so an operator can tell a reset that freed the checkout from one that left it for the holding worker to clear. For a `checkpointed` row, that same "Retry now" control is labelled "Continue now" and invokes the unchanged `runs.retryNow` mutation with `recoveryMode: 'checkpoint'`. **How those actions are offered differs by state (issue #593).** A `deferred` or `checkpointed` run has not ended in an error — one waits on a scheduled retry, the other on a scheduled continuation — and keeps "Retry now"/"Continue now", "Terminate", and "Reset & restart" as side-by-side buttons. A `failed` run instead exposes **one "Recover" control**: a popup listing whichever recovery choices are currently eligible (the retry-family action under its own resume/recheck label plus the cli/model/reasoning overrides, and "Reset & restart" with its unchanged confirmation), where choosing one closes the popup, submits only that action, and turns the trigger into a disabled status label (`Retrying…`, `Resetting…`, `Waiting to restart…`) until it resolves. Retry and reset are two ways to recover the *same* errored run, so as two live buttons both could be submitted while the first was unresolved; consolidating them puts both behind one pending state. That state is the durable one — both mutations open a `manual-retry` dispatch, so `resolvePendingRunRequest` reports either as the run's outstanding `restart` request (issue #561) and the block therefore holds for a second viewer and across a reload — with the local mutation adding only what the derived fact cannot say: *which* choice this operator picked, so the label can name it during the HTTP round-trip. `swarm run reset <runId>` (`src/cli/commands/run.ts`, issue #429) is the second surface over the same service, printing that same per-step report and exiting non-zero with the refusal message: like `swarm queue clear` / `swarm worktrees prune` it goes straight to Postgres + Redis, so a wedged run can be freed with the worker — and the API serving the dashboard — stopped. **"Force re-review"** (the `runs.forceReReview` mutation over `forceReReview`, `src/dispatch/force-re-review.ts`, issue #511) is the run-recovery action for a different stuck state — not a wedged row but a *deliberately stopped* one: a completed Review run whose cap-reaching `request-changes` verdict set `manual-intervention-required`. It grants the PR one extra review slot on its ledger record and enqueues the corrective Respond-to-review run as a synthetic `pull-request-review` event carrying `forcedReReview`; the ordinary pipeline (response → follow-up Review) then continues unchanged. Guards run before either mutation and the grant precedes the dispatch, so a failure between them leaves an unconsumed grant and no work — which the next call re-uses. Both writes are conditional (the grant fires only while none exists; the dispatch's dedup key is a deterministic hash of (repo, PR, reviewed head)), so repeated clicks and concurrent requests resolve to one corrective cycle rather than duplicating it. That deterministic key is permanent, though, and a dispatch can resolve *dead* — `no-trigger`, `skipped-not-eligible`, `skipped-duplicate`, `superseded`, `failed`, `cancelled` — without ever starting Respond-to-review (confirmed live: a worker process that hadn't yet picked up this feature's own code evaluated the synthetic event as an ordinary, author-less webhook and refused it). `publishForcedDispatch` treats only a non-terminal dispatch or a `completed`/`phase-succeeded` one as "already handled"; anything else is dead, and it chains a fresh dedup key off the dead row's own id (`forceReReview`'s `dispatch: 'retried'` result) rather than reporting the dead attempt as done — bounded at five chained attempts so a persistently broken corrective path fails loudly instead of looping. It is authorized exactly like "Reset & restart" — `member`+ on the run's project — and the dashboard renders it inside the existing "Manual action required" callout using that action's interaction shape (confirmation modal, disabled-pending trigger and confirm button, per-step success report, refusal message inline).


PM retry dispatch intent is separate from Implementation branch reuse. Manual and automatic retries
carry `resumePmPhase` so an item already reporting "In progress" still re-enters its original phase,
while `implementationBranchProvisioned` is persisted only after successful task-worktree acquisition.
The retry uses the existing branch only when that explicit checkpoint is present; a run row by itself
never proves that provisioning happened.

### User-initiated termination (issue #166)

A **running** or **deferred** run can be terminated from the dashboard (the `runs.terminate` tRPC mutation). The API server and worker are separate processes, so termination never touches a PID: `terminate` records the intent in a durable, run-id-keyed Redis set and publishes a notification (`src/queue/cancellation.ts`). For a **running** run the worker aborts the agent via the per-run `AbortController` threaded into the phase (the same SIGTERM→SIGKILL path shutdown uses), and settles the row as `failed` with a user-termination reason — `handlePhaseFailure` checks the cancellation set to tell a user termination apart from a worker-shutdown abort (which still defers). For a **deferred** run `terminate` atomically cancels the canonical dispatch record and flips the row `deferred → failed` together (`cancelDeferredRunInDb` — a cancelled dispatch refuses every future claim, so no retry, slot release, or reconciliation can resurrect the work; issue #284); if a concurrent automatic pickup wins that race, the transition loses, the run is now `running`, and the cancellation flag drives the worker to terminate it instead. Keying on the immutable run id (not the task id) means a later retry of the same task is never terminated by an old request, and `retryNow` clears the flag before re-running. The mutation is idempotent: an already-terminal run returns its settled state rather than erroring. Captured logs are preserved (the aborted agent's output is stored like any failed run); only the board "failed" comment is skipped, since an intentional stop isn't a stall to investigate.

**A run that never started never reports a pickup (issue #912).** Both of the checks above — the pre-start marker read and the pushed `task-cancel` — used to abort a controller and then let the run proceed anyway, because the abort signal only ever reaches the *agent* subprocess. Everything a phase does before spawning one still ran: the dependency gate, worktree provisioning, and — the visible damage — the board move by which Implementation and Respond-to-review report their pickup into the phase's own column. So a run killed 360 ms after its row was created settled `phase-failed` + `cancelled` and, two seconds later, moved its card to **In progress**, where it sat with no run and nothing that would ever pick it up: dependency recheck skips a card whose status already matches the phase it is waiting on, so only a manual **Reset & restart** cleared it (live: `under-control-platform` task 21, 2026-09-13). The fix is to *prevent* the move rather than revert it — the observed move landed after the dispatch had already settled, so a revert issued at settle time would race an in-flight write — and it is one policy applied at the three sequential points the same run passes: `beginRunCancellationTracking` now reports whether it aborted for a cancellation and `processJob` throws `RunTerminatedError` on that rather than calling `executePhase`, so **no assignment is pushed at all**; the worker checks `isAssignmentCancelled` immediately before its `running` progress send and settles the terminal cancelled frame **without entering the phase**; and each pickup move is skipped for an already-aborted `signal`, closing the window in which a cancel lands during the phase's own pre-agent round trips. The first two read the *cancellation* flag rather than `signal.aborted`, because both controllers are also linked to their process's shutdown signal and a shutdown abort must keep deferring; the phase-level guards have no such distinction available and read the signal, which is harmless — a deferred run's retry makes the move. Nothing about a run that genuinely started and was terminated mid-flight changes: all three guards fire strictly before the agent is spawned, so a card in the phase's column always has (or had) a real run behind it. Settlement is untouched throughout — the same `phase-failed` + `cancelled: true` + `user-terminated` outcome, through the same `cancelClaimedDispatch`.

**Reaching the worker (issue #549).** A worker holds no `REDIS_URL` to read the cancellation marker with. The notification is therefore forwarded rather than consumed: the control-plane dispatch consumer subscribes (`src/router/dispatch-cancellation.ts`), resolves the run id to the dispatch it pushed and the worker it pushed it to (the run-id index on `src/router/dispatch-results.ts`), and pushes a `task-cancel` control frame down that worker's socket. The worker aborts the matching in-flight assignment through the same per-run `AbortController` (`cancelAssignment`, `src/transport/assignment-execution.ts` — a `dispatchId`-keyed registry of this daemon's in-flight assignments) and settles the dispatch terminal-`failed` with `cancelled: true`, which `src/router/dispatcher.ts` raises as a `RunTerminatedError` so the *shared* settle path applies the same user-termination outcome the pre-start marker check does — never a deferral, which would re-run the killed phase. The frame is additive, so `TRANSPORT_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is not bumped: a daemon predating it logs and ignores the frame rather than closing its socket. Two things stay durable-first: a cancellation recorded *before* the assignment was pushed is caught by a single marker read straight after the push (its notification predates the index entry, so it found nothing), and a run that is not executing on a connected worker — queued, deferred, already settled — is left entirely to the marker, exactly as before. **The worker answers a cancel it cannot apply (issue #724).** A frame naming a dispatch that is *not* executing on the receiving worker used to be logged and dropped — `handleTaskCancel` answered nothing — so the click changed only the run's final label and the row still settled on the `timeoutMs + RESULT_WAIT_MARGIN_MS` back-channel timer (verified live on 2026-08-12: markers written at 18:18, rows settled at 18:23 and 20:56 regardless). The worker is the only party that knows whether the phase is still executing there, so it now settles such a cancel with the same terminal `failed` + `cancelled: true` result an in-flight cancellation produces, and re-reports the *real* terminal result instead when it still holds that dispatch's undelivered (issue #718's queue) — so a Terminate arriving after the phase actually finished settles the run on its true outcome. That answer needs the phase and task a terminal result frame requires, which a cancel frame did not carry: `TaskCancelSchema` gained them as **optional** fields, filled from what the router recorded at registration (`src/router/dispatch-results.ts`), additive in both directions and so still no version bump — an older control plane omits them and the worker keeps the log-only behaviour rather than sending a malformed frame, and an older worker ignores them. No new settle path and no new router policy: the answer rides `deliverDispatchResult` → `adaptResultToPhaseRun` → `RunTerminatedError` → the shared settle, and a dispatch this router is no longer awaiting drops the frame, so the answer can never settle an unrelated attempt. One case is not answerable from the worker at all: a worker whose transport is down, where nothing can be pushed and the phase may genuinely still be executing, so writing the row from the API would take the terminal state away from its owner. That case used to wait out the lease window; it is now bounded from two sides — by the router itself when a termination was requested (issue #827, next), and by the transport signal alone when none was (issue #859, below). **…and the router bounds the wait on a worker that has gone quiet (issue #827).** That last case is narrowed, not removed, and by an explicit **liveness heuristic** rather than by any proof about the phase. The obvious test — "does the worker hold a live `worker_sessions` row?" — is *not* usable on its own: the control plane releases a worker's session on **every** `/worker/stream` close (`src/router/worker-transport.ts`), and a phase runs independently of the heartbeat loop and so routinely outlives the session it was pushed on (issue #718), so an absent live session is what an ordinary blip looks like too. What the push-failure branch reads instead is **silence**: the retained session row survives release precisely so `lastHeartbeatAt` can be read as *last seen* (`getRetainedSessionForWorker`), and only a worker that has said nothing for longer than `max(2 × SWARM_WORKER_HEARTBEAT_TTL_MS, 2m)` qualifies — comfortably past both the heartbeat cadence (a third of the TTL) and the reconnect ladder's jittered 30s ceiling, so a daemon that is merely coming back is never mistaken for a gone one. Such a run gets a `SWARM_OFFLINE_WORKER_CANCEL_TIMEOUT_MS` wait (default one minute), after which the router ends *its own* result wait with a synthetic terminal `failed` + `cancelled: true` frame (`failDispatchResultWait`, the issue-#719 seam). So this row is still written by the shared settle, not from the API: the frame rides `deliverDispatchResult` → `adaptResultToPhaseRun` → `RunTerminatedError` → the shared settle, which releases the parked dispatch job and every capacity it holds along with the rows, and records the same `failed` + neutral reason + recorded origin + `user-terminated` diagnosis any other termination does. Because the arming test is a heuristic, the timer decides on the facts as they stand when it fires rather than on the ones that armed it: it re-runs the silence test, and a worker that came back inside the wait is **re-sent the `task-cancel`** (issue #724's path — it is still the only party that knows whether the phase is executing there) instead of being settled behind its back; the settle is additionally gated on the run still resolving to the same registration, since `scheduleDispatchRetry` reuses a dispatch id across attempts. A real result arriving first consumes the registration, so the late timer finds nothing. **The reconnect itself re-states a missed cancellation, whether or not a wait was armed.** That wait re-pushes only from a timer it armed, and it arms one only for a worker already silent when the Terminate landed — while the everyday sequence is the reverse: the socket drops, the operator terminates seconds later with the retained heartbeat still fresh (so nothing is armed), and the daemon reconnects moments after that. Nothing re-stated the cancellation to it, since the notification fires once. So the socket open is the second and last place the durable marker is consulted (`handleWorkerStreamOpen`, `src/router/worker-transport.ts`): for exactly the dispatches this router is awaiting on the reconnecting worker (`listAwaitedDispatchesForWorker`, `src/router/dispatch-results.ts`) it reads the marker and re-pushes `task-cancel` for each run that carries one. It is best-effort like the push it repeats — an unreadable marker reads as “not cancelled” and the whole pass is swallowed, so a socket never fails to open on it — and it is bounded by what this router is actually executing there, so an ordinary first connection reads nothing at all. A worker that *did* already receive the cancel is simply told again: `deliverDispatchResult` consumes the registration, so the duplicate terminal answer (issue #724) is a no-op. Prompted by a live 2026-08-29 repro (`rover` task 5, `planning`, worker `jacek_tp_rover`) whose session had been released minutes before the Terminate and whose run row had to be settled by hand in Postgres.

**A run cancelled before it starts never reports a board pickup (issue #912).** Both checks above used to *abort* a run without stopping it. The pre-start marker check handed `executePhase` an already-aborted signal and pushed the assignment anyway; the `task-cancel` the router sends straight behind a push aborted the worker's controller, but that signal only ever reaches the agent subprocess. So everything a phase does *before* spawning its agent still ran — the dependency gate, worktree provisioning, and the "In progress" board pickup Implementation and Respond-to-review report as they start. Live on 2026-09-13 (`under-control-platform` task 21) the dispatch settled `phase-failed` + `cancelled` 5 ms after the marker was read, and the card's move to **In progress** landed 1.9 s *later*: a card in the phase's column with no run behind it and nothing that would ever pick it up, since dependency recheck sees a status that already matches the phase supposedly running it. Only a manual `Reset & restart` cleared it. The move is now prevented rather than reverted — the issue's own timing is why: a revert issued at settle time would race the in-flight move and could re-strand the card. Three guards, each strictly before the agent is spawned, so a run that genuinely started and was killed mid-flight is untouched and its card legitimately stays put. `beginRunCancellationTracking` now *reports* that it aborted for a cancellation, and `processJob` raises `RunTerminatedError` before `executePhase` — nothing is pushed; `runAssignmentDbFree` re-reads its own `dispatchId`-keyed registry immediately before entering the phase and answers with the same terminal cancelled frame an in-flight cancel produces; and the two phases that report a pickup skip it for an already-aborted signal, which closes the residual window where a cancel lands during the phase's own pre-agent work (the dependency gate is a transported round trip on a federated worker — the bulk of that 1.9 s). Each guard reads the *cancellation*, not the raw abort signal, because both controllers are also linked to their process's shutdown signal and a shutdown abort must keep deferring. Settlement is unchanged in all three cases: the same `RunTerminatedError` → shared settle path, the same `user-terminated` diagnosis, the same cancelled dispatch.

**A phase whose worker simply vanished is reaped on the transport signal, not on the lease (issue #859).** Everything above needs an operator to ask first. A worker that drops and never comes back with nobody asking anything was left entirely to the clock: the dispatch stayed `running` until the control plane's own result wait gave up at `timeoutMs + RESULT_WAIT_MARGIN_MS` or the 5-minute reconciler reached its expired lease — 34–38 minutes in the live incident that prompted it (`rover` run `3279e913`, a Respond-to-review orphaned at 15:30 and cleared by hand after 16:03). The lease is the wrong bound because it is sized to the *phase's* budget rather than to worker liveness, and the cost is not just wall-clock: the orphan keeps holding the PR-scoped hold (issue #850), its task's checkout, and a project capacity slot, so every phase queued behind any of the three defers on a dispatch that will never do anything. The signal was already there and already discarded — `onWorkerTransportLost` wrote one note into each affected run's output stream and nothing else (issue #723). It now also arms a bounded reap (`src/router/transport-loss-reaper.ts`): if the worker has no live socket here again after `max(2 × SWARM_WORKER_HEARTBEAT_TTL_MS, 2m)` — the same worker-liveness window #827 uses, shared as `src/router/worker-liveness.ts` so the two cannot drift — each dispatch this router is still awaiting on it is ended through the *same* seam both precedents use, `failDispatchResultWait`, with its own reason ("the worker's transport session was lost and did not return within the grace"), so run history tells it apart from a lease-window expiry and from an operator termination. The timer is never cancelled and the decision is re-made when it fires, so a blip that reconnects inside the grace is left alone and a dispatch that settled or was re-pushed elsewhere meanwhile is skipped. Because every drop arms its own grace, the window is measured from a dispatch's **most recent** loss, not from whichever loss armed the firing timer: each loss carries the interruption generation that identifies it, and a timer whose dispatch has dropped again since steps aside for the later drop's grace rather than settling a worker that has been absent for a moment (review #5069436530). Connectivity is the test rather than #827's `worker_sessions` silence heuristic, because here the close was actually observed. The frame is a plain `failed` with no `cancelled`, so it maps to a non-deferrable `AgentRunError { kind: 'error' }` and the worker's shared failure path settles the run and releases the PR hold, the task claim and the capacity slot, waking whatever queued behind them. **No re-dispatch follows** — deliberately: a Respond-to-review that had already pushed must not be replayed blindly, so whether to run the phase again stays an operator's `swarm run reset`. A router restart inside the grace loses the timer, and the lease reconciler remains the backstop for that, exactly as it is for #719's reap.

**Saying that a request is outstanding (issue #561).** Both `terminate` and `reset` return as soon as the intent is recorded, so there is a period — up to the router's own result wait on the transport path, and non-zero even with #549 delivering the frame, since the agent still has to unwind — in which the row is unchanged and nothing on the page says a request landed. `runs.getById` therefore resolves an additive `pendingRequest` (`resolvePendingRunRequest`, `src/api/routers/runs.ts`) from facts the request paths *already* write: the run-id-keyed Redis cancellation marker, for a `running` run, and otherwise the run's active dispatch when it is still waiting and carries `waitReason: 'manual-retry'` — the reason only `resetRun`/`retryNow` record, so an ordinary deferred run's scheduled auto-retry never reads as an operator request and never disables the button meant to unwedge it. Nothing new is stored and no write path changes: gating terminate-pending on `running` and restart-pending on a *waiting* dispatch is what makes the settle (or the worker's claim) clear the state on its own. The dashboard disables the matching button, relabels it from the action to the wait ("Waiting to stop…" / "Waiting to restart…"), and renders the explanation plus the bound the run records (`describeTerminateWait` / `describeRestartWait`) — so the state survives a reload, reads the same for every viewer, and a second click cannot record the same intent again. A read that throws degrades to "no request outstanding" rather than failing the page.

**Worktree settlement when a termination settles (issue #361).** Once a terminated run has fully stopped, its checkout, worktree lease, and persisted recovery record are reconciled to one consistent outcome by `reconcileTerminatedWorktree` (`src/worktree/termination-cleanup.ts`), shared by both paths: the running-run path calls it from the worker's `finalizeFailedRun` after the agent exits (the phase deliberately *preserves* a cancelled run's worktree so this reconciler owns the decision), and the deferred path calls it from `runs.terminate` after the atomic `deferred → failed` claim succeeds (so it never touches a checkout while the run might still be active — a lost race leaves cleanup to the running-run path). An **explicit resumable session** is preserved (`recovery.state = "preserved"`): the checkout is kept and its live lease released, because the database recovery record — not a live lease — pins the checkout for retention (`hasResumableDeferredRun`) until a resume. With **no resumable session** a checkout is removed only once it is provably safe — not owned by a *different* live run, `isClean()`, and free of unpushed commits — and a session id never outlives the checkout it would have resumed. Otherwise the work is **retained** with a blocked reason (`dirty`, `unpushed`, or `live-leased`), recorded on the run without reopening the cancelled dispatch. It fails closed: any git-validation error surfaces as a blocked reason rather than a forced removal. A later fresh run may still encounter an older clean collision; automatic collision-time reclamation is a follow-up phase. A third caller, **"Reset & restart"** (`resetRun`, `src/dispatch/run-reset.ts`, issue #424), is the only one that passes the settlement's opt-in options: it supplies a `hasLiveOwner` resolver (`hasLiveRunForTask`, shared with the provision-time liveness check above — which additionally weighs executing dispatches, a stricter bar suited to an automatic gate than to a deliberate operator override), so a lease **no live run owns** is recognised as a stale marker and reclaimed instead of being reported `live-leased` forever, and — on every reset since issue #744 — `discardProtectedWork`, which discards a dirty/unpushed checkout with a loud warning and reports what was thrown away. Both stay opt-*in* at the settlement: without them the settlement fails closed exactly as above for every other caller, and a checkout a *different* live run genuinely holds stays `live-leased` even for a reset.

On a **terminal** `phase-failed` (such as `error`, `timeout`, or `stalled`), `processJob` also posts a failure comment on the backing Issue for the work-item-carrying phases (planning/implementation) via `reportPhaseFailureToBoard` → `pm.addComment`, so a human watching the board sees *why* the item stalled. For a recognised response stall, `src/worker/failure-diagnosis.ts` labels the task **likely scope exceeded** only when the captured run shows at least ten minutes and 1,000 non-terminal output bytes *and* the latest successful Planning artifact declares multiple independent concerns; missing metadata or a lookup error falls back to **provider stalled early**, and a harness timeout alone never claims task size. Quota/rate-limit, capacity, launch/authentication, worker-shutdown, and user-termination conditions take precedence. The serialised diagnosis is persisted beside the raw error and rendered on both the board and run detail; the comment is best-effort and skipped for intentional user termination.

## Single-user scope

No per-org config and no multi-tenant Firestore layer. One Postgres row per SWARM project, one set of GitHub credentials (per persona) per project. The API server is loopback-only (`127.0.0.1`) and, as of #281 task 2, gated by per-user session auth (below) rather than a shared secret; as of #281 task 4 each request is then scoped to the caller's project membership (below), so an authenticated user no longer sees every project. As of #281 task 5 a project may *opt in* to a `discoverable` visibility that layers a limited public-discovery view and a request/approve join flow on top of that strict members-only default (below), without wiring any execution/routing permission to visibility. Revisit the per-project credential model as remaining ADR-001 work (worker registration/routing) lands.

**Multi-user foundation (ADR-001, issue #281) — in progress.** The identity domain landed first: a `SwarmUser` Zod schema (`src/identity/schema.ts`), a `users` table (`src/db/schema/users.ts`), a provider-neutral identity read-model service (`src/identity/service.ts`), the installation-level `instanceAdmin` flag, and a `swarm users` operator CLI. A SWARM user is a person who signs in to this installation — **not** an SCM identity and **not** an implementer/reviewer GitHub credential.

**Session auth (task 2) is now live.** The shared `DASHBOARD_TOKEN` bearer guard is gone: a user gets a password via `swarm users set-password` (stored as a `scrypt` salt+hash in `users.password_hash`, never plaintext), signs in at `/login`, and receives an opaque session token — stored only as a SHA-256 in `user_sessions`, delivered as an HTTP-only, `SameSite=Strict` cookie (`Secure` off localhost), never in a response body or log. The tRPC context (`src/api/trpc.ts`) resolves the caller from that cookie (`src/identity/auth.ts` `resolveSession`); every procedure except `ping` is an `authedProcedure` that 401s without a session, and logout revokes it. Because every request now carries a cookie via `credentials: 'include'`, the API server fronts all routes with a credentialed CORS layer (`src/lib/cors.ts`, `CORS_ORIGIN`, default `http://localhost:5173`) so the documented separate-origin dev setup (SPA on Vite, API on `API_PORT`) can send it — inert for the recommended same-origin deploy, which never pre-flights.

**Local single-user mode (issue #298).** For a single-operator local install, `SWARM_SINGLE_USER_MODE=true` (`src/lib/env.ts`; disabled-by-default, only the literal `true` enables it, and set in `.env.docker.example`) swaps the cookie requirement for a zero-setup identity: `createApiApp` resolves the mode once per instance and, in `createContext`, lazily bootstraps and caches a passwordless reserved `localhost-admin` `instanceAdmin` (`ensureLocalAdminUser`, an atomic idempotent upsert on the existing `users.identifier` unique constraint — no schema, no migration) and supplies it as `ctx.user` even with no `swarm_session` cookie, so a project-scoped procedure runs against a real installation admin exactly as if signed in. The `resolveSession` path, `/auth/login`, `/auth/logout`, and session minting are untouched and remain in force whenever the flag is disabled; the single-user path never mints or reads a session. The dashboard `/login` route consults `auth.me` and, when an identity already resolves, redirects into the app rather than showing the form — so a direct visit to `/login` is skipped in single-user mode without any browser-visible mode flag. **The mode is an authentication policy and nothing else.** It also bypassed the dispatch gate for a time (issue #373), and issue #552 removed that: one flag governing both decisions made the mode unusable with control-plane dispatch — the control plane has no local executor to bypass to, so `gateDispatch` (`src/worker/consumer.ts`) answering "no selection" left every dispatch durably pending. A single-user install now registers and enrolls its one local worker (`docs/onboarding-worker.md`) and is routed to it like any other deployment; nothing on the dispatch path reads the flag.

The same flag also governs **dispatch (Phase 2/2, issue #373)**: the host process is the implicit local executor for *every* project. `gateDispatch` (`src/worker/consumer.ts`) short-circuits to its `undefined` no-selection result — the same one an unfederated project yields — *before* reading the roster (`listProjectDispatchCandidates`) or constructing the assignee provider, so `processJob` reuses the existing local path (`acquireProjectSlot`, capability-aware local target selection, a null worker identity) and never touches enrollment, sharing consent, assignee affinity, live sessions, worker fencing, or worker capacity even when worker/enrollment rows exist. It creates no synthetic workers, `worker_sessions`, or `worker_project_enrollments` rows, mutates no consent, and rotates no credential — the federated eligibility, fencing, affinity, and capacity behavior are fully preserved and simply restored the moment the flag is disabled.

**Project membership (task 3, layer 2) is the read model authorization enforces.** It adds a `ProjectMembership` Zod schema and per-project role (`projectAdmin | member | contributor`, ordered by privilege) with `canAdministerProject`/`canWriteProject`/`canReadProject` predicates (`src/identity/membership.ts`), a `project_members` table (`src/db/schema/projectMembers.ts`, unique per `(project, user)`, cascade-deleting with either its project or its user), a repository (`src/db/repositories/projectMembersRepository.ts`), a provider-neutral membership read-model service (`src/identity/membership-service.ts`, whose `listAccessibleProjectIds` resolves an installation admin to *all* project ids and any other user to their membership set), and a `swarm members` operator CLI. Membership is created directly (repository/CLI/admin) or, as of task 5 (below), via an approved open-project join request. It is the read model that **project-scoped authorization (task 4, now live — below)** and **open-project policy (task 5, now live — below)** build on.

**Membership administration is reachable over the API, not only from the machine holding `DATABASE_URL` (issue #805).** The `members` tRPC router (`src/api/routers/members.ts`, mounted on `appRouter` as `members`) exposes the same four operations `swarm members` does — `list`, `add` (by login handle, resolving an existing SWARM user; an unknown identifier or project is `NOT_FOUND`, an existing membership `CONFLICT`), `setRole` and `remove` (both keyed on the `userId` `list` returned, both `NOT_FOUND` for a non-member) — each gated by `assertProjectAccess(user, projectId, 'projectAdmin')`, so it draws exactly the boundary the membership-request procedures do. It **adds a second path rather than replacing the first**: no schema change, no migration, and no new policy — the role model, ranking and predicates in `src/identity/membership.ts` are reused as-is, the CLI is untouched and stays the `DATABASE_URL`-side path that seeds a project's first administrator, and there is deliberately no last-administrator guard on either side, because the CLI has none and the two must not disagree. `list` reads one joined query (`listMembersWithUsersForProject` → `membership-service.listMembersWithUsers`, an explicit column list that never selects `password_hash`, ordered by `users.identifier`) rather than the CLI's `getUserById` per row; writes go straight to the repository, as `projects.ts` already does for membership requests. `add` re-checks that the project exists because `assertProjectAccess` returns early for an `instanceAdmin` without touching the database, so an unknown id would otherwise surface as a foreign-key 500 instead of `NOT_FOUND`. The router is **not** mounted on the operator router (`src/api/operator-router.ts`, the internet-exposed subset, which widens only via its own issue). Its dashboard half landed as phase 2 (issue #806): a **Members** tab on the project detail screen (`dashboard/src/components/projects/members-panel.tsx`), last in `PROJECT_TABS` and listed in `PROJECT_ADMIN_TABS`, so it renders inside the same `ProjectAdminOnly` frame the other configuration tabs do and a typed `?tab=members` URL meets that denial rather than a screen of `FORBIDDEN` cards. It adds no server code: the panel owns its `members.list` query and its three mutations, invalidating that one query key after each write (membership is configuration, not liveness, so it is not polled), it checks no role of its own — the frame plus `assertProjectAccess` are the two opinions, and a third would only drift — and it renders each rejection as the server's own message, which already names the fix. The three role labels are shared with the profile's My Projects tab through `dashboard/src/lib/project-roles.ts` (`PROJECT_ROLE_COPY`, a `Record` over `ProjectRole` so a fourth role is a compile error), and the `<select>`s are rendered from `ProjectRoleSchema.options` so their vocabulary and order come from the domain. Membership requests, inviting or creating users from the dashboard, and a roster for non-administrators all remain out of scope.

**Project-scoped authorization (task 4, layer 2) is now enforced.** `src/api/authz.ts` turns the membership read model into the API's enforcement layer — the second of ADR-001's three authorization layers (installation role → project membership → worker enrollment). Every project-keyed tRPC procedure (`routers/projects.ts`, `routers/runs.ts`, nested `routers/credentials.ts`) calls `assertProjectAccess(user, projectId, minRole)` or scopes a cross-project list through `filterAccessibleProjects` / `accessibleProjectScope`. An `instanceAdmin` bypasses every check (layer 1 override); a user with no membership cannot even learn a project exists (reads and mutations alike surface `NOT_FOUND`, never `FORBIDDEN`, so real project ids never leak); a member below the required role gets `FORBIDDEN`. The role → capability matrix, defined once in `authz.ts` and mapped from the `canReadProject`/`canWriteProject`/`canAdministerProject` predicates:

| Capability | Min role | Procedures |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Read a project, its runs, its (masked) credentials | `contributor` | `projects.getById`/`list`/`viewerAccess`, `runs.list`/`queued` (with a `projectId`)/`getById`/`getLogs`/`getOutput`, `credentials.list` |
| Drive a project's runs | `member` | `runs.retryNow`/`terminate`/`putBack` |
| Administer config, credentials, membership, or delete the project | `projectAdmin` | `projects.update`/`delete`, `credentials.set`/`delete`, `members.list`/`add`/`setRole`/`remove` |

Creating a project (`projects.create`) is open to any authenticated user, and the creator is written a `projectAdmin` membership row in the same call, so they can administer what they just made without an operator seeding membership first. Host-level procedures that are not keyed to a project (`ping`, `auth.me`, `settings.*`, `scm.verifyGithubToken`) stay `authedProcedure` only — installation-admin gating of global settings is a separate, later concern, not project-scoped authorization. `quota.getQuotas` is `authedProcedure` too but is **owner-scoped rather than unscoped** (issue #823): it filters on `ctx.user` through the join in `listCliQuotasForOwner`, with no `instanceAdmin` override, because a machine's remaining allowance is a fact about that machine's owner rather than something administering an installation entitles you to read.

**Installation-wide views are layer 1, not layer 2 (issue #647).** Three reads named no project at all and instead spanned the whole instance: the *unscoped* `runs.list`, `runs.queued`, and `workers.list`, which back the global `/runs` and `/workers` screens. (Two of them still do — `runs.list` left this gate again with issue #821, the paragraph below.) Those are an operator's view of the installation — the workers roster includes machines enrolled in no project — so they are gated by `assertInstanceAdmin(user, view)` (`src/api/authz.ts`) on the installation role alone, and no project membership grants them. That check is synchronous (the flag is already on `ctx.user`, so it implies no database read) and throws **`FORBIDDEN`, not the `NOT_FOUND`** the project-scoped helpers use: the caller named no project id, so there is no existence to hide and the honest answer is that the view is not theirs. Because only an admin reached those branches, they no longer derived `accessibleProjectScope` — the membership-filtered variants were removed rather than left unreachable (issue #821 brought `runs.list`'s back). Everything **project-scoped** is untouched, which is the point: an enrolled worker owner still reads their runs and their workers per project (`runs.list`/`queued` with a `projectId`, `workers.list` with a `projectId`, `workers.roster`) under `assertProjectAccess(…, 'contributor')`, and `workers.getById` deliberately stays on `accessibleProjectScope`, because a worker owner reaches worker detail by clicking through their own project's roster. The dashboard mirrors the boundary rather than defining it: `InstanceAdminOnly` (`dashboard/src/components/layout/instance-admin-only.tsx`) *is* the component the guarded route mounts — both routes then, `/workers` alone since #821 — so a typed URL, a filtered deep link, and an in-app link hit one frame and a denied screen never mounts (its cross-project queries are never issued); its sidebar link is hidden for a non-admin; and `/` forwards by role (`landingRouteFor`, `dashboard/src/lib/instance-admin.ts`) — an admin to `/runs`, anyone else to `/projects` — which also settles where the login screen lands. Single-user installs are unaffected: `SWARM_SINGLE_USER_MODE` resolves the reserved `localhost-admin` as an `instanceAdmin`.

**The cross-project runs list is layer 2 again, bounded per reader (issue #821).** #647's premise held for the workers roster and the queue but not for run *history*: a member's own runs are exactly the kind of thing membership answers, and gating the list on the installation role left them able to see runs one project at a time and nowhere able to see all of their work at once. So the *unscoped* `runs.list` no longer calls `assertInstanceAdmin`. It resolves `accessibleProjectScope(ctx.user)` instead and reads three ways: `null` (an `instanceAdmin`) queries unfiltered exactly as before; a non-empty scope goes into the repository's existing `ListRunsFilter.projectIds` boundary (`src/db/repositories/runsRepository.ts` — the authorization scope, distinct from the caller's own optional `projectId` filter); and an **empty** scope short-circuits to `{ data: [], total: 0 }` without querying, because the repository treats an empty `projectIds` as no filter at all and would otherwise widen a member-of-nothing back to every project. `runs.queued` and `workers.list` are untouched and stay on `assertInstanceAdmin`: the queue is an operator's dispatch view and the roster includes machines enrolled in no project, so neither has the membership answer run history has. The dashboard follows: `/runs` mounts its screen directly with no gate and the sidebar offers it to everyone, while the Queued section on that screen — and its `runs.queued` query — is issued only for an administrator, or once the reader picks a project the read is scoped by anyway. `landingRouteFor` is deliberately unchanged: an admin still lands on `/runs`, everyone else on `/projects`.

**The project screen's configuration tabs are layer 2, and the dashboard now says so (issue #655).** The `projectAdmin` row of the table above was enforced but not *reflected*: a `contributor` or `member` could open the project-detail page's Settings, Agent Configuration, Pipeline, Project Management, and Source Control tabs, where every read and write came back `FORBIDDEN` — a read-only credential-management screen that could do nothing.
