Those five tabs are now the administrator's alone (`PROJECT_ADMIN_TABS`, `dashboard/src/lib/project-nav.ts`), while **Runs and Workers stay every enrolled member's** — the same split issue #647 drew between an installation-wide screen and a member's view of their own work, one level down. The client learns the boundary from the server rather than guessing at it: `projects.viewerAccess` reports `{ canAdminister }` via `mayAccessProject(user, projectId, 'projectAdmin')` — the non-throwing twin of `assertProjectAccess`, exactly as `workers.getById` reports per-enrollment `viewerCanAdminister` (issue #477) — under the same `contributor` gate and existence-hiding `NOT_FOUND` as `getById`, so asking what you may do never answers that a project exists. It is deliberately its own query rather than a field on `getById`, whose cache is overwritten by `projects.update`'s result (a plain `ProjectConfig`, carrying no viewer capability) on every config save. `ProjectAdminOnly` (`dashboard/src/components/projects/project-admin-only.tsx`) is the frame the whole configuration half renders through, so a typed URL, a bookmarked `?tab=` deep link, and an in-app tab click hit one gate and a denied tab's panels never mount — none of their `projectAdmin`-gated queries (credential lists, PM provider lists, board discovery) is issued — with the tab bar's omission of the tab a courtesy on top. An absent access read fails closed, and the screen waits for it alongside the project so the tabs never flash in and back out. **Nothing server-side changed and no capability widened**: `projects.update`, `projects.credentials.*`, and the whole `pm` router still each assert `projectAdmin` for themselves, and an `instanceAdmin` still administers every project (which is what `mayAccessProject` answers, so the dashboard needs no separate installation-role branch).

**Open-project policy + join/request flow (task 5) — the final foundation slice.** Task 4's default is strictly members-only; task 5 adds an *opt-in* public face without loosening it. It keeps four permissions deliberately separate — **discovery ≠ joining ≠ worker registration ≠ task routing** — so public visibility never equates to execution permission (ADR-001 access model). A per-project `visibility` policy (`src/config/schema.ts` `ProjectVisibilitySchema`: `private` default | `discoverable`, a `text` column on `projects`) drives it:

- **Discover** — `projects.listDiscoverable` (any authenticated user) returns a **limited** read of `discoverable` projects the caller cannot already access: `id` + `name` only, projected in the query itself (`listDiscoverableProjectsFromDb`) so credentials/config/repo/run internals never even leave the DB. It excludes the caller's own projects; an `instanceAdmin` (who already sees everything) gets nothing new.
- **Join / request** — `projects.requestMembership(projectId)` files a **pending** membership request (`project_membership_requests` table, mirroring `project_members` with a `pending|approved|rejected` status and a *partial* unique index allowing one pending request per `(project, user)`). This resolves ADR-001 open question #1 in favour of **request/approve over immediate membership**: joining an open project grants no access on its own. A private/unknown project is `NOT_FOUND` (existence never leaks); an existing member or pending request is `CONFLICT`.
- **Approve / reject** — `projects.listMembershipRequests`/`approveMembershipRequest`/`rejectMembershipRequest` reuse `assertProjectAccess(user, projectId, 'projectAdmin')`, so only a `projectAdmin`/`instanceAdmin` acts on requests (a non-member gets `NOT_FOUND`, a lower-role member `FORBIDDEN`). Approval creates a `contributor` (**read-only**) `project_members` row atomically with resolving the request, and is idempotent w.r.t. an existing higher role.

Critically, visibility and membership remain separate from worker registration (#132) and task routing (#130): public visibility never grants execution permission. The highest role joining ever yields is `contributor`, which grants read and nothing more.

**Registered worker identity (#132 Phase 1).** Where a `SwarmUser` is *who* a person is and a `ProjectMembership` is *what* they may do on a project, a **worker** is *where* a user can execute — a locally operated execution environment they own (ADR-001's third identity layer). It adds a `Worker` Zod schema (owner user id, a safe machine `displayName`, declared CLI `capabilities` reusing `AgentCliSchema` — `claude | antigravity | codex` — and, since issue #467, the declared `supportedPhases` its daemon can execute; `src/identity/worker.ts`), a `workers` table (`src/db/schema/workers.ts`, FK to `users` `ON DELETE CASCADE`, unique per `(owner, displayName)`, unique credential hash), a repository (`src/db/repositories/workersRepository.ts`), a provider-neutral `worker-service` (`src/identity/worker-service.ts` — credential issue/hash primitives, `registerWorker` returning the raw credential **once**, `refreshWorkerCapabilities`, `renameWorker` (the owner's own machine label, dashboard-only — `workers.rename`, strict ownership, no `instanceAdmin` override), and the `resolveWorkerByCredential` auth seam), and a `swarm workers` operator CLI. The **worker credential** is distinct from an SCM PAT, stored only as a SHA-256, and returned raw once; a federated host supplies that raw value as `SWARM_WORKER_CREDENTIAL` so #339 can bind execution to its authenticated worker identity.

**A worker's declared CLI set is durable against its own PATH probe (#783).** `workers.capabilities` used to collapse two different facts into one column — *what the daemon last found on its PATH* and *what this machine's owner says it should run* — and since `refreshWorkerCapabilities` is called unconditionally on every handshake (`src/router/worker-transport.ts`), the second was overwritten by the first at every reconnect. `swarm workers set-cli` therefore never stuck, and the only durable override was `SWARM_WORKER_TRANSPORT_CLIS`, which works by *replacing the probe on the machine itself* and so never reaches the control plane as an intention. The two facts are now two columns: `capabilities` keeps its meaning and its write path exactly (the daemon's last self-probe), and a nullable `declared_capabilities` holds the owner's declaration, with `NULL` meaning "no declaration" — verbatim the pre-existing behaviour, so nothing is backfilled. One pure helper resolves what everything routes on: `effectiveCapabilities()` (`src/identity/worker.ts`) is the probe when there is no declaration and **`declaration ∩ probe`** otherwise — the declaration outranks a re-probe, which is the durability, while a CLI the probe *proved absent* is still never dispatched (`discoverAvailableClis` drops a CLI only on `ENOENT` and declares an unsettled probe anyway, issue #559). `rowToWorker` returns that effective set as `Worker.capabilities` — so the eligibility gate, the dispatch candidate list and both roster read models honour a declaration with no edit of their own, and the domain also carries the two raw facts as `probedCapabilities` / `declaredCapabilities`. Note the knowing asymmetry that buys: the *column* `capabilities` is the probe, the *domain field* is the effective set. The three sites that read a `workers` row directly rather than through `rowToWorker` — the dispatch claim's under-lock eligibility re-check and the two enrollment `allowedClis ⊆ capabilities` checks — call the same helper. Both safety properties are enforced on the declaration write, in one locked transaction (`setWorkerDeclaredCapabilities`): a declaration that would drop a CLI an active enrollment requires is refused with the existing `WorkerCapabilityReductionError` (the handshake's 409 rule, unchanged for a worker with no declaration — though with a declaration in force that check now judges the *effective* set, so a transiently narrowing probe no longer 409s a reconnect), and a declaration naming a CLI the machine's last probe never reported is refused with `WorkerCapabilityNotProbedError`. A declaration can therefore only *narrow* what the machine reports; widening past the machine's own reality stays `SWARM_WORKER_TRANSPORT_CLIS`'s job, which sets the probe and so composes cleanly with an intersecting declaration. `swarm workers set-cli` writes through the `declareWorkerCapabilities` service seam and takes `--auto` to clear the declaration — as does the worker detail page's own owner-only CLI control since issue #787 (see "Per-worker detail read model" below), so declaring a set needs no CLI or DB access; the handshake logs a warning when a stored declaration names a CLI the machine no longer probes, and that same drift is stated on the detail page.

**Worker session leases (#336 Phase 2) — one live claim per worker, fenced.** On top of Phase 1's identity, a **worker session** is the single live claim on a registered worker's execution environment, so two daemons can never drive the same machine at once. It adds a `WorkerSession` Zod schema (`src/identity/worker-session.ts` — a per-worker monotonic **fencing token**, `lastHeartbeatAt`, a nullable `currentRunId`), `worker_sessions`, its atomic acquire/heartbeat/release repository, and the provider-neutral `worker-session-service`. A session expires after `SWARM_WORKER_HEARTBEAT_TTL_MS` (default 60s); re-acquisition bumps the fencing token and stale holders cannot heartbeat or attach work. The host worker now acquires and refreshes this lease from `SWARM_WORKER_CREDENTIAL`, and #339 atomically locks the session row to validate its id/token and reserve dispatch capacity before execution.

**A reconnecting daemon reclaims its own live lease (#608).** A control plane that dies never releases the leases of the sockets it held, so the returning daemon used to meet a live row with a seconds-old heartbeat and had to wait out the remainder of the TTL — a plain router restart cost a machine that never went away its session for most of a minute. The handshake therefore carries an optional **proof of possession** (`reclaim`: the `sessionId` + `fencingToken` the control plane minted for that daemon — `WorkerSessionReclaimSchema`, `src/identity/worker-session.ts`), and `acquireLease` refuses a live lease only when the caller does *not* present a matching one (`isReclaimOf`). Three properties make that safe, and none of them is new: the fencing token still **bumps** on a reclaim, so a superseded holder's heartbeats, run attachments, and dispatch results stay rejected; `currentRunId` is still **cleared**, so a reclaimed lease never silently adopts an in-flight run; and a caller with only the shared `SWARM_WORKER_CREDENTIAL` — a genuinely competing second daemon, or a *restarted* daemon, since the pair lives in memory alone and is never persisted — presents no proof and is still refused with `worker session already held` (HTTP 409). The field is additive and optional, so `TRANSPORT_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is deliberately not bumped. What remained bounded by backoff rather than by the lease was the daemon's own reconnect ladder, and **issue #611 split that ladder in two** (`src/transport/worker-client.ts`, `createReconnectLadders`). A handshake the control plane *answered and refused* — a 409 session conflict, a capability rejection, a 500 it generated itself — still climbs the original exponential schedule (`DEFAULT_BACKOFF.maxMs`, 30s), unchanged. A handshake **nothing answered** — the request never completed, or an edge returned 502/503/504/520–524/530 for an origin that is not there (`WorkerTransportUnreachableError`) — climbs a *separate* counter capped at `unreachableMaxMs` (5s), and an answer of any kind clears it. The length of an outage therefore stops deciding how long a daemon idles once the router is serving again: that gap is the low cap plus equal jitter, rather than the up-to-30s wait a 60-second redeploy used to earn while nobody was home. It is a bound rather than a reset, deliberately — a 502 *is* a recovering server, so the equal-jitter floor is kept and a fleet still spreads its retries across `[2.5s, 5s]` instead of stampeding a control plane that has just come up.

**That same handshake decides the *previous* generation's fate (#719).** The token bump was, until this issue, inert: `dispatches.worker_fencing_token` was written at bind time and compared exactly once, pre-execution (`sessionClaimRefusal`), so a dispatch whose worker session had been superseded stayed `leased`/`running` — holding that worker's allocation and a project slot — until the control plane's own wall-clock guard gave up at `timeoutMs + RESULT_WAIT_MARGIN_MS`. The transport's fencing closed the stale *socket* (4408) and nothing else. Each daemon now mints one memory-only `instanceId` and carries it on every handshake; the control plane persists it on `worker_sessions` and reports whether an acquire replaced the same instance. The handshake settles the old claims only when the acquiring daemon is a **different** instance: it fails every `leased`/`running` dispatch of that worker bound to a different token, settles their `running` run rows on the same reason, and ends each one's control-plane result wait (`reconcileSupersededWorkerClaims` + `failDispatchResultWait`) so the awaiting job unwinds now — releasing the project slot and running the existing capacity/availability promotions from its own `finally` — instead of parking for the rest of the window and then settling the same run a second time. This keeps a live daemon safe when a successful handshake response was lost and it later returns with a stale proof, or with no proof after the intervening 409. A daemon predating `instanceId` falls back to `isReclaimedSession` (`src/identity/worker-session.ts` — the returned session's own id at exactly `reclaim.fencingToken + 1`), preserving the prior wire behaviour. The handshake rather than the 5-minute reconciler tick remains essential: a tick cannot tell a takeover from the same daemon coming back to a phase that is still executing (and, since #718, still holding its terminal result), so keying it on the token mismatch alone would reap a live run. The settle reason names the supersede rather than the timer's "did not report a result within the lease window", so run history says which actually happened; a claim this router is no longer awaiting (it restarted since the push) still gets its durable row settled, and the lease reconciler remains the backstop when the transport is down and no successor handshakes.

**Worker-project enrollment, sharing consent, and rosters (#337 Phase 3) — the routability seam + read models.** An enrollment links a worker to a project with `pending|active|suspended` status, allowed CLIs, **allowed pipeline phases** (`allowedPhases` — `NOT NULL`, non-empty, defaulting to every phase; issue #509), a per-worker `concurrencyAllocation` (`NOT NULL`, a positive integer defaulting to `1` — issue #480; "no per-worker cap" is not an expressible state), and owner-controlled sharing consent. The provider-neutral service owns those writes and secret-free roster/owner views.

`allowedPhases` is the worker owner's **per-project routing choice** and is deliberately a different axis from `Worker.supportedPhases`, which is what the machine's daemon declares it can execute and re-declares on every reconnect. The two are ANDed by the eligibility predicate (`permitsPhase(e, phase)` is the named seam, beside `isRoutable`), so the same worker can be offered `implementation` on one project and the whole repertoire on another, and a project-wide `pipeline.<phase>.enabled: false` still overrides both. Unlike `allowedClis`, it is **not** validated as a subset of what the machine declares: a CLI capability reduction is refused while an enrollment still requires it (`WorkerCapabilityReductionError`), which makes `allowedClis ⊆ capabilities` a maintained invariant, whereas a daemon narrows its phase repertoire freely — that is the point, since the owner's selection must survive a reconnect — so containment cannot be an invariant and enforcing it on the write path would reject a save that merely preserves stored state. The column default is every phase rather than the worker's own repertoire for the same reason: `supported_phases` states whichever *program* last operated the row, and since issue #536 a narrower set means a daemon on an older build rather than a different kind of program, so seeding a durable owner choice from it would permanently narrow enrollments nobody chose to narrow — and defaulting to every phase leaves dispatch behaviour identical for every enrollment that predates the field. Busy/capacity is derived from the worker's live session plus unexpired selected-worker claims on durable dispatches (with the older `currentRunId` pointer as a compatibility fallback), never client-supplied. `isRoutable(e) = e.status === 'active' && e.sharingConsent` is consumed by #339; revocation blocks future claims but never terminates running work.

**Enrolling into a project you administer needs no separate approval (issue #784).** Both conditions `isRoutable` ANDs are human decisions granted by two roles — a `projectAdmin` approves the enrollment, the machine's owner consents to sharing — and when one person holds both, they made both in the act of enrolling. `workers.enroll` therefore creates the enrollment `active` with `sharingConsent: true` when the caller owns the worker **and** `mayAccessProject(ctx.user, projectId, 'projectAdmin')` (`src/api/authz.ts`), passing both values explicitly to `enrollWorker` rather than leaving them at the service's `pending`/`false` defaults. The ownership conjunct is load-bearing, not redundant with the procedure's own resolve: `resolveOwnedWorker` carries the `instanceAdmin` layer-1 override and `mayAccessProject` answers `true` for an `instanceAdmin` unconditionally, so the admin-standing test alone would auto-approve an installation admin enrolling *someone else's* machine — the rule is joint owner-and-admin standing, never elevated privilege. Deliberately unchanged: every other caller (a contributor or member offering their machine to a project they do not administer) still lands `pending` with sharing off, and `swarm workers enroll` keeps its explicit `--active`/`--consent` opt-in, which is the form for bootstrapping a machine that is not yours. Nothing here retroactively grants an enrollment that already exists, and consent stays revocable exactly as ADR-001 requires.

**An enrollment must name a repository the project declares and the worker's checkout is (issues #690, #946).** A worker holds one local checkout, so a project that does not own that repository can only produce refused work — and both moments that pairing becomes knowable are policed in `worker-enrollment-service.ts`, against the declaration issue #687 persists. Both read the project **record** (`findProjectRecordByIdFromDb`) rather than the scoped config every other caller takes, and ask whether the project declares the worker's repository **anywhere** in its `repositories` list: `findProjectByIdFromDb` narrows to the project's *default* entry, so entries 1..n used to be invisible to both checks, which made a multi-repository project's roster unreachable — the enrollment was refused outright, and an enrollment written before the declaration was re-suspended on every handshake, which no operator action could outlast (issue #946). `enrollWorker` compares each declared entry with the worker's stored `repository` (through the shared `repoSlugsMatch`, so the *config* side is normalised too) and throws `EnrollmentRepositoryMismatchError` naming the checkout and **every** repository the project owns, which the tRPC router surfaces as `BAD_REQUEST` and `swarm workers enroll` as one actionable line; `suspendEnrollmentsForMismatchedRepository(workerId, declaredRepository)` walks the worker's enrollments after a handshake persisted a declaration and suspends each one whose project declares that repository nowhere, logging both sides. What this unlocks is the **one worker per repository, several per project** roster: a project owning several repositories now holds a distinct worker for each. It changes nothing else about what such a project shares — one SCM provider (issue #727), one credential set, one `pipeline`/`agents` config and one `maxConcurrentJobs` across its repositories, all deliberate and untouched. A single-repository project behaves exactly as it did, refusal wording included. Four decisions are deliberate. **It only ever suspends** — nothing here creates, approves, or re-activates an enrollment, including when a later declaration matches again, and including an enrollment the narrower pre-#946 rule suspended: enrollment is a human decision (ADR-001 — a project administrator approves, the owner consents), and a machine must not be able to restore its own routability by re-pointing a checkout. Widening the comparison therefore leaves those rows suspended; a project administrator re-activates each once, and the next handshake no longer undoes it. **Suspension rather than deletion**, so the enrollment keeps its constraints for the operator who fixes the pairing, and (like every other revocation here) blocks only future dispatch. **A worker that declared no repository is neither refused nor suspended**: an unidentifiable checkout — never connected, an older daemon, no readable `origin` — must not lock an operator out of enrolling their machine. And **a failure of the handshake-time pass never blocks a handshake** (`policeEnrollmentsAgainstDeclaration`, `src/router/worker-transport.ts`, logs and continues): it is housekeeping on the control plane's side of the connection, and the worker's own pre-flight check (issue #688) refuses a mismatched assignment regardless. There is deliberately **no `suspension_reason` column** — the reason *is* the declaration and the project's repositories, which the read models below carry live and which therefore cannot go stale the way a stored sentence would. **The dashboard read model reads the same list the checks do:** `DashboardWorkerEnrollmentDetail.projectRepos` is assembled from `findProjectRecordByIdFromDb` through the same `projectRepositorySlugs` helper, so it carries **every** repository the project declares, normalised, and `[]` when the project no longer resolves. The Workers screen's comparison is therefore membership in that list (`repositoryMismatch`, `dashboard/src/lib/worker-enrollment-view.ts`) rather than equality with a single entry: no mismatch banner for a worker correctly enrolled on entries 1..n, and a genuine mismatch names every repository the project owns, which is what lets an operator tell a typo from a repository the project does not have. Unknown on either side — a machine that declared nothing, or a project that no longer resolves — is still not a mismatch. The banner is explanation and never a routing decision: the dispatch gate compares against the *task's* repository (issue #714).

**Worker connectivity roster (#133) — the dashboard's read-only aggregate.** On top of the same foundations, `listDashboardWorkers(projectScope)` (`src/identity/worker-enrollment-service.ts`) composes the one cross-project view the dashboard's **Workers** screen renders: machine display name, owner identity, declared CLI capabilities, the declared `supportedPhases` repertoire (issue #467, so version skew is visible on the screen — a daemon built before #536 declares five phases rather than six — rather than being inferred from work that never starts; the repertoire is shown in full on the worker detail view, with no phase promoted over another, and the Workers table's Capabilities column lists CLIs only, issue #542), `online`/`offline`, a nullable `lastSeenAt`, the job it is executing, and per-project enrollment/approval summaries — each summary now also naming its own `allowedClis`, so the table's Capabilities column can show the CLIs at least one visible enrollment actually allows (a cross-project union, `effectiveClis` in `workers-table.tsx`) rather than the raw declared set: a machine can declare more than any project has turned on for it, and showing the declared superset there would read as "this project can dispatch here with any of these" when it can't. It adds no schema and no second liveness rule — `online` is `getLiveSessionForWorker` (so `SWARM_WORKER_HEARTBEAT_TTL_MS` stays the single TTL), and `lastSeenAt` comes from a new *retained*-session read (`getRetainedSession` → `getRetainedSessionForWorker`) that deliberately ignores liveness so an expired or gracefully released worker keeps its last heartbeat; a worker that never connected reports `null`. **Authorization on the unscoped read is the installation role** (issue #647): the roster spans the whole instance — every registered worker, un-enrolled machines included — so `resolveRosterScope` asserts `instanceAdmin` and passes the unrestricted `null` scope, and a non-admin gets `FORBIDDEN` rather than a membership-filtered subset (their view is the project-scoped one below). `workers.getById` still scopes by `accessibleProjectScope`, stripping inaccessible enrollments from the row, because a worker owner reaches one machine's detail from their own project's roster. The active job is validated against run lifecycle exactly as `deriveWorkerRunState` does *and* filtered by project, so an in-flight run in an inaccessible project never leaks through a worker the viewer shares another project with. Since issue #473 it is returned as a `currentRun` summary (`DashboardWorkerRun` — run id, project, task id, phase, and the work-item/PR title, URL and number) rather than a bare run id, so the screen can describe the job the way the Runs table's Task column does instead of printing a UUID; the summary is assembled field-by-field from the run row, so a job payload, error text, or usage can never ride along on a roster read model. The tRPC `workers.list` query (`src/api/routers/workers.ts`) exposes it read-only, serializing `lastSeenAt` to ISO at the boundary; no machine path, credential, token, or routing affordance is on this surface. Since issue #574 that query also serves **one project's** roster — the project detail page's Workers tab, and the view an enrolled worker owner keeps — through an optional `projectId`: `resolveRosterScope` swaps the installation-admin gate for `assertProjectAccess(…, 'contributor')` plus a single-project scope, so the tab reuses `listDashboardWorkers` with no service change and inherits its three scoping rules verbatim (no enrollment elsewhere, no out-of-scope active job, and no un-enrolled machine even for an `instanceAdmin`), under the same access rule and the same existence-hiding `NOT_FOUND` as `workers.roster`. Since issue #657 the unscoped variant comes back **viewer-first**, and since issue #808 the rest of it comes back **grouped by owner**: `viewerWorkersFirstThenGroupedByOwner` (the same router) puts the machines the caller owns ahead of everyone else's — in the read model's own oldest-first order, unchanged, because that group is the one an operator already knows by heart — and then emits one contiguous run per remaining owner, the runs ordered by owner display name (tie-broken on the owner's unique `identifier`, since display names are not unique) and each run's machines ordered by machine display name. #657 left that remainder in raw registration order, which interleaved every teammate's machines and made scanning for one person's a linear read of the whole roster. A row whose owner user row no longer resolves (`owner === null`) forms its own run and sorts **last**: it has no label to alphabetise by, and a stand-in would scatter those rows through owners they have nothing to do with; it is never the viewer's own, whose user row resolved to produce `ctx.user`. That is ordering alone — it runs *after* scoping has decided what is visible, reads the row's existing `owner`/`displayName`, and adds no field, no query, and no second answer to "which machines may I see". **The project-scoped variant orders by the project's own configured worker order instead** (issue #750): `inProjectOrder` sorts the rows by `listProjectWorkerIdsInOrder(projectId)`, one extra scoped read, so a project's Workers tab shows the same sequence the dispatch gate schedules by rather than a per-viewer one — showing each operator their own machines first would misdescribe a project whose order is now a real, persisted, administrator-set fact. The unscoped global roster keeps viewer-first-then-by-owner because it spans projects and has no such order.

**Per-worker detail read model (#477).** `getDashboardWorkerDetail(workerId, projectScope)` returns that same row for **one** worker, widened with the full per-project enrollment detail (`DashboardWorkerEnrollmentDetail` — enrollment id, status, effective allowed CLIs, effective allowed pipeline phases (issue #509), concurrency allocation, sharing consent, the derived `isRoutable`, and the enrollment's own `projectRepos` — issue #690, widened by #946 from the project's default entry to every repository it declares) plus the worker's `ownerUserId`, so the dashboard's worker detail view explains routability without a roster query per project. It adds no schema, no second visibility rule, and no second assembler: the row half is still built by `assembleDashboardWorker`, the enrollment half names each safe field explicitly, and **visibility is `listDashboardWorkers`' rule verbatim** — an administrator (`projectScope === null`) sees any registered worker including an un-enrolled one, any other viewer only a worker enrolled in a project they may access, with inaccessible enrollments stripped. It returns `null` both for an unknown worker and for one the viewer may not see, and the tRPC `workers.getById` query turns both into the same `NOT_FOUND`, so worker existence never leaks. That procedure additionally reports **what the viewer may change** — `viewerIsOwner` for the worker (the machine's *actual* owner only, no `instanceAdmin` override, mirroring `resolveStrictlyOwnedWorker`/`resolveOwnedEnrollment`) and `viewerCanAdminister` per enrollment (via the non-throwing `mayAccessProject(user, projectId, 'projectAdmin')`, `src/api/authz.ts`) — so the screen offers only controls that would succeed while the write procedures stay the enforcement point, re-checking ownership/role for themselves. `rename` (the machine's own `displayName`), `setDeclaredCapabilities` (its durable CLI declaration — see below), `setConsent`, `updateConstraints`, `approveEnrollment`, and `setStatus` are the detail view's six drivers; `setDraining` is the seventh since issue #926 — a **Pool membership** card offering the machine's owner Drain / Return to the pool and saying, from the active job the view already reads, whether it has gone idle and is therefore safe to restart, so issue #919's drain is reachable without the CLI. It reads that from the *presence* of `currentRun` — the run-lifecycle fact the server derived — and never from a title: `workItemTitle` is absent for every PR-driven phase, so a card keyed on it reports a running machine as idle, and the title it does show is resolved exactly as the Active job section resolves it (`resolveRunTitle`, `dashboard/src/components/runs/work-item-cell.tsx` — the PR's for a PR-driven phase, the work item's otherwise), with neutral "still running a job" copy when none has resolved. It is deliberately the one owner action here with no confirmation (reversible, effective only from the next dispatch, undone by the button that replaces it), and it renders as a deliberate operator state rather than a fault: the row's `drainingSince` also marks the Workers table's Status column *beside* Online/Offline, since a drained machine that is online is still online. Presentation only — no procedure, read-model field, or dispatch behaviour is added by it; `enroll` (offering a worker to a *new* project) is the one owner-self-service write that still admits the `instanceAdmin` override (`resolveOwnedWorker`) — it reads as administering the project side of the offer rather than a decision about the machine itself, and it isn't exposed on this view at all.

**The detail view is also where an owner declares the machine's CLIs (#787).** It is the one read model widened with the two *raw* halves of the CLI axis #783 split apart — `declaredCapabilities` (the owner's durable statement, `null` when none has been made) and `probedCapabilities` (the daemon's last self-probe) — set beside `ownerUserId` in `getDashboardWorkerDetail`, so `assembleDashboardWorker` stays the sole assembler of the roster row and every roster keeps rendering only the effective set. Both are needed because the effective set cannot describe itself: the control offers one checkbox per *probed* CLI (a declaration naming anything else is refused, so an unprobed option would be an option that fails) plus a "Use auto-detected CLIs" action sending `capabilities: null`, and it flags a declaration the latest probe no longer backs — the drift the handshake logs server-side — instead of silently showing the intersection. The write is `workers.setDeclaredCapabilities`, gated by `resolveStrictlyOwnedWorker` exactly like `rename` (no `instanceAdmin` override: this is a statement about someone's own machine), calling the `declareWorkerCapabilities` seam and translating its two guards for the browser — `WorkerCapabilityReductionError` → `CONFLICT` (the tRPC 409, matching the handshake's own status for the same rule), `WorkerCapabilityNotProbedError` → `BAD_REQUEST`. Neither is re-implemented client-side. It adds no storage, no validation, and no handshake behaviour of its own.

**The reason a mismatched enrollment isn't taking work is live facts, not a stored sentence (issue #690).** The row carries the machine's declared `repository` and each enrollment detail its project's `projectRepos`, **both in the shared normalised `owner/repo` form** — so the screen's plain equality test (`repositoryMismatch`, `dashboard/src/lib/worker-enrollment-view.ts`), which since issue #946 asks whether the declaration is a **member** of that list, *is* the comparison the write path makes, without importing `src/scm/repo-slug.ts`, whose slug reader spawns `git` and cannot be bundled for a browser. The worker detail view reports the declared repository beside the two capability axes (all three are self-declared; the repository and the phase repertoire are read-only, while the CLI set is the owner's to declare since issue #787) and renders the mismatch on the enrollment block as "this machine's checkout is X, but this project is Y" — naming every repository the project owns when it declares more than one, so a typo is distinguishable from a repository the project does not have — derived on every render rather than from a sentence stored when the mismatch was detected. It is shown whatever the enrollment's status is: a mismatch normally *is* a suspension, but an active mismatched one (a policing pass that failed, say) is exactly the case an operator most needs explained, since the gate routes work there and the daemon then refuses it. Both fields are non-secret by the same standard as the rest of this surface — a repository slug is not the machine's path (`SWARM_WORKER_REPO_ROOT` never travels) and not a credential — and they are named explicitly in the assembler like every other field.

**Assignee resolution + identity linking (#130 Phase 1) — the affinity lookup.** ADR-001's routing rules make assignment an *execution-affinity* rule: a work item assigned to a PM identity that maps to a SWARM user may run only on that user's eligible worker — **for the phases affinity is gated on** (`AFFINITY_GATED_PHASES`, `src/worker/eligibility-gate.ts`; ADR-001 as amended by issue #469). Today that is `implementation` alone: `planning` is exempt, and the PR-driven phases carry no assignee at all. Planning's exemption was originally argued from portability — only a database-holding worker could run it, so routing it by assignment could only ever prevent work rather than place it better. Issue #536 removed that premise (a DB-free worker now runs Planning), and the exemption is deliberately kept on the reason that outlived it: Planning produces no branch, worktree-bound artifact, or PR, so there is nothing about it that belongs on a particular person's machine, and gating it on affinity would only make a plan wait for one worker. Revisiting that is a routing decision of its own, not a consequence of the phase becoming portable. **(1) A provider-neutral read of who an item is assigned to**: `WorkItemAssignee` + `WorkItem.assignees` + the `PMProvider.supportsAssignees` capability flag (`src/pm/types.ts`), populated by the GitHub Projects adapter from the backing Issue/PR (see "PM: GitHub Projects" above). Because `trigger.workItem` is already built from an authoritative `pm.getWorkItem()` (`src/triggers/handlers/pm-status.ts`), assignees flow to the trigger layer. **(2) A SWARM identity-linking seam**: a `UserIdentity` Zod schema (`src/identity/user-identity.ts` — a `(provider, handle)` pair owned by one `users` row), a `user_identities` table (`src/db/schema/userIdentities.ts`, FK to `users` `ON DELETE CASCADE`, **unique per `(provider, handle)`**), a repository (`src/db/repositories/userIdentitiesRepository.ts`), and the resolver `src/identity/assignee-resolver.ts` (`resolveUserForAssignee(assignee, provider)` / `resolveAssignedUser(workItem, provider)`), whose `provider` argument is the caller's `PMProvider.type` so the seam stays provider-agnostic. This answers ADR-001 open question 5 by construction: a handle maps to **at most one** SWARM user (the unique index), keys are normalized (trimmed + lowercased, so casing never splits a link), and an unlinked handle resolves to `undefined` rather than a guess. Links are seeded by an operator (`swarm identities link`) — automatic linking (OAuth / SCM account discovery) and a dashboard UI are deliberately out of scope. The worker eligibility gate consumes these links to enforce owner affinity before execution.

**Worker eligibility evaluation (#338, #130 Phase 2) — the pure half of the dispatch gate.** `evaluateWorkerEligibility` (`src/identity/worker-eligibility.ts`) folds ADR-001's routing prerequisites into one structured verdict: `{ eligible: true }` or `{ eligible: false, reason }`, where `IneligibilityReason` is `missing-enrollment` (no enrollment, or one not yet `active`) | `missing-consent` (consent never granted or revoked) | `worker-draining` (an operator took the machine **out of the dispatch pool** so it can be restarted — issue #919) | `worker-unavailable` (no live session, or the enrollment's `concurrencyAllocation` — this worker's share of the project, always a positive integer — fully used; the project's `maxConcurrentJobs` bounds it further) | `repository-mismatch` (the machine's one local checkout is a different repository than the task's — issue #714) | `missing-phase-capability` (the machine's daemon never declared this phase — issue #467) | `phase-not-permitted` (the machine declares it, but this enrollment's owner did not select it for this project — issue #509) | `missing-cli-capability`. Checks run in the ADR's order — active enrollment → sharing consent → draining → connection/health → free capacity → the repository the checkout is → declared phase support → the enrollment's allowed phases → declared CLI capability — and the **first** missing signal is returned, so a caller has one reason to show rather than a set; the first two checks are `isRoutable` split apart only so a revoked consent reads as `missing-consent`. **The draining check** (issue #919) reads `Worker.drainingSince` — nullable, machine-wide, `NULL` while the machine is in the pool and otherwise the instant an operator drained it — and sits beside sharing consent, deliberately *ahead* of the connection/capacity check: both are the operator's own standing statement that this machine takes no work, and a machine drained *in order to* be restarted is usually also offline, so judging drain later would report the self-clearing `worker-unavailable` for a wait that waiting cannot fix. What the machine is already running is untouched by construction, since the gate runs only before a phase starts. **The repository check** (issue #714) takes the *task's* repository as a required input — the scoped project's own `repo`, which issue #684 phase 2 pins to the job's repository rather than the project's default entry — and compares it against the candidate's declared `Worker.repository` (issue #687) through the shared `repoSlugsMatch` (`src/scm/repo-slug.ts`), which normalises the config side too. It sits ahead of every capability because a checkout is the most fundamental property of the pairing: a worker holding the wrong tree can run no phase of this task at all, whichever phases and CLIs it declares — while connection and capacity stay ahead of *it*, because "some worker is merely busy" remains the best news available and a machine that does hold the repository but is offline must still read as `worker-unavailable`. A worker that declared **no** repository is deliberately not refused (an unidentifiable checkout must not become unroutable — the provision-time `origin` verification and issue #688's assignment refusal remain its guards, exactly as issue #690 decided for enrollment). It is **target-scoped, never target-choosing**: it judges exactly one candidate target from `agents.<phase>.targets` against that target's effective CLI (its own `cli`, else the phase's coded `DEFAULT_*_CLI` passed as `phaseDefaultCli`) and requires that CLI to be both a declared worker capability *and* allowed by the enrollment — it never reorders or falls back across targets, so Phase 3 can call it once per target and keep configured target priority across workers instead of collapsing back to a single model. The module is dependency-light and side-effect-free (like `src/pm/dependencies.ts`): the DB-backed signals arrive as a caller-resolved `WorkerAvailability` (`connected` from the fenced worker session, `activeRuns` from unexpired durable dispatch claims). The dispatch wiring, scheduler ordering, assignee affinity, and the scheduler-only `assignee-worker-unavailable` reason are Phase 3 (below).

**Federated dispatch gate (#339, #130 Phase 3) — the scheduler that consumes all three.** The gate (`src/worker/eligibility-gate.ts`) runs inside `processJob` **after** trigger resolution and **before** any worktree provisioning or agent invocation, on every phase and every (re)dispatch — with **one rule for every deployment**, single-user installs included, since issue #552 deleted the `isSingleUserMode()` bypass that used to short-circuit it (see "Local single-user mode" above). It reads the project's enrolled workers (`listProjectDispatchCandidates`, `src/identity/worker-enrollment-service.ts` — worker + enrollment + resolved availability in the project's **configured worker order**, which is the documented "first free eligible worker" sequence; since issue #750 that order is a persisted, `projectAdmin`-settable property of the enrollment row — `worker_project_enrollments.order_index`, read as `(order_index, created_at, id)` — rather than enrollment-creation order, which is exactly what a project nobody has reordered still gets, since every existing row migrated to position `0`. Ordering is a preference *between* otherwise-eligible workers and never a grant: an unavailable, over-capacity, unapproved or otherwise ineligible worker is still skipped for the next eligible one, and neither target priority, assignee affinity, the preserved-worker pin, nor pool scheduling is affected), applies ADR-001's affinity rule **for the phases it covers** (issue #469 — Planning is exempt and takes the unassigned path), and judges each candidate with Phase 2's predicate. Selection is **target-priority-first, worker-choice-second**: it walks `agents.<phase>.targets` in configured order and stops at the first target *some* eligible worker can run, so a higher-priority Codex target wins whenever some enrolled worker can run Codex even if a Claude-only worker is free for a lower-priority Claude target — and it never silently falls back to `targets[0]`. A per-run "Retry now" override still pins exactly one target, which the gate evaluates rather than routes around; `src/worker/target-policy.ts` is the one place that reading of the policy lives, so the gate, local capability routing (#346), run creation, retry reset, and the phase invocation all agree on the chosen target.

**Which of several eligible workers wins is a pool decision (issue #533).** "First in enrollment order" is only correct when one dispatch is in play; with the consumer processing several at once it can spend a scarce capability another runnable phase uniquely needs — a Review eligible on two machines taking the only Planning-capable one, leaving Planning nowhere to run while the other machine idles. So once the chosen target has more than one eligible worker, the gate reads the project's other runnable dispatches (`loadRunnableDispatchDemands`, `src/worker/pool-demand.ts` → `listRunnableDispatchesForPool` — non-terminal, no worker claimed yet, due now, in the queue's own `priority` → `availableAt` → FIFO order, capped at `POOL_DEMAND_LIMIT`), turns each into the workers that could serve it, and computes a **maximum bipartite matching** over the pool's free slots (`src/worker/pool-scheduling.ts`), taking its own share. Four properties make that safe to run per dispatch, with no new coordination: it is **pure and deterministic**, so the same input always yields the same assignment (not the same thing as two concurrent gates agreeing — each substitutes its own affinity-narrowed set for itself and reads contenders as supersets, so two gates can pick the same worker and race the claim, which costs the one deferral a raced claim already costs); it **never withholds work** — an unmatched dispatch falls back to its own first-eligible pick, because the demand that won the slot may be a dispatch whose wake-up has not fired, so nothing here can defer a phase that could otherwise run (which is also why it adds no starvation risk, and why priority/ageing stay entirely in the dispatch row's existing ordering); it **never overrides a hard constraint** — eligibility, affinity, and target priority are decided before it, and `claimWorkerForDispatch` remains the only authority on capacity, so a raced snapshot costs at most the same one deferral it does today; and a **failed or absent pool read is not an error** (`undefined` → today's behaviour). A contender's demand is reconstructed from its dispatch row without assignee affinity or the `implementationUnplanned` target variant, both of which would cost a read per waiting dispatch, and a contender whose stored payload names no repository is judged against every machine rather than narrowed by a guessed one (issue #714 — `undefined` means the project's *default* entry, which the project a gate scoped need not be) — each approximation only widens a contender's reconstructed set, so the gate errs towards keeping its own pick rather than diverting for contention that isn't real. A contender that *does* name its repository is narrowed by it, so contention is only ever counted between dispatches that could actually share a machine.

Routing outcomes:

- **Unassigned item** (no assignees, or none linked to a SWARM user — ADR-001 open question 5) → a free eligible project worker: the pool's choice among them when other work is contending (issue #533, above), otherwise the first in that deterministic order.
- **Assigned item** whose assignee resolves to a SWARM user, on an **affinity-gated phase** (`AFFINITY_GATED_PHASES` — `implementation` today) → *only* a worker owned by that user. There is **no cross-user fallback**: the work waits. On a non-gated phase — `planning` (issue #469, whose portability premise issue #536 removed without changing the exemption; see "Assignee resolution" above) — the assignment is not read at all and the item takes the unassigned-item path above.
- **Continuation of a preserved checkout** (issue #567) → *only* the machine that holds it, which outranks affinity and every other narrowing. See "Preserved-checkout pinning" below.
- **Nothing eligible** → a structured refusal, never a silent drop. `DispatchIneligibilityReason` is Phase 2's eight reasons plus the three scheduler-only ones, `assignee-worker-unavailable`, `preserved-worker-unavailable` and `preserved-worker-unknown` (issue #954); the gate throws `WorkerIneligibleError`, and `handlePhaseFailure` treats it exactly like a dependency block — a **token-free** deferral (nothing was provisioned, no model spend) on the dependency gate's cadence with its own `workerEligibilityRecheckAttempt` budget, settling `failed` with an actionable board comment (grant sharing consent, approve the enrollment, enroll a worker that can run the configured CLI) only once that budget is exhausted. **The row records which kind of wait that is** (issue #607): `isAvailabilityRefusal` (`src/worker/eligibility-gate.ts`) partitions the whole reason vocabulary — a `Record` over the union, so a new reason fails to compile until it is classified rather than defaulting into a bucket — into the refusals a machine becoming available clears (`worker-unavailable`, `assignee-worker-unavailable`, `preserved-worker-unavailable`), which keep the dispatch wait reason `worker-eligibility`, and the refusals only a human can clear (`missing-enrollment`, `missing-consent`, `missing-cli-capability`, `missing-phase-capability`, `phase-not-permitted`, `repository-mismatch` — a checkout is re-declared only at handshake, so nothing a connecting machine does re-points one — `worker-draining`, since reconnecting does not undrain, so a machine coming back must not wake a dispatch a drain refused, and `preserved-worker-unknown`, since no machine coming online can supply a record nobody wrote — issue #954), which read as `worker-authorization`. The split is durable and read-model-visible only: **both kinds keep the same cadence, attempt counter, and budget**, and `preserved-worker-unavailable` still records its own `preserved-worker` reason ahead of either (below). It exists because the two waits behave differently for an operator — one clears by itself in seconds, the other never does until somebody acts — and because a wake-up policy has to be able to tell them apart. **A prioritized continuation's dispatch dedup claim is retained across this wait too** (issue #780): the trigger handler claims the PR+SHA (or head/base) slot before the gate runs and the wait releases nothing, so the deferral refreshes that claim for the length of its own cadence and records `continuationDispatchClaimed` on the dispatch's persisted payload — the one payload both the timed re-check and #610's promotion republish — so the woken handler reuses the claim instead of being refused by it. Without it the retry re-entered its handler, collided with its own live claim, and the dispatch settled `completed` with no run row at all; the claim is *held*, never skipped, so two independent events for one PR+SHA still produce exactly one dispatch. `deferBeforeRun`'s two pre-run waits and this one retain it through one shared helper (`retainContinuationDispatchClaim`, `src/worker/consumer.ts`).

**The availability wait is woken, not polled (issue #610).** That deferral schedules its re-check on a fixed 5-minute timer, so a dispatch refused a moment before a worker connected sat still next to an idle machine for up to the whole interval (observed live: two board deliveries deferred `reason=worker-unavailable retryDelayMs=300000` at 07:24Z, both workers idle from 07:24:46Z, nothing running until the timer fired at 07:29Z). Two moments now promote it — the same move issue #262 made for the concurrency wait:

- **A worker connects** — the authenticated `/worker/stream` open (`handleWorkerStreamOpen`, `src/router/worker-transport.ts`), the one place the control plane learns a machine became reachable. Kept there rather than in `registerConnection` so the connected-worker registry keeps owning no scheduling decision.
- **A worker frees capacity** — a dispatch bound to it settles, releasing its execution claim, in `processJob`'s `finally` (`src/worker/consumer.ts`) next to the capacity promotion. That is the correct signal for a **federated transport worker** too: its phase runs remotely and the settle happens here, after the result frame arrives.

Both call `promoteAvailabilityWaitsForWorker` (`src/dispatch/dispatcher.ts`), which asks two different questions of the two waits: a `worker-eligibility` row is offered by any project the worker is **routable** enrolled in (active enrollment + sharing consent), while a `preserved-worker` row is keyed on the **recorded machine** (`runs.recovery.preservedWorkerId`) and never on enrollment, because the pin is honoured for a machine that is no longer an enrolled candidate at all. `worker-authorization` is never promoted: no machine coming online grants consent, so waking it would only spend its budget faster.

Per row the order is **remove the delayed wake-up → conditionally re-date → publish the replacement**, so a dispatch never holds two live wake-ups. Only rows whose wake-up is still delayed are candidates, which is what makes the removal deterministic; the re-date is conditional on the row still sitting on the exact `wake_seq` that was read, so concurrent wake-ups from two workers resolve to at most one promotion. Nothing else about the deferral changes: the stored payload and `workerEligibilityRecheckAttempt` are untouched, so the woken job re-enters the full gate spending the budget the previous settle persisted and may defer again; the timed re-check is still the fallback when no wake-up arrives; and the whole promotion is best-effort and fully swallowed — a queue hiccup must never fail a settled run or keep a socket from opening.

**Preserved-checkout pinning (issue #567) — the narrowing that keeps a continuation's work.** A continuation's state is machine-local and has no server-side copy: a Tier 2 checkpoint file, a resumable agent session, and a deterministic-delivery sidecar all live in `.swarm-workspaces/task-<id>` on the worker that stopped. Worker selection had no notion of *where the state is*, so a continuation re-dispatched to any other eligible machine found nothing to adopt, provisioned a fresh checkout, and re-ran the phase from scratch — at full token cost, with nothing in the run record saying so (observed live on task 549: a rate-limited Implementation checkpointed on `m3_pro_tp`, and "Continue now" started it over on `m5_pro` three seconds after that machine created its own empty checkout).

Two halves fix it. **Recording**: every settle that leaves a checkout for a continuation to adopt — a resumable/checkpointed/delivery-resume deferral, and a cancellation that preserved its session — copies the attempt's own `runs.worker_id` into `runs.recovery.preservedWorkerId` (`recordRunPreservedWorker`). It has to be recorded at that moment because `worker_id` is the *last attempt's* worker and is overwritten at every bind; `recovery` is free-form `jsonb`, so no migration. The key survives the rewrite that a re-bind performs (`resetRunToRunning` carries it onto the `recovered` record) and is dropped only when the run stops recovering — which `recoveryWriteSql` reads off a *null* recovery write, the one that means "this attempt gave the checkout up". A settle that did **not** give it up must therefore write a record rather than nothing, and a *refused adoption* is exactly that case: the gate would not adopt the checkout, but the directory is still on disk on the machine that wrote it. On the federated path it used to write nothing — the worker's terminal `task-execution-result` frame carried only a status and an error string, so `adaptResultToPhaseRun` rebuilt every non-cancelled terminal failure as a plain `AgentRunError` and `finalizeFailedRun` wrote `recovery: null`, erasing the machine (observed live on run `06e71e23-2508-4aef-9e0d-61e133c11833`, task 131: a continuation blocked adopting its checkpoint, that settle nulled `runs.recovery`, and the operator's "Retry now" thirteen minutes later was dispatched to a machine that had never held the task). Since issue #952 the frame carries the recovery gate's own refusal (`TaskExecutionResult.blockedReason` — a plain string, like `failureKind`, so a reason a newer worker learned first cannot fail the whole settle to a parse error) and the dispatcher rebuilds a `BlockedRecoveryError` from it, so the settle writes the same `{ state: 'blocked', blockedReason }` record its DB-backed twin writes: a non-null write, which the sticky merge carries `preservedWorkerId` onto for free, with no change to the pin logic. Since issue #553 deleted the in-process executor this is the only path that reaches that branch at all. The field is optional and additive in both directions, so `TRANSPORT_PROTOCOL_VERSION` is deliberately not bumped. **Enforcing**: `jobContinuesPreservedCheckout` (`src/dispatch/preserved-worker.ts`) recognizes the four payload shapes that adopt a checkout — `recoveryMode: 'resume'`, `recoveryMode: 'checkpoint'`, `resumeSession` (the automatic retry's equivalent of the first), and `resumeDelivery` — and never `recoveryMode: 'fresh'`; for those, the gate's permitted set becomes that one machine. The pin **replaces** affinity rather than intersecting with it: the two can only disagree when the item was reassigned after the checkout was preserved, and intersecting would wait forever for a machine that cannot hold the state anyway.

Its refusal is `preserved-worker-unavailable`, with the dispatch wait reason `preserved-worker`, and it is **the one wait with no budget**. Failing the run abandons work that is still on disk, and a timed fallback to another machine is this defect with a delay on it — so the wait is unbounded, names the machine — on the dispatch row (`lastError` carries the refusal in full, not just the `waitReason` category) and in a run-detail callout (`runs.getById` resolves `preservedWorker` to a display label, and reports whether the run is *currently* waiting on the pin rather than merely carrying one) — and ends only when an operator chooses. It deliberately does **not** write the run's `error` column: the gate refuses before `tryCreateRun`, so a continuation's wait has no run row of its own to settle, and writing onto the row it carries would clobber the `checkpointed` status and session id that row exists to hold. A *structural* refusal from the pinned machine (consent revoked, enrollment suspended, phase not permitted there) keeps its own reason and its own bounded budget: those name something only a human can fix and will not resolve on their own.

**A continuation whose machine is unknown is refused, not routed (issue #954).** The pin read carried a back-compat carve-out — no recorded `preservedWorkerId` meant "route this unpinned" — which was the very thing the unreadable-row branch beside it calls the defect: the checkout is machine-local, so handing the continuation to whichever worker is free re-runs the phase from scratch at full token cost with nothing in the record saying so. The read is now a **tri-state** (`resolvePreservedWorkerPin`, `src/worker/consumer.ts`): pinned / nothing to pin / machine unknown. The carve-out narrowed rather than disappeared — a run whose `runs.worker_id IS NULL` never bound a machine, so there is no machine that *could* have been recorded and it routes exactly as before; a run whose last attempt did bind one and yet records no pin is the unknown case. `runs.worker_id` is deliberately not used as a substitute pin: it is the *last bind's* worker, so an already mis-routed continuation would name the wrong machine — this is a refusal, not a better guess. The gate decides, rather than the read throwing, because only the gate knows whether the project is federated at all: the refusal is emitted **after** the `candidates.length === 0` → `unfederated` return, which is what keeps an unfederated project's continuations running exactly as today, structurally rather than as a special case. Its reason is `preserved-worker-unknown`, classified a **non-availability** refusal — so it takes the ordinary bounded `workerEligibilityRecheckAttempt` budget and the existing `worker-authorization` dispatch wait reason, with no new wait reason, no DB change and no dashboard change. The classification is the whole point of the pairing: `preserved-worker-unavailable` waits without a budget because the machine is known and really will come back, while here no machine coming online can supply a record nobody wrote, so an unbounded wait would be permanent. It ends instead in an actionable terminal failure naming "SWARM has no record of which machine holds this checkout" and pointing at "Reset & restart", the one action that gives the preserved work up and frees the phase to run anywhere. **That ending settles the carried run row too** (`failCarriedRunAfterGateRefusal`, `src/worker/consumer.ts`): the gate refuses before `tryCreateRun`, so the exhausted dispatch has no run of its own, and the row the continuation carries would otherwise stay retry-pending with nothing left to dispatch it — which the reconciler's orphan backfill then repairs by re-dispatching it, refusing it again, for good. So where the *wait* deliberately leaves that row alone (above — its `checkpointed` status and session id are what the retry continues from), the terminal refusal fails it with the same reason and clears the retry-shaped columns (`failRunFromStatus`), which gives nothing up that could have been used: the record needed to place the checkout is the very thing that was never written. `recovery` is left untouched, so the settle erases no pin. This is scoped to `preserved-worker-unknown` alone — an exhausted *structural* refusal names something an operator can still fix, so its row stays resumable.

Two consequences elsewhere. The **token-free re-check deferrals** (this gate's and the dependency gate's) now carry their payload through verbatim (`deriveRetryJobPayload`) instead of re-deriving recovery intent: both refuse before anything is provisioned, so their "retry" is the same attempt still waiting, and re-deriving would drop a `'checkpoint'` mode outright and replace a `'resume'` session id with a freshly minted one — turning the wait into the start-over it exists to prevent. And **"Reset & restart" is the escape hatch**: it clears the pin (`clearRunRecovery`), converting `preservedWorkerId` into `abandonedWorkerId` so the run durably records that it started over rather than continued, and it needs nothing from the pinned machine — the dispatch is cancelled control-plane side and the local checkout reconciliation is a no-op for a checkout on another host — which is exactly what makes it usable while that machine is unreachable. The abandoned checkout is then an ordinary orphan on its machine, reclaimed by the normal provisioning/prune path rather than pinned forever.

Because the gate sits on the common dispatch path it re-checks **before retries and later phases**: a consent revocation, enrollment suspension, health loss, or capability removal between attempts blocks the *next* dispatch, while a run already in flight is untouched. A selection becomes executable only when the dequeuing process authenticates as that exact worker and `claimWorkerForDispatch` locks its live fenced session, re-validates enrollment/consent/CLI and that the machine is still in the pool (issue #919 — with the `workers` row taken `FOR UPDATE`, so a drain write and a concurrent claim are totally ordered and an assignment already in flight is never disturbed), counts active unexpired claims, and persists the worker/session/fencing token on the dispatch (and worker/token on the run). A different host defers; simultaneous claims serialize on the session row and cannot exceed the enrollment's `concurrencyAllocation` (the project's `maxConcurrentJobs` bounds it further); retries clear the claim, while terminal/expired dispatch states cease consuming capacity. A project with no enrollments remains unfederated and runs locally as before.

**Control-plane dispatch (ADR-003 §2, issue #407).** The BullMQ consumer + this gate live on the **router** (`src/router/dispatcher.ts`), which makes every dispatch true split delivery: the control plane decides *and* the worker executes in a different process, on the same host or a remote one — the same program either way since issue #551, and the *only* arrangement since issue #553 deleted the in-process alternative. It reuses `processJob` verbatim — the whole claim → trigger → automation gate → eligibility gate → `bindSelectedWorker` → run-row → durable settle → self-enqueue → merge-automation machine is shared. `processJob` takes a small `ProcessJobDeps` seam (`src/worker/consumer.ts`), built in one place (`createControlPlaneDispatchDeps`); it injects three things: a **connectivity predicate** folded into the gate so only workers holding a live `/worker/stream` socket here are selectable (`isWorkerConnected`, `src/router/worker-connections.ts` — a DB-live-but-not-connected worker is skipped as `worker-unavailable`, so on an affinity-gated phase an assignee's offline worker still waits rather than routing elsewhere — `planning`, being exempt from affinity, routes to any other capable worker instead, issue #469); a **bind identity** resolved from the *selected* worker's live session (the control plane claims the fenced slot on its behalf); and an **executePhase** that composes the system prompt + target branch server-side (`src/router/assignment-composition.ts`, reusing the pure `src/pipeline/prompts/*` builders), builds a `TaskAssignment` carrying the **non-secret** project-config slice (never a persona token — `src/transport/assignment.ts`), pushes it to the worker, and awaits the worker's `TaskExecutionResult` (correlated by `dispatchId` through `src/router/dispatch-results.ts`) to adapt back into the shared settle path. That adaptation rebuilds the classified failure a reported deferral stands for — `DeliveryDeferredError`, an `AgentRunError` carrying the reported kind, or, for a `dependency` frame, a `DependencyBlockedError` reconstructed from the blockers the worker reported (issue #438) — so each one lands on the budget its kind names, exactly as a failure raised on this side would. With no eligible/connected worker the dispatch stays durably `pending` via the same `WorkerIneligibleError` token-free deferral. The control-plane host's own worker is the same DB-free transport entrypoint every other worker runs (`src/transport/connect-entry.ts`, over loopback), so the router is the queue's only consumer and there is one executor program rather than two (issues #551/#553). A worker that drops before reporting is settled from the transport signal itself when it does not come back inside the worker-liveness grace (issue #859, above), and when a **different daemon process** takes that worker's lease the handshake settles it from *that* signal (issue #719, above); the dispatch-lease reconciler stays the clock-based backstop behind both. The **DB-less remote worker** this once listed as out of scope has since landed (issues #394, #417, #418 — see "Worker connection modes" above): it resolves no persona token and opens no database, running source delivery under the operator's own token and routing the credentialed metadata reads/writes, the review-verdict ledger, and the follow-up-Review enqueue through the delivery API. Since issue #536 it also runs **`planning`**, the last phase the DB-free gate refused, so no phase is excluded from that path any more — the eligibility gate's phase check (issue #467, above) remains what routes around a *daemon* that declares fewer, rather than being a permanent carve-out for one phase. Still out of scope: over-the-wire secret delivery (unnecessary — the split keeps each project credential server-side instead).

**Planning verification settlement (issue #818).** A failed verifier, including one whose exit is successful but whose edits fail Planning's normal scope/split validation, restores every captured planning artifact and continues with the original plan. A cancellation is propagated through Planning's normal failure path instead, so it cannot publish a plan, create split children, move the work item, or add its `planned` label.
